# Lectures by Niraṣjana Swami on Śrī Caitanya-caritāmṛta

## Lectures by Nirañjana Swami on Śrī Caitanya-caritāmṛta

## Introduction

Last year, I was very enlivened to find that the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust (BBT) had published a new onevolume edition of *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. The two previous one-volume editions had some disadvantages. With one, it was difficult to read because the print was exceptionally small. With the other, the original Bengali was not there. Though the latest is larger and heavier than the previous two, the print size is adequate and the Bengali is there. When I saw it, I knew I had to find a place in my carry-on luggage bag to make it my constant travel companion. Shortly thereafter, a copy was given to me in Vṛndāvana. I now cannot go anywhere without it.

Śrīla Prabhupāda*’*s *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* is one of the many precious gifts Śrīla Prabhupāda left in this world. Everything is there—*gaura-līlā*, *kṛṣṇa-līlā*, and all the highest truths available in Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇava theology. We simply have to dive deep and mine the valuable jewels contained in the oceanic depths of *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. Śrīla Prabhupāda has already done so much of this work for us by providing his own brilliant commentary, which also includes the commentaries of Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura and Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura.

Śrīla Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī, the author of *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* has written (*Ādi-līlā* 2.117):

*siddhānta baliyā citte nā kara alasa*  
*ihā ha-ite kṛṣṇe lāge sudṛḍha mānasa*

A sincere student should not neglect the discussion of such conclusions, considering them controversial, for such discussions strengthen the mind. Thus one’s mind becomes attached to Śrī Kṛṣṇa.

The first paragraph of Śrīla Prabhupāda*’*s commentary to this verse is as follows:

There are many students who, in spite of reading the *Bhagavad-gītā*, misunderstand Kṛṣṇa because of imperfect knowledge and conclude Him to be an ordinary historical personality. This one must not do. One should be particularly careful to understand the truth about Kṛṣṇa. If because of laziness one does not come to know Kṛṣṇa conclusively, one will be misguided about the cult of devotion, like those who declare themselves advanced devotees and imitate the transcendental symptoms sometimes observed in liberated souls. Although the use of thoughts and arguments is a most suitable process for inducing an uninitiated person to become a devotee, neophytes in devotional service must always alertly understand Kṛṣṇa through the vision of the revealed scriptures, the bona fide devotees and the spiritual master. Unless one hears about Śrī Kṛṣṇa from such authorities, one cannot make advancement in devotion to Śrī Kṛṣṇa. The revealed scriptures mention nine means of attaining devotional service, of which the first and foremost is hearing from authority. The seed of devotion cannot sprout unless watered by the process of hearing and chanting. One should submissively receive the transcendental messages from spiritually advanced sources and chant the very same messages for one’s own benefit as well as the benefit of one’s audience.

In the spirit of this purport, I will continue, for the rest of my life, to submissively hear Śrīla Prabhupāda*’*s *“*transcendental messages*”* from the pages of his *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. I pray, also for the rest of my life, that I will continue chanting these very same messages for my own purification. By so doing both, it is my fervent desire to dive deeper and deeper into the depths of the heart of my spiritual master to mine the *prema-ratana-dhana*, the jewel of divine love, that most precious gift he came to give.

Although I am totally inadequate, I also pray that at least *“*someone*”* will derive some benefit from my insignificant effort, offered within the pages of this book, to serve Śrīla Prabhupāda*’*s divine order to *“*chant the very same messages,*”* as he would want them chanted to others.

I would like to take this opportunity to extend my gratitude to all those who have made this volume of *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* lectures possible. My sincere gratitude goes out to Līlā-Smaraṇa devī dāsī, for overseeing the project; to her husband, Gaṇeśa Prabhu, for his generous support and friendship; to Nanda Kumāra dāsa and Śrī Rādhā devī dāsī, brother and sister, who are both full-time university students but still find the time to transcribe, edit, and proofread; to Kalpataru devī dāsī, Mahārāṇī devī dāsī, Vraja Kumāra dāsa, Vāruṇī dāsī, Bhaktin Tatyana, and Bhaktin Larysa for their transcribing work; to Grahila dāsa who also contributed to the editing; to Amala Gopī devī dāsī for layout; to Anubhāva devī dāsī for design and illustrations; to Vasudama dasa for organizing the printing; and I would especially like to thank Micheal Gugelev and Draupadī devī dāsī for their kind and generous donations.

*Nirañjana Swami*

March 30, 2004

*Rāma-navamī*

## Part 1 - Ādi-līlā

## THE SPIRITUAL MASTERS

**Ādi-līlā: 1.54**

**Minsk—1998**

\`

**Text 54**

*ṛte ’rthaṁ yat pratīyeta*  
*na pratīyeta cātmani*  
*tad vidyād ātmano māyāṁ*  
*yathābhāso yathā tamaḥ*

**What appears to be truth without Me is certainly My illusory energy, for nothing can exist without Me. It is like a reflection of a real light in the shadows, for in the light there are neither shadows nor reflections.**

**Purport**

In the previous verse, the Absolute Truth and its nature have been explained. One must also understand the relative truth to actually know the Absolute. The relative truth, which is called *māyā*, or material nature, is explained here. *Māyā* has no independent existence. One who is less intelligent is captivated by the wonderful activities of *māyā*, but he does not understand that behind these activities is the direction of the Supreme Lord. In the *Bhagavad-gītā* (9.10) it is said, *mayādhyakṣeṇa prakrtiḥ sūyate sacarācaram*: the material nature is working and producing moving and nonmoving beings only by the supervision of Kṛṣṇa. (…) That which is relative, temporary and far away from the Absolute Truth is called *māyā*, or ignorance. This illusion is exhibited in two ways, as explained in the *Bhagavad-gītā*. The inferior illusion is inert matter, and the superior illusion is the living entity. The living entities are called illusory in this context only because they are implicated in the illusory structures and activities of the material world. Actually the living entities are not illusory, for they are parts of the superior energy of the Supreme Lord and do not have to be covered by *māyā* if they do not want to be so. The actions of the living entities in the spiritual kingdom are not illusory; they are the actual, eternal activities of liberated souls.

This purport fully explains the meaning of this verse. This is a very important verse because it helps us to understand the illusory nature of this material world. If we can have a small understanding of the material world’s illusory nature, it can help us to understand the Absolute. In the beginning of the purport, Śrīla Prabhupāda says, “One must also understand the relative truth to actually know the Absolute.” The Absolute Truth is not so easily understood because the Absolute is beyond the material world, and thus the Absolute Truth cannot be understood by mental speculation. It cannot be understood by the material mind, through logic and reasoning, or by our blunt material senses. The Absolute Truth is beyond our imperfect sense perception, and therefore the material mind and senses are not sufficient instruments for understanding.

We can only begin to understand the Absolute Truth when we hear about the existence beyond this material world from absolute sources such as the Supreme Lord. The Supreme Lord spoke this verse. This verse is one of the four original and essential verses of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* which were manifested within the heart of Lord Brahmā. The entire text of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* was expanded from these four verses. After Lord Brahmā had performed severe austerities and penances for one thousand celestial years, this verse and the other three verses were revealed to him within his heart. They were dictated to him by the Supreme Lord (*SB* 1.1.1): *tene brahma hṛdā ya ādi-kavaye muhyanti yat sūrayaḥ.* “Vedic wisdom was revealed within the heart of Lord Brahmā.”

Since Lord Brahmā was the first created being of this universe, there was nothing else in existence at the time. He therefore needed to understand the nature of his own existence, and thus, the Lord dictated to him the instruction to perform austerities.

It is described in the Second Canto of the *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam* that when Lord Brahmā took his birth from the lotus flower emanating from the navel of Garbhodakśayī Viṣṇu, he was surrounded by darkness; nothing else had been created. Therefore, in an attempt to understand where he was, he climbed out of the seat of the lotus flower, down its stem, and came to the small portion of the Garbhodaka Ocean in Lord Viṣṇu’s navel. The Garbhodaka Ocean is the ocean in which Garbhodakśayī Viṣṇu lies.

In darkness, Lord Brahmā could not see where he was going. When he came to the Garbhodaka Ocean, he stuck his head into it and heard the vibration of the sound “*tapa,”* which means austerity. He took this sound to be divine and climbed back up the lotus flower and began performing austerities for one thousand celestial years. By dint of those austerities, the Lord became pleased and revealed this truth within his heart.

The previous verse states, “Prior to the cosmic creation, only I exist, and no phenomena exist, either gross, subtle or primordial. After creation, only I exist in everything, and after annihilation, only I remain eternally.” (*Cc. Ādi* 1.53) In other words, Kṛṣṇa was explaining to him how He is ultimately eternal and always existing. Everything that exists throughout the entire creation is resting on Him.

In this verse, the Lord says, “Whatever appears to be truth without Me is certainly My illusory energy, for nothing can exist without Me.” (*Cc. Ādi* 1.54) That is the nature of this temporary world; it appears to be real because we cannot see the transcendental reality. It is very difficult to see everything’s connection to Kṛṣṇa because everything seems to be disconnected from Him; everything appears to act independently from the will of the Lord.

Although material nature appears to act independently, the Supreme Lord is actually the controller of everything. Therefore, in this verse it is described that some things may appear to be true, but if they are not connected to the Lord, they are illusion. An example is the sun. It is described that the Absolute Truth is the substance, and the existence of the relative truth depends on its relationship with the Absolute. Similarly, the sunlight is the actual substance, and relative to the sunlight are darkness and the reflection of the sun. Neither darkness nor reflection can exist within the sun. Darkness is present when the sun is covered. Likewise, in this universe the sun appears to be a self-luminous object. It is a continuous source of light within the universe. However, when it is covered we see darkness. In the same way, a reflection is a projection of the sunlight into darkness. Similarly, it is said that the moon is luminous because of its reflection of the sun. Thus, the light of the moon is dependent upon the light of the sun, and the reflection of light is dependent upon the source of the light.

It is described that the material world is a reflection of the spiritual world. Whenever we see a reflection of light, we know there is a source for that light. In the same way, for whatever we see in this material world, we should know that its source is in the spiritual world. However, in the material world, when one is covered and cannot see the spiritual world, everything appears to be real. In truth, everything in this material world is illusory because it is temporary. That which is real is that which is eternal. That point is also explained in Śrīla Prabhupāda’s purport.

When the material manifestation appears to be very wonderful, it is due to a perverted reflection of the supreme sunshine, the Absolute Truth. It appears to act very wonderfully, but it acts wonderfully because of its connection with the Supreme Lord. Without being connected to the Lord nothing can happen. In the material world it is very difficult to see that connection, because we are covered by the illusory energy of the Lord.

The Lord has spiritual energy, which is eternal and full of knowledge. The Lord’s marginal energy is the living entities, which are also eternal. We, the living entities, can become covered by the Lord’s third energy, which is *māyā* or illusion, and it is visible in fruitive activities. These are the three primary energies of the Supreme Lord.

When the living entity is under the illusory curtain of *māyā,* he cannot see everything as being connected to the Lord. Rather, he sees everything connected to himself. That is the nature of *māyā*. The nature of the illusory curtain is that it covers us, and we see everything revolving around ourselves. Not only can we not see everything else connected to the Lord, but we also cannot see ourselves connected to the Lord. Therefore, we think that we can be independent. It is an illusion to think that we can be independent, because we cannot exist without the Lord.

Śrīla Prabhupāda gives the very simple example of the arm, which must be connected to the body. Any person with average intelligence knows that the leg, the arm, or the head cannot be separate from the body. Similarly, devotees of the Lord do not differentiate the Supreme Lord from anything or any being, including themselves. They understand that they also have a connection with the Supreme Lord. That connection is that they are eternal servants of the Supreme Lord. However, when they are covered by this illusory curtain, they connect themselves to this material world, and try to establish so many connections to themselves. However, they are all connected to this body, which is not the self.

That is the greatest form of illusion—false-ego. Illusion means to accept that which is not: “I am not this body, but everything I do is always connected to this body.” If we do not understand the difference between the body and the self, we cannot differentiate between material activity and spiritual activity. Under illusion, we try to connect everything to the false sense of ourselves. Therefore, the primary source of illusion in the material world is the sense of enjoyment when one thinks in terms of “I” and “mine.”

In the Second Canto of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* it is described that the illusory energy of the Lord cannot take precedence, being ashamed of her position. Those who are bewildered by the illusory energy always speak nonsense by thinking in terms of “I” and “mine.” Those who are bewildered by *māyā* always speak this way: “I am the enjoyer. I am the controller. These things belong to me. I am this body. I am happy. I am sad. I am hot. I am cold.” However, these are all connected to the body and not to the self. Happiness and distress are connected to the pleasures and pains of this body.

Kṛṣṇa says in the *Bhagavad-gītā,* “One who is not disturbed in mind even amidst the threefold miseries or elated when there is happiness, and who is free from attachment, fear and anger, is called a sage of steady mind.” (*Bg.* 2.56) He does not identify with the happiness and distress of this material world because he knows happiness and distress are only connected to the body and senses through the sense objects. Kṛṣṇa says (*Bg*. 2.14):

*mātrā-sparśās tu kaunteya*  
*śītoṣṇa-sukha-duḥkha-dāḥ*  
*āgamāpāyino ’nityās*  
*tāṁs titikṣasva bhārata*

**“O son of Kuntī, the nonpermanent appearance of happiness and distress, and their disappearance in due course, are like the appearance and disappearance of winter and summer seasons. They arise from sense perception, O scion of Bharata, and one must learn to tolerate them without being disturbed.”**

The happiness and distress one feels in connection with this body is not real; it is only connected to the body. The living entity is supposed to experience another form of happiness—happiness on the spiritual platform, the happiness in the spiritual world. However, being covered, we have no experience of that happiness, and we mistake the happiness that we experience in this material world as real happiness. Real happiness is not thinking, “I am happy”—this is only in connection with the body. When the body feels pleasure, we think we are happy. When the body feels pain, we think we are sad. Whenever we identify with anything in this material world, it is always subject to duality. “I experience something good, and when it is gone it is bad. I experience happiness, and when it is finished I am sad. I am experiencing sadness, and when I forget how sad I am, I am happy.” This is the duality of the material world, but it has no connection with reality.

*Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* explains that ultimately these dualities of the material world do not exist. They only appear to be real due to our conditioned intelligence. It is simply due to seeing things separate from Kṛṣṇa, and it is an imaginary experience. It is compared to the act of dreaming and desiring. We dream at night of something very wonderful, and when we wake up, we feel very bad because we see it was only a dream. On the other hand, if it is a very horrible dream, when we wake up, we feel very happy because it was only a dream. Both the happiness and distress that we experienced had no connection with reality; it was only connected to a dream.

That is what it is like in the material world. We experience happiness and distress, but it is actually not connected to reality. It has nothing to do with the real happiness that exists in the spiritual world. It is only a perverted reflection; it is not real. It is just like the reflection we see of light in the darkness. We know that the reflection is coming from some other source of light. In the same way, this material world is a reflection of the spiritual world, and whatever pleasure we experience in this material world is only a faint reflection of that which exists in the spiritual world, because the spiritual world is the actual substance.

Similarly, it is described here that in the spiritual world there is no need for sunshine, moonshine, fire, or electricity. In the spiritual world, everything is self-effulgent. The light and the effulgence is emanating from the transcendental body of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Even the light we see in this world—the light we see coming from the sun—is actually a reflection of the *brahmajyoti* coming from the spiritual world. The sun appears to be self-luminous, but it is getting its energy from the reflection of the *brahmajyoti* emanating from the transcendental body of the Lord. This is described in the *Brahma-saṁhitā*: “I worship Govinda, the primeval Lord, who is the source of all power. The glowing effulgence emanating from His transcendental form is absolute, complete, and unlimited, and it displays the countless varieties of planets with their different opulences and millions and millions of universes.” It is that effulgence coming from the body of the Lord that is the actual source of light. Therefore, in the spiritual world there is no need for sunlight, moonlight, fire, or electricity. In the spiritual world, everything is light. It is only in this material world that there is darkness. This material world is compared to a cloud in the spiritual sky, and wherever there is a cloud, it is blocking the light.

In the very beginning, when Lord Brahmā took his birth in the universe, everything was in darkness. The sun had not yet been created, and he was within the cloud of the *mahattattva,* which rests in a small portion of the spiritual sky. Thus, the nature of the material world is darkness. The illusion of the material world, being covered by that darkness, is when we cannot see things connected to Kṛṣṇa.

For example, the sunlight is connected to the effulgence of the body of the Lord, and the *mahat-tattva* cloud is also created from the energies of the Supreme Lord. The Lord expands Himself into the different *puruṣa-avatāras* and enters into each universe as Garbhodakśayī Viṣṇu. From the abdomen of Garbhodakśayī Viṣṇu comes Lord Brahmā, the first created being in the universe, and then Lord Brahmā creates all varieties within the universe. The Lord has given him both intelligence and the seeds of creation, just as a gardener is given seeds to plant, but does not create the seeds.

Thus, we have daydreams of things we would like to have or things we would like to avoid. Daydreams are also illusion. We think of something that we would like to have that would make us happy. Then, when we get it and become attached to the pleasure derived from it, it becomes a source of distress. We do not foresee the distress; we only dream of the happiness it will bring us. We do not think that when it is taken away, we will be distressed. “No, as long as it is connected to me, I will be happy.” However, the material nature has a tendency of taking everything away and changing that which was the source of happiness to one of distress.

For example, there is the story of King Citraketu. He was very anxious to get a son, but all his queens were barren and could not produce a son. Even though he was extremely wealthy and had everything in his kingdom that he could possibly want, he could not be happy because he did not have a son. Everything appeared to be vacant to him. He was given a benediction by Aṅgirā Muni that one of his wives would be able to produce a son for him. He was told that this son would be named Harṣa-śoka, which means “happiness and distress.” Aṅgirā Muni said, “This son will be a great source of joy for you and will also be a great source of distress.” Nevertheless, King Citraketu did not think about the distress. He was thinking that probably his son would not behave very properly, but that was fine. At least he would have a son, and that would make him happy. He took the food preparation that Aṅgirā Muni offered to him for his wife to take. By eating it, she would be able to produce a child.

King Citraketu had many queens, which was allowed in Vedic times. He gave the preparation to one queen, Kṛtadyuti, and she became pregnant and produced a son. When the son appeared, King Citraketu was ecstatic, and this news spread throughout the kingdom. King Citraketu started distributing charity to all the *brāhmaṇas* throughout the kingdom because he wanted his son to have a very good reputation and live a long and happy life. He did everything within his power to get such a benediction.

However, the co-wives of the king became very envious. Soon after the birth of the child, the king gave the wife who had produced the child much special attention, and he somewhat neglected the rest of the queens. One very sinful queen conspired with the other queens, and they decided to poison the son. One night, they poisoned Harṣa-śoka, and when Queen Kṛtadyuti entered into the chambers to see her son, she saw the lifeless child. She began beating her breasts and cried. When the king heard her crying, he entered into the chamber and saw her holding the lifeless child in her arms. He was thrown into such lamentation that it became intolerable for him.

It is described in the purport of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* that this child was an enemy of King Citraketu in a previous life. He had taken birth as the king’s son simply to give him pain. Nevertheless, King Citraketu was thinking, “No, this child will be the greatest source of my happiness.” All he was thinking about was the happiness he would get by producing a son. However, one cannot experience happiness without distress in this material world. It is not real happiness; it is only the reflection.

The *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* therefore describes that it is only an imaginary experience. Just like the night dream and daydream. In the daydream, we dream of something we would like to have or something we would like to avoid. We create a reality that has no existence beyond our own imagination. That is as far as it goes; it is not real. It seems real because that is the power of the illusory energy of the Lord. The only thing that is real is that which is connected to the Lord. “What appears to be truth without Me is certainly My illusory energy, for nothing can exist without Me. It is like a reflection of a real light in the shadows, for in the light there are neither shadows nor reflections.” (*Cc. Ādi* 1.54)

Therefore, it is very important to hear the message of *Bhagavad-gītā* and *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. In order to understand that which is beyond our experience of this world, we have to receive the transcendental message from the absolute source.

\* *What is happiness for people in this world?*

Real happiness is in connection with the Supreme Lord. It is described that one experiences real happiness when the Lord is pleased by his activities. The perfection of our life is to please the Lord by our activities. When the Lord is happy, we automatically become happy. Similarly, when the hand gives food to the stomach, the stomach and the hand are both satisfied. The hand’s nourishment is dependent upon the stomach. In the same way, when the Lord is pleased by our activities, we automatically become happy because the Lord wants to give us this happiness. He wants to share with us the pleasure of the spiritual world.

It is described that there are three types of happiness:

1\) Happiness derived from sense gratification or fruitive activity,

2\) happiness derived from salvation, and

3\) happiness derived from rendering devotional service to the Lord. The third type of happiness—rendering devotional service to the Lord—is real happiness, whereas the first two are temporary. Wherever there is temporary happiness, distress must follow. Real happiness is *ānanda*; it is transcendental to material happiness and continues forever. However, we have no experience of that happiness because we mistake happiness in this world for the mitigation of our distress. We think we are happy when our senses or mind are temporarily satisfied with some pleasure, but it is only a temporary mitigation of our distress.

Śrīla Prabhupāda gives the example that if somebody beats you over the head for twenty-three hours and then stops for the last hour, you would think that this is very wonderful. But that is not real happiness; it is only temporary relief from distress. Sense gratification is just like that. It does not last very long. We try to make it last long, but time has a way of making it go by very quickly. Therefore, real happiness is beyond that happiness. Real happiness is that which Kṛṣṇa directly gives to His devotees. It is the happiness derived from pleasing the Lord. When the Lord is pleased by our activities, that is the greatest source of our happiness. That happiness is eternal and will never be taken away.

## THE CONFIDENTIAL REASONS FOR THE APPEARANCE OF ŚRĪ CAITANYA MAHĀPRABHU

**Ādi-līlā: 4.162-181**

**Kishinev—June 30, 2000**

**Text 162**

*gopī-gaṇera premera ‘rūḍha-bhāva’ nāma*  
*viśuddha nirmala prema, kabhu nahe kāma*

**The love of the** ***gopīs*** **is called** ***rūḍha-bhāva*****. It is pure and spotless. It is not at any time lust.**

**Text 163**

*premaiva gopa-rāmānāṁ*  
*kāma ity agamat prathām*  
*ity uddhavādayo ’py etaṁ*  
*vāñchanti bhagavat-priyāḥ*

**The pure love of the gopīs has become celebrated by the name ‘lust.’ The dear devotees of the Lord, headed by Śrī Uddhava, desire to taste that love.** ***(Quoted from BRS 1.2.285)***

Because their desire to please the Lord was so intense, it was called “lust.” However, it had no connection to gratifying their senses. Gratification of one’s own senses is material lust, but when the object of gratification is Kṛṣṇa, it is completely spiritual.

**Text 164**

*kāma, prema,—doṅhākāra vibhinna lakṣaṇa*  
*lauha āra hema yaiche svarūpe vilakṣaṇa*

**Lust and love have different characteristics, just as iron and gold have different natures.**

**Text 165**

*ātmendriya-prīti-vāñchā—tāre bali ‘kāma’*  
*kṛṣṇendriya-prīti-icchā dhare ‘prema’ nāma*

**The desire to gratify one’s own senses is** ***kāma*** **\[lust\], but the desire to please the senses of Lord Kṛṣṇa is** ***prema*** **\[love\].**

**Purport**

(…) The predominated *gopīs* were bound to Kṛṣṇa in such pure love. For them there was no question of sexual love based on sense gratification. Their only engagement in life was to see Kṛṣṇa happy in all respects, regardless of their own personal interests. They dedicated their souls only for the satisfaction of the Personality of Godhead, Śrī Kṛṣṇa. There was not the slightest tinge of sexual love between the *gopīs* and Kṛṣṇa.

The author of *Śrī Caitanya-caritāmṛta* asserts with authority that sexual love is a matter of personal sense enjoyment. All the regulative principles in the *Vedās* pertaining to desires for popularity, fatherhood, wealth and so on are different phases of sense gratification. Acts of sense gratification may be performed under the cover of public welfare, nationalism, religion, altruism, ethical codes, Biblical codes, health directives, fruitive action, bashfulness, tolerance, personal comfort, liberation from material bondage, progress, family affection or fear of social ostracism or legal punishment, but all these categories are different subdivisions of one substance— sense gratification. All such good acts are performed basically for one’s own sense gratification, for no one can sacrifice his personal interest while discharging these much-advertised moral and religious principles. But above all this is a transcendental stage in which one feels himself to be only an eternal servitor of Kṛṣṇa, the absolute Personality of Godhead. All acts performed in this sense of servitude are called pure love of God because they are performed for the absolute sense gratification of Śrī Kṛṣṇa. However, any act performed for the purpose of enjoying its fruits or results is an act of sense gratification. Such actions are visible sometimes in gross and sometimes in subtle forms.

Prabhupāda is summarizing all the points that Śrīla Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī will later explain. Religion is more than simply following the codes of religion. One who follows the codes of religion often does so out of fear.

For example, a person may offer his food because he wants to be relieved from *karma*. This stage of engaging in religious activity is born of fear and not wanting to suffer. The desire to avoid suffering is not a selfless desire.

The *gopīs* are described here to be on a transcendental platform because they were not following any kind of religious or moral codes. They were breaking moral codes. They were not afraid to suffer because they were always thinking of Kṛṣṇa’s pleasure and were motivated by the desire to continuously please Him. Such personal sacrifices for the pleasure of the Lord makes the *gopīs’* love the highest.

*devarṣi-bhūtāpta-nṛṇāṁ pitṝṇāṁ*  
*na kiṅkaro nāyam ṛṇī ca rājan*

(SB 11.5.41)

Anyone who completely surrenders to Kṛṣṇa is no longer indebted or obliged to anyone else—neither the sages, nor the forefathers, nor humanity, nor the people in general, nor one’s kinsmen. He is always maintained by the Lord and is under the protection of His internal potency. Even though such a liberated soul is in a material body, he has no connection with it because he does not identify with it. His identification is only in his relationship with Kṛṣṇa.

*īhā yasya harer dāsye*  
*karmaṇā manasā girā*  
*nikhilāsv apy avasthāsu*  
*jīvan-muktaḥ sa ucyate*

(BRS 1.2.187)

Anyone whose mind and senses are completely engaged in the service of the Lord is a liberated soul, even though still remaining in the body. A liberated soul who is fully surrendered and engaged in service to Kṛṣṇa certainly has no other obligations to fulfill. All other obligations, besides satisfaction of the Lord, are ultimately connected to the body.

Śrīla Prabhupāda explains that public welfare, nationalism, altruism, ethical codes, health directives, personal comfort, and liberation are all connected to the body. Liberation is also connected to the body because one wants to become freed from entanglement of the body, but real liberation from material bondage is not complete until one is sheltered in service to the Supreme Lord.

The purport talks about the fear of social ostracism because the *gopīs* also had to face such a situation. They were leaving their husbands in a perfect Vedic setting. It was not a *Kali-yuga* setting where everybody wants to leave their husbands, and the husbands want to leave their wives.

This was not the culture at the time of the *gopīs*; it was very strict. The *gopīs*, because of their deep love for Kṛṣṇa, would sacrifice everything to love Kṛṣṇa better and to please Kṛṣṇa better. Therefore, *yoga-māyā*, the internal potency of the Lord, creates such a situation where the devotees will even transgress the regulative principles to love Kṛṣṇa better.

In the spiritual world, there is the conception of husband and wife, but it is only a conception. It is all arranged by *yoga-māyā* and it is meant to enhance the pastimes of the Lord. In the spiritual world, everything and everybody is connected to Kṛṣṇa. Everybody loves Kṛṣṇa. This is the meaning of the desire to please the senses of the Lord, which is *prema,* love.

Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī continues:

**The object of lust is only the enjoyment of one’s own senses. But love caters to the enjoyment of Lord Kṛṣṇa, and thus it is very powerful. Social customs, scriptural injunctions, bodily demands, fruitive action, shyness, patience, bodily pleasures, self-gratification and the path of varṇāśrama-dharma, which is difficult to give up—the gopīs have forsaken all these, along with their families, and suffered their relatives’ punishment and scolding, all for the sake of serving Lord Kṛṣṇa. They render loving service to Him for the sake of His enjoyment. That is called firm attachment to Lord Kṛṣṇa. It is spotlessly pure, like a clean cloth that has no stain.**

**Therefore lust and love are quite different. Lust is like dense darkness, but love is like the bright sun. Thus there is not the slightest taint of lust in the gopīs’ love. Their relationship with Kṛṣṇa is only for the sake of His enjoyment.**

**O dearly beloved! Your lotus feet are so soft that we place them gently on our breasts, fearing that Your feet will be hurt. Our life rests only in You. Our minds, therefore, are filled with anxiety that Your tender feet might be wounded by pebbles as You roam about on the forest path. (Quoted from SB 10.31.19)**

**The gopīs do not care for their own pleasures or pains. All their physical and mental activities are directed toward offering enjoyment to Lord Kṛṣṇa. They renounced everything for Kṛṣṇa. They have pure attachment to giving Kṛṣṇa pleasure.**

**O My beloved gopīs, you have renounced social customs, scriptural injunctions and your relatives for My sake. I disappeared behind you only to increase your concentration upon Me. Since I disappeared for your benefit, you should not be displeased with Me. (Quoted from SB 10.32.21)**

**Lord Kṛṣṇa has a promise from before to reciprocate with His devotees according to the way they worship Him. “In whatever way My devotees surrender unto Me, I reward them accordingly. Everyone follows My path in all respects, O son of Pṛthā.” (Quoted from Bg. 4.11)**

**That promise has been broken by the worship of the gopīs, as Lord Kṛṣṇa Himself admits.**

**O gopīs, I am not able to repay My debt for your spotless service, even within a lifetime of Brahmā. Your connection with Me is beyond reproach. You have worshiped Me, cutting off all domestic ties, which are difficult to break. Therefore please let your own glorious deeds be your compensation. (Quoted from SB 10.32.22) Now, whatever affection we see the gopīs show for their own bodies, know it for certain to be only for the sake of Lord Kṛṣṇa. (Cc. Ādi 4.166-181)**

**Purport**

The selfless love of Godhead exhibited by the *gopīs* cannot have any parallel. We should not, therefore, misunderstand the carefulness of the *gopīs* in their personal decoration. The *gopīs* dressed themselves as beautifully as possible just to make Kṛṣṇa happy by seeing them. They dedicated their bodies, and everything they possessed, to the service of Śrī Kṛṣṇa, taking it for granted that their bodies were meant for His enjoyment. They dressed themselves with the understanding that Kṛṣṇa would be happy by seeing and touching them.

The *gopīs* had no ulterior motive. Out of love they were only thinking of giving Kṛṣṇa pleasure. Of course, this is often misunderstood. Śrīla Prabhupāda always emphasized that persons who are not devotees of the Lord find it very difficult to understand the love of God that is manifest by the *gopīs*. Prabhupāda also explains that the subject of the very intimate exchanges of Lord Kṛṣṇa with the *gopīs* is not meant for public discussion, because people in general think of these relationships in terms of sexual love. *Ātmavan manyate jagat* (unknown source). A person thinks according to his own mentality and projects it on everything else.

Since the Supreme Lord does not have any desires that He needs fulfilled, there is no trace whatsoever of sexual love in His dealings with the *gopīs*. He is *ātmārāma,* completely self-satisfied. He is simply giving pleasure to the *gopīs,* and the *gopīs* are thinking of giving pleasure to the Lord. They are satisfying the Lord by dressing themselves very nicely for Him. The Lord derives great pleasure from this, and there is a continuous reciprocation going back and forth. When the *gopīs’* ecstasy and love for the Lord increases, the Lord’s love increases, and when the *gopīs* see the Lord’s love increase, their happiness and love increase. Thus it is an ever-increasing reciprocal exchange with unlimited variety. The varieties that we experience here in the material world are simply perverted reflections of spiritual variety.

Prabhupāda explains that when a tree is reflected on the water, the bottom of the tree is at the top of the reflection. In a similar way, this material world is a perverted reflection of the spiritual world. That which is very degraded in the material world is actually a perverted reflection of the highest state of love in the spiritual world. It is completely free from lust and is eternally manifested in the spiritual world. In the material world we see the perverted reflections, but we think that they are real. But actually, that reflection has its source in the spiritual world.

The relationships of friendship, parental affection, and conjugal affection all exist here in the material world. However, in the spiritual world these relationships are all in connection with Kṛṣṇa. Because everything in the material world is motivated by sense gratification, all the relationships are instead connected to oneself. Every one of us is in need; we are not self-sufficient. Only Kṛṣṇa is self-sufficient. Therefore, lacking self-sufficiency, we establish connections through friendship and love in this material world. Through family and society, we attempt to fulfill what we lack. This feeling is present because we have lost our connection with Lord Kṛṣṇa. Connected to Kṛṣṇa, all our desires are even better fulfilled by Kṛṣṇa than we could fulfill them ourselves.

When we hear descriptions of the devotees in the spiritual world expressing lamentation, like the *gopīs* lamenting due to feelings of separation from Kṛṣṇa, we may think that they are not satisfied. But their feelings of lamentation are spiritual emotions—not mundane lamentation—which are actually the greatest ecstasy. Their lamentation actually enhances their love for Kṛṣṇa and their absorption in Him. Therefore, their thoughts are always entangled in Kṛṣṇa.

The feelings of separation are created by the internal potency of the Supreme Lord to heighten the pleasure of union. It is not possible to understand these affairs of the spiritual world via the relationships that we have in the material world. One can only understand them by hearing transcendental descriptions and narrations, which are given in *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. Even then, one can only just begin to understand. The ocean of devotion is very deep, and thus it is not possible to know its full depths.

Rūpa Gosvāmī therefore says that one can drown in just one drop of this ocean. As inconceivable as it seems, the *ācāryas* and Śrīla Prabhupāda are trying to help us understand one drop in the ocean of love of God through their instructions and purports.

Due to this deep love, the *gopīs* sacrificed social customs, scriptural injunctions, and their relationships with relatives only for Kṛṣṇa’s sake. They even sacrificed the risk of disobeying Kṛṣṇa’s instruction when He told them to go home. He was speaking according to Vedic injunctions about all the duties of the wife, yet the *gopīs* were ready to sacrifice those injunctions, even coming directly from the Lord, simply to give pleasure to Kṛṣṇa.

“It is a symptom of real affection that one breaks the order of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, not caring for the regulative principles, to associate with Him.” (*Cc. Antya* 10.6)

If one carries out Kṛṣṇa’s order, then He is certainly pleased. But when the *gopīs* chastise Him and disobey His order, their spontaneous love steals His mind away from the hymns of the *Vedas*. Such love controls the Lord.

## LORD CAITANYA IN FIVE FEATURES

**Ādi-līlā: 7.1**

**Moscow—May 27, 2002**

**Text 1**

*agaty-eka-gatiṁ natvā*  
*hīnārthādhika-sādhakam*  
*śrī-caitanyaṁ likhyate ’sya*  
*prema-bhakti-vadānyatā*

**Let me first offer my respectful obeisances unto Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu, who is the ultimate goal of life for one bereft of all possessions in this material world and is the only meaning for one advancing in spiritual life. Thus let me write about His magnanimous contribution of devotional service in love of God.**

**Purport**

A person in the conditioned stage of material existence is in an atmosphere of helplessness, but the conditioned soul, under the illusion of *māyā*, or the external energy, thinks that he is completely protected by his country, society, friendship and love, not knowing that at the time of death none of these can save him. The laws of material nature are so strong that none of our material possessions can save us from the cruel hands of death. In the *Bhagavad-gītā* (13.9) it is stated, *janma-mṛtyu-jarā-vyādhi-duḥkha-doṣānudarśanam*: one who is actually advancing must always consider the four principles of miserable life, namely, birth, death, old age and disease. One cannot be saved from all these miseries unless he takes shelter of the lotus feet of the Lord. Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu is therefore the only shelter for all conditioned souls. An intelligent person, therefore, does not put his faith in any material possessions, but completely takes shelter of the lotus feet of the Lord. Such a person is called *akiñcana*, or one who does not possess anything in this material world. The Supreme Personality of Godhead is also known as Akiñcanagocara, for He can be achieved by a person who does not put his faith in material possessions. Therefore, for the fully surrendered soul who has no material possessions on which to depend, Lord Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu is the only shelter. (…)

Queen Kuntī says in her prayers (*SB* 1.8.27): *namo ’kiñcana-vittāya.* “I offer my obeisances unto You, Kṛṣṇa, who is the friend of *akiñcana*—those who are materially impoverished.”

She also says (*SB* 1.8.26):

*janmaiśvarya-śruta-śrībhir*  
*edhamāna-madaḥ pumān*  
*naivārhaty abhidhātuṁ vai*  
*tvām akiñcana-gocaram*

“My Lord, Your Lordship can easily be approached, but only by those who are materially exhausted. One who is on the path of \[material\] progress, trying to improve himself with respectable parentage, great opulence, high education and bodily beauty, cannot approach You with sincere feeling.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda gives a very wonderful purport to this verse, where he speaks about both Akiñcana-gocara and *akiñcana:*

**Purport**

Being materially advanced means taking birth in an aristocratic family and possessing great wealth, an education and attractive personal beauty. All materialistic men are mad after possessing all these material opulences, and this is known as the advancement of material civilization. But the result is that by possessing all these material assets one becomes artificially puffed up, intoxicated by such temporary possessions. Consequently, such materially puffed up persons are incapable of uttering the holy name of the Lord by addressing Him feelingly, “O Govinda, O Kṛṣṇa.” It is said in the *śāstras* that by once uttering the holy name of the Lord, the sinner gets rid of a quantity of sins that he is unable to commit. Such is the power of uttering the holy name of the Lord. There is not the least exaggeration in this statement. Actually the Lord’s holy name has such powerful potency. But there is a quality to such utterances also. It depends on the quality of feeling. A helpless man can feelingly utter the holy name of the Lord, whereas a man who utters the same holy name in great material satisfaction cannot be so sincere. A materially puffed up person may utter the holy name of the Lord occasionally, but he is incapable of uttering the name in quality.

Therefore, the four principles of material advancement, namely (1) high parentage, (2) good wealth, (3) high education and (4) attractive beauty, are, so to speak, disqualifications for progress on the path of spiritual advancement. The material covering of the pure spirit soul is an external feature, as much as fever is an external feature of the unhealthy body. The general process is to decrease the degree of the fever and not to aggravate it by maltreatment. Sometimes it is seen that spiritually advanced persons become materially impoverished. This is no discouragement. On the other hand, such impoverishment is a good sign as much as the falling of temperature is a good sign. The principle of life should be to decrease the degree of material intoxication which leads one to be more and more illusioned about the aim of life. Grossly illusioned persons are quite unfit for entrance into the kingdom of God. (*SB* 1.8.26)

This purport is brilliant. Śrīla Prabhupāda makes very clear the position of a devotee who is *akiñcana* and the position of the Lord, who is Akiñcana-gocara. The Lord can be approached by those who are *akiñcana*. Queen Kuntī explains in this verse that those who are not materially exhausted cannot chant Kṛṣṇa’s names feelingly.

The holy name of the Lord is non-different from the Lord, and our constitutional position is to be fully dependent on Him. The whole cause of our conditional existence is due to misuse of our minute independence. When living entities misuse their minute independence, they turn their attention away from Kṛṣṇa and towards the world of illusion, although the Lord can satisfy all their necessities of life.

This is described by Bilvamaṅgala Ṭhākura, who explains that when devotees, by good fortune, develop unflinching devotion to the transcendentally beautiful, youthful form of the Lord, liberation stands waiting at their doorstep with her hands folded, waiting to serve them. Just behind liberation are also piety, economic development, and sense gratification, who are also very eagerly waiting for the opportunity to serve. Therefore, if we develop devotion to the Lord, all these things become available to us.

In reference to this verse, there is a difference between *dharma* and *sanātana-dharma*. *Dharma* refers to pious activities. By the performance of pious activities, one obtains economic development. With economic development, one gets an opportunity to gratify the senses. When one satisfies the senses to the limit and still cannot find satisfaction of the self, he becomes frustrated and strives for liberation. In each of these activities, the living entity is searching for satisfaction. When one wants material opulence as a result of his religious activities, he pursues economic development, which will enable him to enjoy. When he can no longer enjoy the objects of sense gratification, he wants to enjoy relief by liberation. In every stage he wants satisfaction, but there is no full satisfaction in any of those stages.

However, in devotional service there is full satisfaction. One gets the results of all these endeavors for satisfaction, but not necessarily immediately. Someone may misunderstand and think, “If I engage in devotional service I should become wealthy immediately, because economic development is waiting to serve me.” But of course, one who serves with that motive is really not engaging in pure devotional service.

There are many examples in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* of those who approach the Lord with mixed motives. Nevertheless, even if one has mixed motives, the Lord can purify those motives. The Lord can satisfy the devotee far more than the achievement of *dharma, artha, kāma,* or *mokṣa* because the Lord gives Himself to the devotee. When the Lord gives Himself, the devotee feels wonderful satisfaction and does not want anything else. The devotee is fully satisfied with the lotus feet of the Lord. Pure devotees of the Lord are always praying in this way, “I do not want anything except Your devotional service.”

Prahlāda Mahārāja prayed in this way. He said, “I do not want to be a merchant. I do not serve You for something in return, for such a person is not very advanced in devotion.” When Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva wanted to offer some benediction, Prahlāda did not want it. He was not interested in any material benediction. He only wanted to serve. Thus, the devotee is so satisfied by the Lord that automatically he no longer has the desire for material wealth. The Lord gives him whatever is necessary for serving Him, and therefore the devotee is satisfied in any condition of life.

We should not foolishly think that by getting devotional service we can get economic development and gratification of the material senses. Devotees have their senses gratified spiritually. Their senses are fully satisfied by beholding the transcendental form of the Lord. As devotees advance in devotional service, gradually elevating to the highest stages of love of God, the Lord fully satisfies their senses. A devotee actually experiences the fragrance emanating from the body of the Supreme Lord. He beholds the beautiful form of the Lord and then feels the loving embrace of the Lord. This is how the Lord reciprocates with the devotee and satisfies his senses. The devotee gets all his senses completely saturated with Kṛṣṇa. That is why he has senses. His senses are meant to be saturated completely in Kṛṣṇa.

The devotee’s mind is meant to be absorbed in thoughts of Kṛṣṇa, just as the minds of the *gopīs* cannot stop thinking about Him. Because they cannot stop thinking about Kṛṣṇa, they are always utilizing their tongues for speaking about that blue cowherd boy and His wonderful activities. When their eyes behold the form of the Lord, they curse Lord Brahmā for making eyes that blink. When their eyes blink only for a moment, they think, “For that one moment when my eyes are blinking, I cannot see Kṛṣṇa.” Imagine the intensity of their feelings when they express, “It is intolerable to blink!” Kṛṣṇa’s form is so beautiful for their eyes to behold.

A devotee smells the fragrance coming from Kṛṣṇa’s body and constantly hears the sound of Kṛṣṇa’s flute. That sound is one of Kṛṣṇa’s arrows, which He uses just as Cupid uses his arrows to shoot into hearts. When a devotee hears the song emanating from Krsna’s flute, immediately his heart is captivated by love for the Supreme Lord. One’s sense of touch is fully satisfied by the Lord’s loving embrace. A devotee who experiences this feels full sense satisfaction. He is so satisfied that he actually loses consciousness of the external world.

The senses are like venomous serpents waiting to strike the object. However, for a devotee whose senses are fully satisfied by Kṛṣṇa, the Lord will give Himself. When Kṛṣṇa gives Himself, it means He gives everything. Such is the power of *bhakti*. It is known as *śrī-kṛṣṇākarṣaṇī*, which means that this is the only means for approaching Kṛṣṇa. *Śrī-kṛṣṇākarṣaṇī* is the stage when a devotee achieves pure love of God and experiences unlimited bliss. He can actually control Kṛṣṇa by his love.

We know that the *gopīs’* love for Kṛṣṇa was so powerful because they gave everything up for Him. Kṛṣṇa could not reciprocate their love. Kṛṣṇa felt Himself completely incapable of repaying them for their love. Nevertheless, He did repay that love of the *gopīs* by appearing as Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

In order to chant Kṛṣṇa’s names with sincere feeling, one has to be *akiñcana*, which means that he is not dependent upon material wealth or mundane relationships in this world. Sometimes, to favor His devotee, the Lord places him in a situation where he has nothing to depend on except Him. Kṛṣṇa will take everything away from His devotee, leaving him fully dependent on Him.

In other words, when a devotee is in a situation where he has no one to depend on except Kṛṣṇa, he is the most fortunate person. He is helpless. All his artificial defenses for protection in this material world have been destroyed. We become dependent on our material possessions and even on our own abilities. We think that by our own abilities we will be able to achieve success. However, Kṛṣṇa says, “I am the ability in man.” (*Bg.* 7.8) Kṛṣṇa knows the heart of the devotee, and He knows how to make His devotee dependent on Him. Therefore, the devotee has to be very expert to be able to understand when Kṛṣṇa is putting him in that most favorable situation. He may find himself in that situation but may not see it as favorable. That is due to the influence of *māyā*. He may think, “Why have I been put in a situation like this? I should have some other result. Why am I not getting it? I should be getting happiness. Why am I not getting happiness? I should be getting profit. Why am I not getting profit?” When a devotee sees that nothing external can fully satisfy him, he should see it as an opportunity to take shelter of Kṛṣṇa’s holy name.

A devotee sees all reversals as favorable for the execution of devotional service. Who does not experience reversals in life? Is there anybody who can claim they never experienced reversals in life? We are going in a particular direction, but some unknown force is sending us another way. A devotee sees it as being a result of his past sinful activities. What is the devotee supposed to do in such a circumstance?

*tat te ’nukampāṁ su-samīkṣamāṇo*  
*bhuñjāna evātma-kṛtaṁ vipākam*  
*hṛd-vāg-vapurbhir vidadhan namas te*  
*jīveta yo mukti-pade sa dāya-bhāk*

“My dear Lord, one who earnestly waits for You to bestow Your causeless mercy upon him, all the while patiently suffering the reactions of his past misdeeds and offering You respectful obeisances with his heart, words and body, is surely eligible for liberation, for it has become his rightful claim.” (*SB* 10.14.8)

Kṛṣṇa says in *Bhagavad-gītā* (2.14)*:*

*mātrā-sparśās tu kaunteya*  
*śītoṣṇa-sukha-duḥkha-dāḥ*  
*āgamāpāyino ’nityās*  
*tāṁs titikṣasva bhārata*

One has to learn to tolerate these things without being disturbed. A devotee is able to tolerate because he accepts it as the *karma* of his past misdeeds. When he tolerates, he should offer his obeisances to Kṛṣṇa and continue to glorify Him. He should continue engaging in Kṛṣṇa’s service with his body, mind, and words, and continue to utilize his senses for the service of the Lord. Engaging the mind means accepting favorably that which is pleasing to the Lord. The business of a materially conditioned mind is to accept and reject, but a devotee should utilize his mind for accepting everything favorable for the service of the Lord and rejecting everything unfavorable. That means accepting one’s current situation as favorable for the service of the Lord: “Kṛṣṇa has put me in this difficult situation for my own benefit. Now, what can I do? How can I take advantage of it? I can approach Kṛṣṇa with sincere feeling. I can chant Kṛṣṇa’s names feelingly.” Chanting feelingly means chanting with the desire to be engaged in the Lord’s service.

When a devotee perceives how his *anarthas* are seemingly inflamed (increased) under certain circumstances, he should see it as an opportunity to pray to the Lord to help him become free from these *anarthas*. One should never think that if one does not see them it means that they are not there. Under certain circumstances they will become aroused; quite often it is in the association of devotees. Such is the power of the association of devotees.

When one sees his *anarthas* inflamed in this way, he should not think, “The devotee is making these *anarthas* appear!” No, they are already there, waiting to come out. When the *anarthas* come out, a devotee should pray to the Lord, “Please help me to become free from these *anarthas.”* He does not pray, “Please help me to become free from the association of devotees.” If somebody thinks that way, he may get the fulfillment of his desires, but those *anarthas* will remain deep within the core of his heart. So while chanting the holy name of the Lord, we should pray to the Lord to help us overcome our *anarthas*, with the understanding that by chanting feelingly and helplessly, the Lord will help us.

We sometimes hear the expression: “The Lord helps those who help themselves.” We help ourselves by cultivating the right attitude in approaching the Lord. The right attitude is to know that only Kṛṣṇa can help us. Lord Caitanya is *patita-pāvana,* the uplifter of the most fallen. We cannot become saved by any means other than His mercy. There is no other way to overcome these *anarthas* within our heart. Prabhupāda says in the purport that these external symptoms, such as material possessions, high education, and respectable parentage, are just like a fever, which is an external symptom of an unhealthy body. The fever is inside the body, just as the *anarthas* are inside the body. When those *anarthas* are in the body, they manifest themselves externally. One has to clean himself from these *anarthas,* and there is no potency as great as the holy name of the Lord. This is how a devotee becomes *akiñcana,* and Kṛṣṇa, being Akiñcana-gocara, gives Himself to that devotee who makes Lord Caitanya his goal in life.

**Ādi-līlā: 7.62–92**

**Donetsk—June 17, 2000**

The verses in the Seventh Chapter of *Ādi-līlā* are a description of Lord Caitanya’s meeting with Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī when He was traveling to Benares (Vārāṇasī). Vārāṇasī was the center of impersonalism. There were many impersonalists and Māyāvādī *sannyāsīs* there who were critical of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu. They thought that Mahāprabhu was a sentimentalist. They accused Him of not studying *Vedānta* philosophy in the association of other learned Vedāntists.

At that time, the recommended process for achieving renunciation was by intense study of the *Vedānta* philosophy, and those persons who took part in *saṅkīrtana,* the congregational chanting of the holy names of the Lord, were considered to be sentimentalists. Therefore, many of the Māyāvādī *sannyāsīs* were critical of Caitanya Mahāprabhu. The leader of all these Māyāvāda *sannyāsīs* was Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī.

Sārvabhauma Bhaṭṭācārya thought that it would be very difficult for Lord Caitanya to maintain His renunciation because He was very young. Sārvabhauma Bhaṭṭācārya recommended that Mahāprabhu hear *Vedānta* from him continuously for seven days. He thought hearing *Vedānta* would help Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu maintain His *sannyāsa* vows.

When Caitanya Mahāprabhu was traveling throughout India on His way to Vrndavāna (the first time), He completely avoided Benares because He felt it was not the right time to go there. However, on His return trip, He decided to go there to fulfill His mission.

He stopped at the house of Candraśekhara Ācārya. While there, a *brāhmaṇa* invited Lord Caitanya to come to his home where he was hosting many Māyāvādī *sannyāsīs*. He begged Him to accept his invitation. Ordinarily, Lord Caitanya would not mix with the Māyāvādī *sannyāsīs,* even though He had taken initiation from Keśava Bhāratī, who was from the impersonalist Bhāratī*-*sampradāya. Thus, He would generally not accept such invitations. But on this particular occasion, He decided that He would accept the invitation of the *brāhmaṇa.*

The following day, He went to the home of the *brāhmaṇa.* When He entered, He washed His feet and then sat down in that same place. He felt Himself to be in an inferior position to all the other *sannyāsīs* and therefore decided that He would sit in the most unclean place, where everyone would wash their feet. Lord Caitanya then manifested, by His mystic power, an effulgence as brilliant as the illumination of millions of suns. Māyāvādī *sannyāsīs* are very much attracted by this effulgence. The Lord sat there for some time, radiating this effulgence, and when the Māyāvādīs saw this, they stood up and respected Lord Caitanya. This was the beginning of Lord Caitanya’s preaching mission in Vārāṇasī. He first wanted to attract their minds and thus thought, “What better way to attract Māyāvādīs than to manifest an effulgence more brilliant than millions of suns?” Māyāvādīs think that when one takes *sannyāsa* and comes to the perfected stage, he becomes Nārāyaṇa.

Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī wanted to ask Caitanya Mahāprabhu some questions (*Cc. Ādi* 7.62–63):

**Text 62**

*prakāśānanda-nāme sarva sannyāsi-pradhāna*  
*prabhuke kahila kichu kariyā sammāna*

**The leader of all the Māyāvādī** ***sannyāsīs*** **present was named Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī, and after standing up he addressed Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu as follows with great respect.**

**Text 63**

*ihāṅ āisa, ihāṅ āisa, śunaha śrīpāda*  
*apavitra sthāne vaisa, kibā avasāda*

**Please come here. Please come here, Your Holiness. Why do You sit in that unclean place? What has caused Your lamentation?**

**Purport**

Here is the distinction between Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu and Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī. In the material world everyone wants to introduce himself as very important and great, but Caitanya Mahāprabhu introduced Himself very humbly and meekly. The Māyāvādīs were sitting in an exalted position, and Caitanya Mahāprabhu sat in a place that was not even clean. Therefore the Māyāvādī *sannyāsīs* thought that He must have been aggrieved for some reason, and Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī inquired about the cause for His lamentation.

In other words, they could not understand this display of humility. They thought that He must be lamenting for some particular reason and thus was sitting in this place. These Māyāvādīs were very much accustomed to great pomp and respect, and thought, “Caitanya Mahāprabhu is actually one of us. He has taken initiation from Keśava Bhāratī. Why should He sit there? He should instead sit with us.” However, at the same time, they were disturbed, thinking, “He is one of us, but He associates with sentimentalists.” Thus, Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī asks the Lord some questions about this. He says, “Do not sit there. Come sit here.”

**The Lord replied, “I belong to a lower order of** *sannyāsīs***. Therefore I do not deserve to sit with you.” Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī, however, caught Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu personally by the hand and seated Him with great respect in the midst of the assembly.**

**Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī then said, “I understand that Your name is Śrī Kṛṣṇa Caitanya. You are a disciple of Śrī Keśava Bhāratī, and therefore You are glorious. You belong to our Śaṅkara-sampradāya and live in our village, Vārāṇasī. Why then do You not associate with us? Why is it that You avoid even seeing us? You are a** *sannyāsī***. Why then do You indulge in chanting and dancing, engaging in Your** *saṅkīrtana* **movement in the company of fanatics? Meditation and the study of Vedānta are the sole duties of a** *sannyāsī***. Why do You abandon these to dance with fanatics? You look as brilliant as if You were Nārāyaṇa Himself. Will You kindly explain the reason that You have adopted the behavior of lower-class people?”**

**Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu replied to Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī, “My dear sir, kindly hear the reason. My spiritual master considered Me a fool, and therefore he chastised Me.” (***Cc. Ādi* **7.66-71)**

Lord Caitanya further explained, “My spiritual master considered Me to be foolish and unqualified to study *Vedānta*.”

Prabhupāda explains in the purport to verse seventy-two that Lord Caitanya was taking the position of those in Kaliyuga. Generally, people in Kali-yuga are not very qualified for studying *Vedānta*. Lord Caitanya explained that His spiritual master considered Him unqualified to study *Vedānta* and told Him, “Instead of studying *Vedānta*, You should chant the Kṛṣṇa *mantra*.” The words used are *‘kṛṣṇa-mantra’ japa sadā* (*Cc. Ādi* 7.72)*,* which mean that He was instructed to always chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra*, which is *ei mantra-sāra*. *Ei mantra-sāra* means that the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra* is the essence of all *mantras,* Vedic hymns, and *Vedānta*. The chanting of the holy name of Lord Kṛṣṇa is the ultimate religious principle for the living entity.

There are many verses from *śāstra* that explain the importance of the chanting of the holy names of the Lord. Prabhupāda would often quote the verse from the *Kali-santaraṇa Upaniṣad:*

*iti ṣoḍaśakaṁ nāmnāṁ*  
*kali-kalmaṣa-nāśanaṁ*

“After searching through all the Vedic literature, one cannot find a method of religion more sublime for this age than the chanting of Hare Kṛṣṇa.” These sixteen words of the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* are the only means by which one can counteract the sinful effects of the Age of Kali.

Therefore, Lord Caitanya is emphasizing here that the essence of all Vedic literature is contained within the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra*. Not only is it the essence, but it is also the commentary. If you take all the Vedic literatures and then make a commentary, the conclusion will be that the chanting of the holy name of the Lord is the ultimate religious principle because everything is contained within chanting. By the process of chanting, one can realize the essence of the Lord within His holy name. In the Age of Kali, the Lord incarnates in His holy name (*Cc*. *Ādi* 17.22):

*kali-kāle nāma-rūpe kṛṣṇa-avatāra*  
*nāma haite haya sarva-jagat-nistāra*

Anyone who associates with the holy name is associating with the Lord directly and is certainly delivered. By the chanting of the holy name of the Lord one can realize His form, qualities, and pastimes, which are all present within the holy name. Moreover, the highest platform of the realization of the holy name is when one realizes that the Lord, with His form, qualities, pastimes, and name, is imbued with *rasa (*the relationship that one has with the Lord).

Lord Caitanya came to make the whole world dance in ecstasy by realizing the four mellows (*rasas*) of loving devotional service. This is also described in *Ādi-līlā* of *Caitanyacaritāmṛta*. The method is through the distribution of the holy name of the Lord. Therefore, the essence of all the Vedic literatures is to chant the holy name of the Lord without offense. That is the perfection of studying all the *Vedas.*

Prabhupāda would also quote the following verse from the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (*SB* 3.33.7):

*aho bata śva-paco ’to garīyān*  
*yaj-jihvāgre vartate nāma tubhyam*  
*tepus tapas te juhuvuḥ sasnur āryā*  
*brahmānūcur nāma gṛṇanti ye te*

Anyone whose tongue is always decorated with the holy name of the Lord is considered to have already performed all kinds of sacrifices in his previous life, to have visited many different places of pilgrimage, and to have all the qualities of an Āryan.

Lord Caitanya established the incessant chanting of the holy name as the ultimate religious principle by His own personal example. His tongue was always decorated with the name of Kṛṣṇa. This is also explained in the *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam*: One endowed with sufficient intelligence will worship the Supreme Lord, who has a golden complexion, is always chanting the name “Kṛṣṇa,” and is always surrounded by His associates. Caitanya Mahāprabhu assumed the role of a devotee of Kṛṣṇa to teach by His example the method of self-realization in this age.

Lord Caitanya repeats the words that He heard from His spiritual master, Īśvara Purī, saying that in this age of Kali there is no religious principle other than chanting the holy names. This is His answer to Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī’s question, “Why are you not studying *Vedānta*?”

It does not mean that one should not study the Vedic literatures. Devotees study Vyāsadeva’s commentary on *Vedānta-sūtra,* the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* and consider it as *Vedānta*. The Māyāvādīs consider Śaṅkarācārya’s commentary on *Vedānta-sūtra,* the *Śārīraka-bhāṣya,* as *Vedānta*. The Māyāvādīs think *Vedānta* means *Māyāvāda-bhāṣya,* which is Śaṅkarācārya’s commentary and the impersonal philosophy.

The Māyāvādī *sannyāsīs* think that real renunciation can be achieved by studying this *Māyāvāda-bhāṣya*. Of course, this does not diminish what was previously said about the essence of all Vedic scriptures being the chanting of the holy names of the Lord. Certainly, it is also the essence, as is studying *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, which culminates in constant chanting and glorification. However, the point that Lord Caitanya is trying to establish is that the study of *Vedānta* alone will not enable one to achieve the perfect stage.

**\[Lord Caitanya said to Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī\] “Since I received this order from My spiritual master, I always chant the holy name, but I thought that by chanting and chanting the holy name I had been bewildered. While chanting the holy name of the Lord in pure ecstasy, I lose Myself, and thus I laugh, cry, dance and sing just like a madman. Collecting My patience, therefore, I began to** **consider that chanting the holy name of Kṛṣṇa had covered all My spiritual knowledge.” (***Cc. Ādi* **7.77–79)**

Prabhupāda explains in the purport to verse seventy-nine that Lord Caitanya is hinting that to chant the holy name of Kṛṣṇa one does not need to speculate on the philosophical aspects of the science of God, as the impersonalists do. They study *Vedānta,* and then they speculate about the conclusions and think that by this process of mental speculation they can achieve the perfect stage.

When Lord Caitanya says that all His spiritual knowledge has been covered, He means that He has lost all inclination for speculation. He was trying to instruct Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī to also give up his speculation and take up chanting the holy name. Lord Caitanya continued explaining to Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī His reasons for which He was chanting the holy name of the Lord and avoiding the study of *Vedānta*. He described how pleased His spiritual master was when he saw Him manifesting ecstatic symptoms of love for God.

Īśvara Purī had told Lord Caitanya, “This is very good. You have actually attained the supreme goal o life. You have pleased me very much by attaining this level of spiritual advancement. Now I feel very obliged to You.” Īśvara Purī then gave further instructions. He said, “You should continue to dance, chant, and perform *saṅkīrtana* in the association of devotees. Furthermore, You should go out and preach the value of chanting Kṛṣṇa *nāma*. By this process, You will be able to deliver all fallen souls.” (*Paraphrasing Cc. Ādi* 7.91–92) This is also the essence of Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s mission. Prabhupāda quotes Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta in the purport, saying that when the disciple has attracted the spiritual master by his sincere service, he develops an attraction for chanting and dancing in the association of all the devotees. In other words, by serving the order of the spiritual master, one develops a taste for glorifying the Lord. This is also explained in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam (1.2.16):*

*śuśrūṣoḥ śraddadhānasya*  
*vāsudeva-kathā-ruciḥ*  
*syān mahat-sevayā viprāḥ*  
*puṇya-tīrtha-niṣevaṇāt*

By serving those devotees who are completely free from vice, great service is done. By such service, one develops a taste for hearing and chanting about Lord Kṛṣṇa. Therefore, when Īśvara Purī saw the ecstasy that was manifest in Lord Caitanya, he was very pleased. Lord Caitanya said, “I become bewildered by chanting this name, and sometimes I laugh, dance, and sing.”

Īśvara Purī said, “Yes, You have achieved the stage of love of God.”

Lord Caitanya responded, “I could not stop. I am incessantly chanting these names of the Lord.” Thus, Lord Caitanya is showing again, by His own example and by strictly following the instructions of the spiritual master, that one is supposed to develop this taste for hearing and chanting in the association of devotees. Without obtaining this taste for hearing and chanting, it is very difficult to go out and induce others to take up chanting.

Therefore, Īśvara Purī says, “Continue to chant, dance, and perform *saṅkīrtana* in the association of devotees.” Certainly, that is the perfect stage—to be in the association of devotees and to relish chanting and hearing the glories of the Lord. One gets this taste by strictly following and serving the instructions of the spiritual master. This is how one awakens the sweet taste of the holy name of the Lord.

Often devotees ask, “How can we get a taste? Where is it to be found? When I chant Hare Kṛṣṇa, I do not experience bliss; tears are not coming to my eyes; I am not falling to the ground.” Of course, one could artificially manifest it—fall on the ground and manifest tears—but that is not ecstatic love of Godhead. Those who imitate the behavior of those who have ecstatic love of Godhead are verily condemned.

In this regard, there was a *brāhmaṇa* who imitated Haridāsa Ṭhākura’s ecstatic symptoms of love. The *brāhmaṇa* was envious because everyone was respecting Haridāsa Ṭhākura due to the symptoms he was exhibiting. The *brāhmaṇa* decided, “I should get respect because he is a low-class man, and I am a *brāhmaṇa*.” He fell on the ground and started crying and rolling, imitating Haridāsa. A snake charmer who saw this could understand the *brāhmaṇa’s* cheating mentality and took a stick and started beating him.

Thus, imitative tears are obviously not also the means for obtaining the mercy of the Lord. One should not try to imitate persons who are manifesting such symptoms. If one wants to acquire some taste for hearing and chanting, as the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* says, “One has to faithfully serve the devotees who have no vice.” By serving them and being in their association one develops some taste. There is no taste when the heart is still impure, and therefore we cannot actually relish the full benefit of the holy name of the Lord.

Rūpa Gosvāmī says that when he chants the two syllables “kṛṣ-ṇa,” his senses become stunned, and the Lord, in the form of the holy name, dances in the courtyard of his heart. He immediately experiences the presence of the Lord from the two syllables “kṛṣ-ṇa.”

Thus, certainly some great pleasure is derived from chanting the holy name of the Lord. It is the highest pleasure. There is no higher pleasure than the constant chanting of the holy name. The *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* states that those persons who are constantly engaged in glorifying the Lord transcend all the miseries of the material world. By becoming absorbed in the holy name of the Lord, they give up identification with this material body.

Once, when Lord Nityānanda was traveling with His associates, they forgot about eating. They did not eat for months. They were simply wandering about in ecstasy, chanting the holy name of the Lord. They were feeling continuous nourishment from the Lord on a transcendental platform. Such transcendental nourishment gives one so much satisfaction that he forgets his material body. That is the result of getting the association of the Lord on the spiritual platform. Śrīla Prabhupāda explains that one can understand that he is advancing by the degree that he loses his attraction to the material world and the material body.

Prabhupāda would often quote a verse from the Eleventh Canto of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* that explains that just as a person who is engaged in eating experiences pleasure, nourishment, and relief from his hunger, similarly, a person engaged in service to the Lord experiences devotion to the Lord, direct perception of the Lord, and detachment from material senses. These three things come simultaneously for a person who is engaged in service to the Lord. Therefore, one knows that he is advancing and becoming detached from the body if he sees that he is becoming more and more conscious of the Lord and of the desires within his heart. He should know that the obstacles are the attachments within the heart. As a result of this attachment, one will not experience a higher taste. When one becomes freed from attachments, he begins to experience some superior taste.

The *Nectar of Devotion* explains that sometimes a devotee who has taste can reflect that taste into the hearts of others. In this way, we may be able to experience some shadow of taste. We get a glimpse of this taste by the association of devotees who have taste. This is how we can experience some taste for chanting the holy name of the Lord—we have to associate with the devotees who are experiencing the Lord’s presence (*SB* 3.25.25): *satāṁ prasaṅgān mama vīrya-saṁvido.* “In the association of pure devotees, discussion of the pastimes and activities of the Supreme Personality of Godhead is very pleasing and satisfying to the ear and the heart.” *Bhavanti hṛt-karṇa-rasāyanāḥ kathāḥ.* (*SB* 3.25.25) *Hṛt* is the heart and *karṇa* is the ear. We develop attraction to hearing, which becomes very pleasing to the ear and the heart. *Tajjoṣaṇād āśv apavarga-vartmani / śraddhā ratir bhaktir anukramiṣyati.* (*SB* 3.25.25)

As we advance on this path, gradually attachments go away. We then become fixed and develop attraction to the Lord. When that attraction to the Lord is developed, real devotion and devotional service can begin. We begin to execute devotional service out of spontaneous attraction to the Lord. However, until we come to that platform, we have to derive this pleasure from the association of those who already have it. Therefore, the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* states that in the association of these devotees, one has to hear, and this will give one some attraction to hearing and chanting. If we want to develop the taste, then we have to serve such persons. One way of serving is by hearing. Īśvara Purī said to Lord Caitanya, “I am obliged to You because You heard me. You took up my instructions. Because You heard, I am feeling obliged to You. You are chanting and dancing, experiencing this taste. This is actual service.”

Rūpa Gosvāmī says in his *Lālasāmayī* that one has to develop eagerness within the heart for this taste and the association of the Lord and become greedy for it.
