We can associate with the Lord by hearing about His pastimes and by associating with His devotees. When a person has attracted the attention of the spiritual master by devotional service, the spiritual master authorizes such a devotee to deliver fallen souls in all possibilities.

Those who are not advanced prefer to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa in a solitary place. However, Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta describes that this is a type of cheating process because they are trying to imitate highly exalted devotees. “Rather, everyone should endeavor to preach the cult of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu in all parts of the world and thus become successful in spiritual life.” (*Cc. Ādi* 7.92, Purport)

In other words, when one derives some satisfaction from the association with devotees, one should go out and give it to others. This is the essence of these teachings. Īśvara Purī said to Lord Caitanya, “Yes, dance and chant with devotees. Furthermore, go out and preach the value of this chanting. By this process, you will be able to deliver others.” Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta also goes on to say (*Cc. Ādi* 7.92, Purport):

One who is not very expert in preaching may chant in a secluded place, avoiding bad association, but for one who is actually advanced, preaching and meeting people who are not engaged in devotional service are not disadvantages. A devotee gives the non-devotees his association but is not affected by their misbehavior.

He is not affected because he is deriving his spiritual satisfaction in the association of devotees. This higher taste actually protects him, and he therefore goes out and gives association to non-devotees and is unaffected by their association.

Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura also makes this same point. He explains that even a householder, in executing household duties, has to work and deal with non-devotees. Nevertheless, he says, “Working and dealing with non-devotees does not mean associating with them. ‘Association’ means that there is affection for the non-devotional qualities. When we start developing affection for the non-devotional qualities, then we are associating with them, and there is danger of becoming contaminated.”

We can protect ourselves by affection for the association of devotees. Affection for associating with devotees is centered on hearing and chanting the glories of the Lord, and we derive satisfaction from those activities. Therefore, when we go out to preach, non-devotee association does not affect us. “Thus by the activities of a pure devotee even those who are bereft of love of Godhead get a chance to become devotees of the Lord one day.” (*Cc. Ādi* 7.92, Purport)

There is much more in Lord Caitanya’s discussion with Prakāśānanda Sarasvatī. Ultimately, Lord Caitanya defeats Prakāśānanda and all his followers, who become Vaiṣṇavas. Such is the mercy of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu, who gives the higher taste. By His mercy, He attracted all these Māyāvādī *sannyāsīs* to the holy name of the Lord. However, as He explained in this instruction, the goals are to hear and chant in the association of devotees, to awaken one’s attraction to the Lord, and to go out and preach the glories of the holy name of the Lord for the benefit of others. This is Lord Caitanya’s movement. This is what Śrīla Prabhupāda has given us in this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. Prabhupāda is Lord Caitanya’s ambassador, and he has given us this mission to relish hearing and chanting in the association of devotees and to go out and give it to others. He said that we should not become cheaters simply by trying to imitate exalted devotees by going to some secluded place and trying to attain the highest platform. It is better that one goes out and preaches Kṛṣṇa consciousness. By so doing, he can achieve the mercy of the Lord.

*\* If I am alone somewhere, doing some work, and some people hear my chanting and start joining me—first jokingly, but then seriously—is it offensive?*

No, it is not an offense to chant in a secluded place. However, it is cheating to think that one can go to a secluded place and by staying in seclusion achieve the elevated stage of love of Godhead.

Prabhupāda is talking about cheaters who go to a secluded place, but their minds are not engaged. Their mind is thinking about all kinds of sense gratification since it is not engaged in relishing the holy name of the Lord. Therefore, instead of associating with the holy name of the Lord, they are simply associating with their material desires, and this is cheating.

Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta therefore says that one should not adopt this cheating mentality. One should chant the holy names of the Lord, go out and preach, and induce others to take up the chanting. By such chanting and preaching, the mind becomes controlled. When one is preaching, he has to think of Kṛṣṇa, and is forced to control his mind. Whereas, when he is chanting the holy name of the Lord, he can sit there and think, “Just see, I am sitting here and I am chanting the holy name of the Lord.” If someone asks him, “Where were you today?” he will respond, “Oh, I was in the forest chanting the holy names for ten hours, completely absorbed.” Actually, he is simply associating with his material desires. Therefore, if he is not really absorbed in chanting the holy names of the Lord, it is better if he goes out and preaches. That is superior engagement for the mind. One has to think of the Lord and depend on Him.

Thus, Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta is describing this cheating mentality. Nevertheless, certainly if one is doing something else by himself (selling flowers, for example), and while doing that he is chanting the holy name of the Lord, then that is not the same thing. We should chant the name of the Lord always—whether we are distributing *prasādam* or whether we are cooking. Whatever we are doing, we can chant the holy name of the Lord.

When Lord Caitanya was cleaning the Guṇḍicā temple, all the devotees were carrying brooms and pots, and as they were handing the pots of water to each other, they were saying *hare kṛṣṇa hare kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa kṛṣṇa hare hare*. Although cleaning the Guṇḍicā temple, they were still chanting Kṛṣṇa’s name. Therefore, whatever we are doing, we should always chant the name of the Lord.

## THE AUTHOR RECEIVES THE ORDERS OF KṚṢṆA AND GURU

**Ādi-līlā: 8.33–49**

**Kiev—April 29, 2000**

We are approaching a section in the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* about Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura. Many of these verses are composed by Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī, and he is glorifying the great achievements of Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura. Because Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī was also expanding upon the pastimes of Lord Caitanya, he did not want to commit any kind of offense by transgressing the etiquette regarding repeating what has already been described by a previous *ācārya.*

Of course, it is our duty to repeat that which we have heard from the previous *ācāryas,* but when compiling literature, one has to be very careful not to present things in such a way to surpass the achievements of the previous *ācāryas*. In other words, one should always be thinking of following in the footsteps of the previous *ācāryas*. He should never think that his writings or his commentaries surpass that which has already been presented. This is the principle of disciplic succession. He should think that whatever he is writing or performing is by the grace of the previous *ācāryas.*

Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī has written about pastimes of Lord Caitanya, but he explains that he is specifically writing about those pastimes that Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura did not elaborate on. In many places of the *Caitanya-bhāgavata,* Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura writes, “I will not write any more about this subject matter out of fear that the book will become too voluminous; however, later all of these pastimes will be described.” Thus, Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura knew that these particular pastimes that he had not written about would later be described. Therefore, out of etiquette, Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī explained, “I have taken the order to expand upon those pastimes that Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura has not described.” Hence the title of the eighth chapter of *Ādi-līlā*, *The Author Receives the Orders of Kṛṣṇa and Guru.* He is giving a full historical account of how he was given the order from superior Vaiṣṇavas to expand on these pastimes.

Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura is a partial incarnation of Śrīla Vyāsadeva and also a cowherd boy in Lord Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes. He appeared in the womb of Nārāyaṇī devī to fulfill his purposes for his descent to this world. Nārāyaṇī devī was the niece of Śrīvāsa Paṇḍita. Śrīvāsa Paṇḍita had four brothers. One brother was named Śrī Nalina Paṇḍita, and he was the father of Nārāyaṇī devī. Nārāyaṇī was married to a great Vaiṣṇava named Vaikuṇṭha dāsa, who departed from this world and went back to the spiritual world while his son was still in the womb of Nārāyaṇī. Nārāyaṇī devī and her son were therefore under the protection of another great Vaiṣṇava*,* Vāsudeva Datta.

It was at this time that Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura studied extensively the pastimes of the Lord. He heard many of the pastimes of the Lord from his mother. His mother was the first recipient of Lord Caitanya’s mercy at the beginning of the *saṅkīrtana* movement. In the house of Śrīvāsa Paṇḍita, Lord Caitanya had taken some betel nut to eat. Nārāyaṇī devī then entered, and the Lord gave her the remnants of this foodstuff. Thus, she became completely empowered with love of God. At that time, Nārāyaṇī devī was only four years old, and Lord Caitanya wanted to show that anybody could develop love of God without constraints. By giving this love to Nārāyaṇī devī, He also wanted to prove to Śrīvāsa Paṇḍita that that was the purpose for which He had descended. Nārāyaṇī devī was one of Lord Caitanya’s contemporaries, and she personally witnessed many of the Lord’s pastimes. Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura thus heard many of the Lord’s pastimes from his mother.

He also learned much from the diaries of Murāri Gupta. Murāri Gupta was a physician in Navadvīpa, and he would keep very nice diaries of the pastimes of the Lord, especially in the Lord’s early childhood. The writings in his diary were called *Śrī Caitanya-carita*. It was from Murāri Gupta’s notes that Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura had personally written of the Lord’s pastimes. Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī is also writing from the diaries of Murāri Gupta, as well as from what he had heard from Raghunātha dāsa Gosvāmī. He has taken these pastimes and has put them into literary form so that we can consume these remnants of foodstuffs, which they have left for us. Whenever we take these remnants of foodstuffs given by the great *ācāryas,* it is meant to be relished in the association of devotees.

**Text 33**

*ore mūḍha loka, śuna caitanya-maṅgala*  
*caitanya-mahimā yāte jānibe sakala*

**O fools, just read** ***Śrī Caitanya-maṅgala*****! By reading this book you can understand all the glories of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.**

**Purport**

Śrī Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura’s *Śrī Caitanya-bhāgavata* was originally entitled *Śrī Caitanya-maṅgala*, but when Śrīla Locana dāsa Ṭhākura later wrote another book named *Śrī Caitanya-maṅgala*, Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura changed the name of his own book, which is now therefore known as *Śrī Caitanya-bhāgavata*. The life of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu is very elaborately described in the *Caitanya-bhāgavata*, and Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī has already informed us that in his *Śrī Caitanya-caritāmṛta* he has described whatever Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura has not mentioned. This acceptance of *Śrī Caitanya-bhāgavata* by Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī indicates his acceptance of the disciplic succession. A writer of transcendental literature never tries to surpass the previous *ācāryas*. (*Cc. Ādi* 8.33)

This point is also very nicely illustrated in the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* in the story about Vallabha Bhaṭṭa, who was very proud of his learning. He was very anxious to have people hear him speak because he wanted to show his expertise on commenting on *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.* He even wanted to display this in front of Lord Caitanya. Lord Caitanya understood his heart and understood the deep pride residing there. Every time Vallabha Bhaṭṭa would try to say something in the presence of the Lord, the Lord would always divert the situation so that he could not speak. Because the Lord would never listen to him speak, all the other devotees of the Lord would follow His example. Thus, every time Vallabha Bhaṭṭa would try to speak to somebody and get somebody to listen to him, nobody would. His whole objective in life was completely destroyed by the Lord.

How could a person become recognized as a great scholar unless someone listens to him? Vallabha Bhaṭṭa was thinking, “With all my learning and knowledge, I cannot get through to anyone. I cannot get anybody to listen to me.” The reason Lord Caitanya refused to listen to him was Vallabha Bhaṭṭa would not accept the commentaries of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* that were previously given by Śrīdhara Svāmī. He thought his own commentaries on *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam* were superior to Śrīdhara Svāmī’s, and so he would not quote from them. Lord Caitanya therefore refused to listen to him to teach him this lesson.

It was very difficult for Vallabha Bhaṭṭa to accept the position that he had been defeated by the Lord. He realized his mistake, but still his pride remained and he could not fully surrender to the Lord. He thus started his own *sampradāya*. However, Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī is not following that example. He is following the principles of disciplic succession.

**Text 34**

*kṛṣṇa-līlā bhāgavate kahe veda-vyāsa*  
*caitanya-līlāra vyāsa—vṛndāvana-dāsa*

**As Vyāsadeva has compiled all the pastimes of Lord Kṛṣṇa in** ***Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,*** **Ṭhākura Vṛndāvana dāsa has depicted the pastimes of Lord Caitanya.**

**Text 35**

*vṛndāvana-dāsa kaila ‘caitanya-maṅgala’*  
*yāṅhāra śravaṇe nāśe sarva amaṅgala*

**Ṭhākura Vṛndāvana dāsa has composed** ***Śrī Caitanya-maṅgala.*** **Hearing this book annihilates all misfortune.**

**Text 36**

*caitanya-nitāira yāte jāniye mahimā*  
*yāte jāni kṛṣṇa-bhakti-siddhāntera sīmā*

**By reading** ***Śrī Caitanya-maṅgala*** **one can understand all the glories and truths of Lord Caitanya and Nityānanda and come to the ultimate conclusion of devotional service to Lord Kṛṣṇa. (*****Cc. Ādi*** **8.34–36)**

This is the difference between a Vaiṣṇava mentality and a non-devotee’s proud mentality. A Vaiṣṇava glorifies another for the very same thing that he himself is doing. However, a proud person would think, “Oh, Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura has written about this, but he has not given very much knowledge; therefore I am forced to write these books so that you can all be enlightened.” In other words, a Vaiṣṇava is not trying to promote himself. In fact, this whole chapter explains that the reason why Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī wrote *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* was because he was asked by others to do it. He was a great Kavirāja, a great writer, and all the Vaiṣṇavas appealed to him: “Please, use your skills for elaborating on the pastimes of the Lord.” A Vaiṣṇava, very naturally, out of humility, thinks himself unqualified. He never thinks that he has the qualification to do something. He feels like a servant of the Vaiṣṇavas.

When all the Vaiṣṇavas requested Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī to write the pastimes, he accepted their order, yet always felt himself unqualified. He even received the benediction of the Madana-mohana Deity when the garland fell from the Deity and was put around his neck. He took that as a sign of confirmation that the Lord wanted him to do this. The Vaiṣṇavas personally witnessed this, and they also could understand that he was blessed by the Lord. It was only at this point that Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī agreed to write. Without the order, he was not interested in doing it.

It is also described by Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura that Lord Nityānanda left His home and traveled for eight years. However, wherever He traveled, He did not preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness because He was waiting to meet Lord Caitanya, at which time He would receive the order to preach; He did not do it otherwise. After receiving the order, He began manifesting His *līlā*—His merciful pastimes of inducing love of Godhead upon living beings.

Similarly, Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī also received the order, and therefore, on that basis, he compiled *Śrī Caitanyacaritāmṛta*. As we see from these verses, he is glorifying Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura. He says that anybody who reads what Śrīla Vṛndāvana

dāsa Ṭhākura has given about the pastimes of the Lord can understand all the glories and truths about Lord Caitanya and Lord Nityānanda, and they can come to the ultimate conclusion. In other words, he is saying that this *Caitanya -maṅgala,* which had previously been written, is complete in and of itself and gives love of Godhead to anybody who hears its pastimes. However, for the pleasure of the Vaiṣṇavas, and because the Lord’s pastimes are unlimited, they can always be expanded upon. Therefore, the Vaiṣṇavas are always anxious to take that order to expand the pastimes of the Lord, and, in this way, give pleasure to the previous *ācāryas,* the Supreme Lord, and all those who are hearing.

Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī actually bestows many benedictions in his writings and glorifies the readers of *Śrī Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. He says, “O readers of *Śrī Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* I take the dust from your feet and I place it on my head. I am your servant. I am very eager to take your footdust, because I am very anxious to hear these pastimes of the Lord.” The Vaiṣṇava mentality is that of always wanting to glorify devotees of the Lord. We see that Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī had this mood. He glorifies the previous authors, the readers, and the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

**Purport**

*Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* is the authoritative reference book from which to understand devotional service, but because it is very elaborate, few men can understand its purport. *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* is the original commentary on the *Vedānta-sūtra*, which is called *nyāya-prasthāna*. It was written to enable one to understand the Absolute Truth through infallible logic and argument, and therefore its natural commentary, *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, is extremely elaborate. Professional reciters have created the impression that *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* deals only with Kṛṣṇa’s *rāsa-līlā*, although Kṛṣṇa’s *rāsa-līlā* is described only in chapters 29 through 33 of the Tenth Canto. They have in this way presented Kṛṣṇa to the Western world as a great woman-hunter, and therefore we sometimes have to deal with such misconceptions in preaching. Another difficulty in understanding *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* is that the professional reciters have introduced *bhāgavata-saptāha*, or seven-day readings of the *Bhāgavatam*. They want to finish *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* in a week, although it is so sublime that even one verse of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, if properly explained, cannot be completed in three months. Under these circumstances, it is a great aid for the common man to read Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura’s *Caitanya-bhāgavata*, for thus he can actually understand devotional service, Kṛṣṇa, Lord Caitanya and Nityānanda. Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī has said *(Brs*. *1.2.101).*

*śruti-smṛti-purāṇādi-pañcarātra-vidhiṁ vinā*  
*aikāntikī harer bhaktir utpātāyaiva kalpate*

“Devotional service to the Lord that ignores the authorized Vedic literatures—the *Upaniṣads*, *Purāṇas*, *Nārada*-*pañcarātra*, etc.—is simply an unnecessary disturbance in society.” Due to misunderstanding *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, people are misled regarding the science of Kṛṣṇa. However, by reading Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura’s book one can very easily understand this science. (*Cc*. *Ādi* 8.36, Purport)

**Text 37**

*bhāgavate yata bhakti-siddhāntera sāra*  
*likhiyāchena iṅhā jāni’ kariyā uddhāra*

**In** ***Śrī Caitanya-maṅgala*** **\[later known as** ***Śrī Caitanya-bhāgavata*****\] Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura has given the conclusion and essence of devotional service by quoting the authoritative statements of** ***ŚrīmadBhāgavatam.***

**Text 38**

*‘caitanya-maṅgala’ śune yadi pāṣaṇḍī, yavana*  
*seha mahā-vaiṣṇava haya tatakṣaṇa*

**If even a great atheist hears** ***Śrī Caitanya-maṅgala,*** **he immediately becomes a great devotee.**

**Text 39**

*manuṣye racite nāre aiche grantha dhanya*  
*vṛndāvana-dāsa-mukhe vaktā śrī-caitanya*

**The subject matter of this book is so sublime that it appears that Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu has personally spoken through the writings of Śrī Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura. (*****Cc. Ādi*** **8.37–39)**

In the purport, Śrīla Prabhupāda states that Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Gosvāmī Mahārāja used to say that the time would come when the people in the world will learn Bengali in order to read *Śrī Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta also used to say that one should read *Śrī Caitanya-bhāgavata* a hundred times. This is how a Vaiṣṇava actually relishes hearing the pastimes of the Lord. He says that every time the devotee hears these different pastimes of the Lord, especially from realized souls, he becomes increasingly satisfied.

Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī is saying that it appears that Lord Caitanya is personally speaking through the writings of Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura. We thus see the same mood of glorification as was described previously. This is something that is very noteworthy in the writings of Śrīla Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī and Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura. It is a very integral part of Vaiṣṇava culture to glorify Vaiṣṇavas. It is such an integral part because generally Vaiṣṇavas do not like to criticize. It is extremely difficult for them to even hear or speak criticism of others; it is painful for their heart. They do not want to speak it, and they also do not even want to see anything that they could criticize. The natural quality of Vaiṣṇavas is that they always want to see and magnify the good qualities of other Vaiṣṇavas. By doing this, they remove attention from themselves. This is very commonly seen throughout the writings of the Vaiṣṇava *ācāryas,* as well as in the personal dealings with others.

There was a *brāhmaṇa* who approached Lord Caitanya and said, “I have spoken criticisms of Vaiṣṇavas in the past because I did not realize their glories. I therefore unknowingly said bad things about them. How can I become freed from having unknowingly committed these offenses?”

Lord Caitanya said, “When a person takes poison and then drinks the nectar of immortality, the nectar can gradually counteract the effects of the poison, and in this way he can become freed from the effects of the poison. Likewise, you have used your tongue to speak ill of Vaiṣṇavas. With that same tongue, you should glorify the activities of the Vaiṣṇavas with proper prayers and poetry for whatever offenses you have unknowingly committed.”

Such glorification is not false praise; it is how a genuine and humble devotee sees others, seeing himself as very fallen. He is always anxious to glorify whatever good qualities he sees in others. Therefore, when he sees these good qualities, his natural tendency is to expand them. We can just see how opposite the non-devotee mentality is. As soon as a proud person sees a small fault, his natural tendency is to want to expand it. He wants to broadcast it to the world. This is due to pride. He always wants to push himself forward as someone who is to be recognized. However, a Vaiṣṇava always tries to stay in the background. If he comes forward, it is only because of an order; otherwise, he is not very anxious.

Similarly, a *mahā-bhāgavata* devotee sees how everyone else is serving Kṛṣṇa, but not himself. Sometimes, by the desire of the Lord, he steps down to the second-class platform to preach and introduce Kṛṣṇa consciousness to others, although he is not inclined to push himself forward. In such a way, a Vaiṣṇava always keeps himself humble; he never thinks himself to be anything but an instrument to be used only under a higher order.

When a Vaiṣṇava does anything that may appear to be putting himself forward, such as writing books, preaching, etc., he is only doing it because he feels pushed by the order of other Vaiṣṇavas. However, because his natural inclination is to always stay in the background, a Vaiṣṇava will always glorify other Vaiṣṇavas, saying, “I have no good qualities. If it appears that there is any success from my endeavors, it is only because I have served the order of a superior Vaiṣṇava.”

Once, upon arriving in Ukraine from the United States, I was speaking on the phone for quite some time with my godbrother, Rādhānatha Mahārāja. We were having a very lengthy discussion, which was entirely based on him trying to prove that his coming here to Ukraine was only because of my order, and that I am his master and he is my servant. Every time he came up with an argument, I had to come up with another argument to defeat him and prove that I am actually his servant, and he is my master. Every time I would offer him an argument, he would take it and defeat it. We were talking on the phone for two hours and I finally ended the whole conversation by saying, “Alright, I am defeated. Now I understand that you have defeated me, but because *you* defeated *me*, it means that *you* are superior to *me*.” The conversation ended there. I won! Of course, the difference between him and me is that he is humble; I was just trying to defeat him!

Sometimes we see that in the society of devotees, it is very natural that they eagerly want to glorify others. They are not very enthusiastic to take credit for themselves. This chapter of *Śrī Caitanya-caritāmṛta* is about Śrīla Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī placing himself in that position. This natural humility is very attractive. When the Lord sees such humility displayed by His devotee, He accepts that devotee as very dear. Humility is described as being the ornament of a devotee:

*titikṣavaḥ kāruṇikāḥ*  
*suhṛdaḥ sarva-dehinām*  
*ajāta-śatravaḥ śāntāḥ*  
*sādhavaḥ sādhu-bhūṣaṇāḥ*

“The symptoms of a *sādhu* are that he is tolerant, merciful and friendly to all living entities. He has no enemies \[*ajāta-śatravaḥ*\], he is peaceful, he abides by the scriptures, and all his characteristics are sublime.” (*SB* 3.25.21)

The qualities described in this verse are ornaments or decorations on the body of a Vaiṣṇava. The Lord becomes so attracted by these ornaments of a devotee that even He feels bound by such devotees. That is what Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī is saying—that Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura has so wonderfully glorified the conclusions of devotional service that it appears that Lord Caitanya is personally speaking through his writings. This is the power of Vaiṣṇavas. They actually have the power to remember the Lord due to their natural quality of humility.

**I offer millions of obeisances unto the lotus feet of Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura. No one else could write such a wonderful book for the deliverance of all fallen souls. Nārāyaṇī eternally eats the remnants of the food of Caitanya Mahāprabhu. Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura was born of her womb. What a wonderful description he has given of the pastimes of Lord Caitanya! Anyone in the three worlds who hears it is purified. I fervently appeal to everyone to adopt the method of devotional service given by Lord Caitanya and Nityānanda and thus be freed from the miseries of material existence and ultimately achieve the loving service of the Lord. Śrīla Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura has written** *Śrī Caitanya-maṅgala* **and therein described in all respects the pastimes of Lord Caitanya. He first summarized all the pastimes of the Lord and later described them vividly in detail. The pastimes of Lord Caitanya are unlimited and unfathomable. Therefore, in describing all those pastimes, the book became voluminous. He saw them to be so extensive that he later felt that some had not been properly described. He ecstatically described the pastimes of Lord Nityānanda, but the later pastimes of Caitanya Mahāprabhu remained untold. The devotees of Vṛndāvana were all very anxious to hear those pastimes. (***Cc. Ādi* **8.40–49)**

Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī goes on to explain how to receive the request to expand on those untold pastimes of Lord Caitanya.

**Ādi-līlā: 8.50–61**

**Kiev—May 3, 2000**

**Text 50**

*vṛndāvane kalpa-drume suvarṇa-sadana*  
*mahā-yogapīṭha tāhāṅ, ratna-siṁhāsana*

**In Vṛndāvana, in a great place of pilgrimage underneath the desire trees, is a golden throne bedecked with jewels. On that throne sits the son of Nanda Mahārāja, Śrī Govindadeva, the transcendental Cupid. Varieties of majestic service are rendered to Govinda there. His garments, ornaments and paraphernalia are all transcendental. In that temple of Govindajī, thousands of servitors always render service to the Lord in devotion. Even with thousands of mouths, one could not describe this service. In that temple the chief servitor was Śrī Haridāsa Paṇḍita. His qualities and fame are known all over the world. (***Cc. Ādi* **8.50–54)**

**Purport**

Śrī Haridāsa Paṇḍita was a disciple of Śrī Ananta Ācārya, who was a disciple of Gadādhara Paṇḍita.

**He was gentle, tolerant, peaceful, magnanimous, grave, sweet in his words and very sober in his endeavors. He was respectful to everyone and worked for the benefit of all. Diplomacy, envy and jealousy were unknown to his heart. The fifty qualities of Lord Kṛṣṇa were all present in his body. (***Cc. Ādi* **8.55–57)**

**Purport**

In the *Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu*, the transcendental qualities of Śrī Kṛṣṇa are mentioned. Among these, fifty are primary (*ayaṁ netā su-ramyāṅgaḥ*, etc.), and in minute quantity they were all present in the body of Śrī Haridāsa Paṇḍita. Since every living entity is a part of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, all fifty of these good qualities of Śrī Kṛṣṇa are originally minutely present in every living being. Due to his contact with material nature, these qualities are not visible in the conditioned soul, but when one becomes a purified devotee, they all automatically manifest themselves. This is stated in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (5.18.12), as mentioned in the text below. (…) (*Cc. Ādi* 8.57)

All these qualities of Lord Kṛṣṇa are listed in *Bhaktirasāmṛta-sindhu*. There it is described that the Lord has sixty-four qualities, of which the living entity, in the perfected state, can obtain up to fifty. There are certain qualities that are only manifest in the Lord. There are five additional qualities that are sometimes manifested in the personalities of Lord Brahmā and Lord Śiva. These are ever-freshness, omniscience, having a body that is *sac-cid-ānanda,* possessing all mystic *yoga* perfection, and changeless. There are also five qualities that are only manifested in the form of Lord Nārāyaṇa: 1) He possesses supreme power; 2) by destroying the demons, He delivers them; 3) unlimited universes are manifested from the pores of His body; 4) He has the ability to attract exalted persons who are satisfied in themselves; 5) He is the original source of all incarnations. These qualities are only possessed by the Lord and not by the living entities. Finally, there are four qualities that are only manifest in Kṛṣṇa: (1) His flute-playing can attract every living entity in the creation; (2) He manifests His *līlā* in Vṛndāvana; (3) He is always surrounded by His loving associates, who are manifesting symptoms of ecstatic love of Godhead; (4) His beauty is unrivaled by any other living entity throughout the whole creation.

These additional fourteen qualities are not manifested by the living entity even in the perfected state. However, here it is described that Haridāsa Paṇḍita manifested all of the fifty qualities of Kṛṣṇa. They were simultaneously present in his character. Thus, these are natural qualities of the living entity. These fifty qualities, which are listed in the *Nectar of Devotion,* are not fully manifested when the living entity is covered by the material energy. However, by being engaged in devotional service to the Lord, these natural qualities automatically become manifest. Thus, Śrīla Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī is glorifying the qualities that are manifest in Haridāsa Paṇḍita.

He describes some of them: He is respectful to everyone; works for the benefit of everyone; is gentle, tolerant, peaceful, and grave; speaks very sweetly; and is very sober in his endeavors. These qualities are very attractive, and Haridāsa Paṇḍita embodied them.

Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī goes on to quote a very famous verse from the Fifth Canto of the *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam,* which Prabhupāda quoted hundreds of times:

*yasyāsti bhaktir bhagavaty akiñcanā*  
*sarvair guṇais tatra samāsate surāḥ*  
*harāv abhaktasya kuto mahad-guṇā*  
*manorathenāsati dhāvato bahiḥ*

“All the demigods and their exalted qualities, such as religion, knowledge and renunciation, become manifest in the body of one who has developed unalloyed devotion for the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Vāsudeva. On the other hand, a person devoid of devotional service and engaged in material activities has no good qualities. Even if he is adept at the practice of mystic yoga or the honest endeavor of maintaining his family and relatives, he must be driven by his own mental speculations and must engage in the service of the Lord’s external energy. How can there be any good qualities in such a man?” (*Cc. Ādi* 8.58, quoted from *SB* 5.18.12)

Of course, nobody should misinterpret this verse and think that automatically one has good qualities simply because he dons Vaiṣṇava dress. This verse explains that for one who is unflinching in unalloyed devotional service to the Lord, automatically these good qualities will manifest. Therefore, this is a necessary criterion for having these good qualities. To the degree that one is unflinchingly engaged in service to the Lord, gradually these qualities will become manifest.

We should also understand that when we speak of good qualities, we speak of the standard that has been given to us by the Lord. We cannot speculate or fabricate what is a good quality and what is not a good quality. It is the Lord who authenticates the good qualities in the living entity; it is the Lord who recognizes these qualities. Therefore, if one is engaged in mental speculation under the external feature of the Lord, whatever qualities one may manifest are not recognized by the Lord. The Lord only recognizes that person who is engaged in unflinching, unalloyed devotional service to Him.

Thus, when the Lord recognizes the living entity, that living entity automatically develops these qualities. Some living entities may manifest them to some degree, but they are only fully manifested when the Lord, Himself, manifests them. This is one of the characteristics of the Lord—He magnifies the qualities of His devotees.

**Text 59**

*paṇḍita-gosāñira śiṣya—ananta ācārya*  
*kṛṣṇa-premamaya-tanu, udāra, sarva-ārya*

**Ananta Ācārya was a disciple of Gadādhara Paṇḍita. His body was always absorbed in love of Godhead. He was magnanimous and advanced in all respects.**

**Text 60**

*tāṅhāra ananta guṇa ke karu prakāśa*  
*tāṅra priya śiṣya iṅha—paṇḍita haridāsa*

**Ananta Ācārya was a reservoir of all good qualities. No one can estimate how great he was. Paṇḍita Haridāsa was his beloved disciple.**

**Purport**

(…) In Jagannātha Purī, or Puruṣottama-kṣetra, there is a monastery known as Gaṅgā-mātā Maṭha that was established by Ananta Ācārya. In the disciplic succession of the Gaṅgā-mātā Maṭha, he is known as Vinoda-mañjarī. One of his disciples was Haridāsa Paṇḍita Gosvāmī, who is also known as Śrī Raghu Gopāla and as Śrī Rāsa-mañjarī. His disciple Lakṣmīpriyā was the maternal aunt of Gaṅgā-mātā, a princess who was the daughter of the King of Puṭiyā. Gaṅgā-mātā brought a Deity of the name Śrī Rasikarāya from Kṛṣṇa Miśra of Jaipur and installed Him in the house of Sārvabhauma in Jagannātha Purī. (…) (*Cc. Ādi* 8.59–60)

Haridāsa Paṇḍita was the disciple of Ananta Ācārya, and he had two very famous disciples. One of them was Lakṣmīpriyā, who was the aunt of Gaṅgā-mātā. Gaṅgā-mātā was formerly a princess, the daughter of the king of Puṭiyā. Gaṅgā-mātā, who was a very great renunciant and very learned in *śāstra,* was known as Gaṅgā-mātā Gosvāmīni. She was vastly glorified and recognized as a disciple of Haridāsa Paṇḍita. It is described that when she was very young, she was not very much inclined to worldly things even though she was a princess and lived in royal opulence. Not attracted to these things, she used to spend much of her time studying different types of *śāstra.*

As she grew older, she became very attractive to everybody because she also displayed many good qualities. However, her parents, the king and queen, were beginning to discuss about getting her married. At the time, she was known as Śacī, which was her name as a child. When Śacī heard the discussion of her parents, she became very disturbed. She said to the king and queen, “I will not marry anybody who is a mortal being!” When the king and queen heard this, they became very disturbed. They had so much anxiety that they actually passed away and the kingdom was left in the care of Śacī, the princess. She did this for some time, but was not satisfied by this activity. Eventually, she decided to leave the responsibility of ruling the kingdom to her many relatives, and she developed a burning desire in her heart to seek out a spiritual master, which became an obsession for her. She therefore decided to leave the kingdom and went to Jagannātha Purī, where she spent some time and then continued to Vṛndāvana.

When she arrived in Vṛndāvana, she had the opportunity to meet Haridāsa Paṇḍita. When she saw the qualities manifested in Haridāsa Paṇḍita, she said, “This is the person in whom I actually want to take refuge.” With tears in her eyes, she came before Haridāsa Paṇḍita, crying and begging him, “Please give me refuge.”

Haridāsa Paṇḍita tested her: “It is not possible for somebody of such caliber as yourself, who lives in royal opulence, to perform *bhajan* in Vṛndāvana while simultaneously maintaining all this mundane opulence. I think you should leave Vṛndāvana, and return home and perform *bhajan*.” When Śacī heard this from Haridāsa Paṇḍita, she did not let these words affect her determination. She performed her *bhajan* in Vṛndāvana and gave up all her royal dress and royal ornaments.

Haridāsa Paṇḍita then approached her once again and said, “I think you should give up your pride, dignity, and fear. You should live by performing *mādhukarī,* which means that you should beg for your food. Simply go from door to door and beg.” When Śacī heard this from Haridāsa Paṇḍita, she liked this instruction very much. She therefore wore very tattered clothing and lived by begging. Although she was a princess, she nevertheless very eagerly accepted the dress of a beggar and went begging from door to door. Gradually, she became very thin and very emaciated. Nonetheless, her natural qualities were still very much attractive to everyone, and they were very eager to offer whatever they had. When Haridāsa Paṇḍita saw the degree of devotion that she had for the Lord, he said to Śacī, “I am very much pleased by your devotion.” He decided to accept her as his disciple: “On the next auspicious day, I will give you *dīkṣā-mantra*.” Thus, Śacī took initiation from Haridāsa Paṇḍita.

This was about the time when Lakṣmīpriyā (the aunt of Gaṅgā-mātā) came to Vṛndāvana. Up until that time, Śacī was rendering very simple service in Vṛndāvana. She would go daily to take bath in the Yamunā, clean the yard at the temple, go on *parikramā,* attend *āratis,* and hear the discourses of the learned *mahāntas.*

When Lakṣmīpriyā came to Vṛndāvana, she was already a disciple and devoted servant of Haridāsa Paṇḍita. Both Lakṣmīpriyā and Gaṅgā-mātā went to live in Govardhana. Every day, they would do *govardhana*-*parikramā* and perform very intense *bhajan*. Lakṣmīpriyā used to chant 300,000 (3 *lakhs*) of the holy name everyday. These were very elevated Vaiṣṇavīs.

When Haridāsa Paṇḍita saw the qualities that were manifest in Śacī, he instructed her, “You should now go to Jagannātha Purī and preach to the respectable personalities in Purī and perform your *bhajan* there.” Thus, on the order of her spiritual master, she went to Purī and performed her *bhajan* and regularly preached to the respectable people in the house of Sārvabhauma Bhaṭṭācārya.

At that particular time, almost all the associates of Lord Caitanya had left the planet. Śacī went to Jagannātha Purī and stayed in the very dilapidated house of Sārvabhauma Bhaṭṭācārya. It was there where she worshipped one of the *sālagrāma*-*śilās* that was left behind by Sārvabhauma Bhaṭṭācārya. It is said that she brought a Deity by the name of Śrī Rasika-rāya from Śrī Kṛṣṇa Miśra in Jaipur, and installed it in the house of Sārvabhauma in Jagannātha Purī.

She very devotedly performed her *bhajan* and preached. Gradually she became renowned as a very powerful preacher of *bhagavat-kathā*. She became very learned in the *siddhānta* of the Gosvāmīs, and whenever she would preach *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam,* she would always present everything according to the *siddhānta* given by the Gosvāmīs of Vṛndāvana. Her preaching became so powerful that eventually the king of Purī came and listened to her. The king of Purī was very much impressed with her *bhagavat-kathā,* and when he heard her speak, he decided that he wanted to offer her some land in charity. He therefore offered her a small parcel of land near the Śvetagaṅgā in Purī. At first, Śacī was not very eager to accept the offering of the king, but after giving it further thought, she decided that it must be the will of the Lord and therefore accepted the offering.

After some time, while she was staying there, she had a very strong desire. This is a very interesting story, wherein it explains how she actually got the name “Gaṅgā-mātā.” A very auspicious day was approaching, and she wanted to take bath in the Gaṅgā. However, remembering the order of her spiritual master to stay in Puri, she decided she would not go. That night, Lord Jagannātha appeared to her in a dream and told her to go take bath in the Śvetagaṅgā in Jagannātha Purī, and He will personally come and take her to the Gaṅgā so that she can take bath.

Thus, on this auspicious day, she went alone and took her bath at midnight at the Śvetagaṅgā. While taking her bath, all of a sudden, the waves became very, very strong. The waves of the Gaṅgā appeared before her and swept her away. They took her into the private quarters of the Jagannātha temple where Lord Jagannātha takes His bath. In that place, she saw many devotees taking bath. They were all chanting very auspicious *mantras.*

Both the *pāṇḍās* and the priests in the Jagannātha temple heard some noise coming from the place where Jagannātha takes His bath. (The *pāṇḍās* are the guards who carry sticks and keep the Westerners and trespassers out of the Jagannātha temple.) They went and told the king, “Something is going on behind the doors.” They brought the king to that place, and when they opened up the door, there was Śacī by herself. No one else was there. When the *pāṇḍās* saw her, they said, “Just see this woman! She is here, trying to steal all the ornaments of Lord Jagannātha.” They spoke very strongly against her, saying, “What else could she possibly be doing here at this time of night?”

Some of the priests thought that this was not a very credible story, but there was nothing they could do. Śacī was taken and put in prison for attempting to steal the ornaments of the Lord. While in prison, however, she was completely undisturbed. She was completely absorbed in *kṛṣṇa-nāma* and was taking full shelter of the holy name of the Lord. She did not consider any of the external circumstances as obstacles and was fixed in her devotion.

That night, the king of Purī had a dream where Lord Jagannātha appeared to him. Lord Jagannātha was very angry. He chastised the king of Purī: “What have you done? Do you not know that you have committed a great offense to this Vaiṣṇavī? Her character is completely spotless. All of you, including the *pāṇḍās* and the priests, must go and remove her from prison. You must beg forgiveness at her feet for the offense you have committed, and you must all take initiation from her.” Upon this instruction of Lord Jagannātha, the following day, the king went to the prison and immediately had her removed. The king and priests surrendered to her, and became her disciples by receiving initiation from her.

It was at that time that she became renowned as Gaṅgāmātā Gosvāmīni, because of the Gaṅgā having come to take her away so she could take bath.

Thus, Gaṅgā-mātā Gosvāmīni was described by Haridāsa Paṇḍita, and here (*Cc. Ādi* 8.60) a little bit about Haridāsa Paṇḍita is described. There is not much reference to Haridāsa Paṇḍita other than what is given here in *Caitanyacaritāmṛta*. However, there is reference about some of his disciples, of which I have spoken about two: Lakṣmīpriyā and Gaṅgā-mātā Gosvāmīni.

We can understand that such is the power of great Vaiṣṇavas—by their association, they make others great Vaiṣṇavas. Gaṅgā-mātā Gosvāmīni could see the wonderful qualities that were manifest in Haridāsa Paṇḍita, and she was very eager to become qualified to become his disciple. Because of her unflinching devotion, she also developed all good qualities.

*yasyāsti bhaktir bhagavaty akiñcanā*  
*sarvair guṇais tatra samāsate surāḥ*  
*harāv abhaktasya kuto mahad-guṇā*  
*manorathenāsati dhāvato bahiḥ*

(SB 8.15.12)

Such good qualities will manifest in the character of one who has unflinching devotion to the Lord. Thus, Gaṅgāmātā Gosvāmīni developed all the qualities that were manifest in her spiritual master, Haridāsa Paṇḍita.

(…) The disciple in the fifth generation after Śrī Ananta Ācārya was Śrī Vanamālī; in the sixth generation, Śrī Bhagavān dāsa, who was a Bengali; in the seventh generation, Madhusūdana dāsa, who was an Oriyā; in the eighth generation, Nīlāmbara dāsa; in the ninth generation, Śrī Narottama dāsa; in the tenth generation, Pītāmbara dāsa; and in the eleventh generation, Śrī Mādhava dāsa. The disciple in the twelfth generation is presently in charge of the Gaṅgā-mātā monastery. (*Cc. Ādi* 8.60, Purport)

This monastery still exists in Jagannātha Purī. It was built in the place where the king of Jagannātha Purī had given land to Gaṅgā-mātā Gosvāmīni. Therefore, we see that there is also a wealth of history of great Vaiṣṇavīs in our Vedic tradition as well.

**Text 61**

*caitanya-nityānande tāṅra parama viśvāsa*  
*caitanya-carite tāṅra parama ullāsa*

**Paṇḍita Haridāsa had great faith in Lord Caitanya and Nityānanda. Therefore he took great satisfaction in knowing about Their pastimes and qualities. (*****Cc. Ādi*** **8.61)**

**Ādi-līlā: 8.62**

**Kiev—May 2, 2000**

**Text 62**

*vaiṣṇavera guṇa-grāhī, nā dekhaye doṣa*  
*kāya-mano-vākye kare vaiṣṇava-santoṣa*

**He always accepted the good qualities of Vaiṣṇavas and never found fault in them. He engaged his heart and soul only to satisfy the Vaiṣṇavas.**

**Purport**

It is a qualification of a Vaiṣṇava that he is *adoṣa-darśī*: he never sees others’ faults. Of course, every human being has both good qualities and faults. Therefore, it is said, *saj-janā guṇam icchanti doṣam icchanti pāmarāḥ*: everyone has a combination of faults and glories. But a Vaiṣṇava, a sober man, accepts only a man’s glories and not his faults, for flies seek sores whereas honeybees seek honey. Haridāsa Paṇḍita never found fault with a Vaiṣṇava but considered only his good qualities.

Śrīla Prabhupāda further elaborates on the characteristics of a Vaiṣṇava*,* saying that if one wants to take the opportunity to find faults, there are certainly faults present in everyone to find. However, one who has a Vaiṣṇava mentality does not look for faults, rather, only considers the good qualities. Haridāsa Paṇḍita was such a Vaiṣṇava. When we speak of the qualities of different Vaiṣṇavas, we should understand that these are the general qualities a Vaiṣṇava displays, although each Vaiṣṇava can display these qualities in their own unique way. Nonetheless, a Vaiṣṇava generally never tries to find faults, rather, always tries to see the good in everyone.

There are different ways that we could look at this statement. We could take advantage of it, thinking, “Others should always look at my good qualities and never look at my faults.” Moreover, if somebody says something about us that may appear to be a slight criticism, we might immediately jump to defend ourselves: “You found fault, therefore you are not a Vaiṣṇava! I am a Vaiṣṇava, but you found fault with me!” However, in a society of Vaiṣṇavas, everything works nicely; everything is perfect because a Vaiṣṇava always thinks himself very low and fallen. This is also the general characteristic of a Vaiṣṇava, and therefore we should take note of the characteristics that Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī has described here.

In these verses, we can see the characteristics of Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī and how he sometimes describes himself to be a worm in stool. Later in this chapter, he will describe how shameful and fallen he thinks he is. Although he took up the work of writing the glories of Lord Caitanya, he thinks himself to be unworthy of such a service. However, because Vaiṣṇavas asked him to do it, and because the Supreme Lord blessed him, he felt obligated. At the same time, he felt shameful for doing it because he considered himself so unqualified. This will be discussed later.

*\* Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī felt himself unqualified to write, but the devotees requested him to do it. It appears that if he had not been asked, he would not have taken the initiative. Thus, how can a devotee, who considers himself fallen, understand what to do to show his* *qualities? How can he demonstrate his qualities, and at the same time, consider himself to be very fallen?*

He demonstrates his qualities because Kṛṣṇa magnifies them. If the Lord or the Vaiṣṇavas recognize one’s qualities, they will reveal them. However, a devotee is not very eager to reveal his own qualities, because he always thinks of himself as very insignificant.

One example of this is the story of Mādhavendra Purī in *Caitanya-caritāmṛta.* One time, Mādhavendra Purī saw Kṣīra-corā-gopīnātha’s *pūjārī* carrying some sweet rice, and he was thinking that he would like to taste it, because he wanted to offer the same high-quality sweet rice to his Deity, Gopāla. He then became very embarrassed for having such thoughts, and said to himself, “I am so fallen. I was thinking of tasting this sweet rice before Gopīnātha tasted it. What a terrible thing to do!”

However, Gopīnātha understood his mentality and knew that he was not thinking of tasting the sweet rice for his own pleasure. He knew that he was thinking of tasting the sweet rice so that he could duplicate that high-quality preparation and offer it to Gopāla. Therefore, because Gopīnātha knew his mentality and wanted to fulfill his desire, He stole the pot of sweet rice from the altar and told the *pūjārī* in his dream, “Go back into the Deity room and look behind the curtain. There you will find a small pot of sweet rice that I have stolen for Mādhavendra Purī. You should take this sweet rice, go out into the street, find Mādhavendra Purī, and give this sweet rice to him.”

Upon receiving this instruction, the *pūjārī* took the sweet rice outside and began calling, “Where is the *sannyāsī* whose name is Mādhavendra Purī?” He finally found Mādhavendra Purī, gave him the sweet rice, and said, “Gopīnātha has stolen this sweet rice and asked me to give it to you.” When Mādhavendra Purī accepted it, he was embarrassed that the Lord would do something like steal for him. He was thinking that if people heard that the Lord personally stole sweet rice for him, his reputation would increase, and he did not want that to happen. He therefore left that place in the middle of the night so that people would not recognize and glorify him.

However, if the Lord recognizes His devotee, the devotee cannot hide it. Even though the devotee tries to hide it, the reputation will follow him. Therefore, the devotee does not have to try to show his qualities to others; he simply has to try to please the Lord. By so doing, the Lord will recognize his sincere desire, and when He wants those qualities to be recognized, He will arrange it.

That is one example of how a Vaiṣṇava thinks. Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī was thinking himself unqualified. On his own initiative, he was not very eager to write. Prabhupāda explains in some later purports in the eighth chapter of *Ādi-līlā* how a writer of transcendental literature, who is a preacher, is not very eager to put himself before others on his own initiative; he does so only under the order of a higher authority.

A general characteristic of a Vaiṣṇava is that he always feels himself unqualified. Thus, when Vaiṣṇava qualities are displayed in a society of Vaiṣṇavas, there are very, very wonderful exchanges that take place. The Vaiṣṇava, who is naturally humble, thinks himself to be worthy of all criticisms, while the other Vaiṣṇavas are thinking that they could never find fault in any Vaiṣṇava. Thus, generally in a society of Vaiṣṇavas there are loving exchanges because there are no envious or duplicitous mentalities. The Vaiṣṇavas are always looking for the good qualities and are always glorifying the others; meanwhile, Vaiṣṇavas are very humble and do not want to accept the glorification. In this mood, very wonderful, relishable exchanges take place.

Although there is generally no criticism in a society of Vaiṣṇavas who possess this mentality, there may sometimes be criticism out of love on the transcendental and spontaneous platform. Lord Caitanya sometimes spoke critically of devotees, but it was an expression of His love for them. For example, it is described in the *Madhya-līlā* that when Lord Caitanya was leaving to go to South India, many of the devotees were approaching Him to ask His permission to be allowed to accompany Him. Mahāprabhu claimed to be going to South India because He wanted to look for His elder brother, Viśvarūpa. Under this pretense, the Lord was actually going to South India to deliver everyone through the distribution of the holy name. He told everybody that He wanted to go alone and did not want anyone to accompany Him.

Then, Lord Caitanya, one by one, began speaking about the different devotees present. He first spoke about how Lord Nityānanda had cheated and tricked Him by misdirecting Him to go to the house of Advaita Ācārya, telling Him that the river Ganges was actually the river Yamunā. Lord Caitanya said, “You also broke My *daṇḍa*! Such things disturb My activities.” Mahāprabhu then started speaking about how Jagadānanda Paṇḍita becomes angry with Him. The Lord explained that Jagadānanda Paṇḍita always wants Him to engage in sense gratification. However, because Lord Caitanya had accepted the role of a *sannyāsī,* He did not accept the offerings of Jagadānanda Paṇḍita. Upon hearing the Lord’s reaction, Jagadānanda ran away in anger and did not speak to Him for three days. Thus, Lord Caitanya was finding faults with Jagadānanda.

He then started speaking about how Mukunda also wanted Him to enjoy different types of comforts. The Lord knows that Mukunda becomes very disturbed when he sees the austerities that He is performing and said, “When I know that he is disturbed, then I become disturbed.”

In this way, Lord Caitanya continued giving different reasons why no one should accompany Him and was thus finding faults in their past behavior. He said, “Although I am a *sannyāsī* and Dāmodara Paṇḍita is a *brahmacārī,* he always keeps a stick in His hand, ready to chastise Me. He thinks I am some kind of neophyte in social etiquette and that he is very advanced in all of these matters.”

How is it that the Lord is speaking and pointing out faults in the devotees? Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī explains that by pointing out the faults of the devotees, Lord Caitanya was actually showing the exceptional qualities of these devotees and the particular type of intimate relationship that each of them had with Him. Moreover, he was pointing out how their love for Him is so strong that when they appeared to transgress social etiquette, the Lord was lovingly accepting their transgressions. The Lord was actually glorifying His devotees by speaking about them as though they had faults.

Similarly, there was one time when Advaita said to Lord Nityānanda, “You are a reject *paramahaṁsa*. You have accepted the renounced order of life simply to fill Your belly!” On the pretense of criticizing Lord Nityānanda, Advaita Ācārya was really glorifying Him by establishing His position as *paramahaṁsa.*

Occasionally, the Vaiṣṇava *ācāryas* even take certain words that are used in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* where it appears that someone has spoken critically of the Lord or His devotees, and they show how these are actually glorifications of the Lord. Therefore, there may even sometimes be some criticism in the society of exalted *Vaiṣṇavas,* but when such criticism is there, it should not be looked at as criticism. It should be considered as a pastime of the Lord. That is why Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura writes in *Śrī Caitanya-bhāgavata* that one should be very careful in taking the side of one Vaiṣṇava against another.

“One should never criticize Vaiṣṇavas. If someone sees some fault manifest in someone, one should understand it as the pastime of the Lord.” Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura is, of course, speaking about a society of exalted Vaiṣṇavas. We are talking about the ideal characteristics of Vaiṣṇavas*,* and whenever we talk about these ideal characteristics, it is for the purpose of helping us to understand what real Vaiṣṇava society is like. However, time and time again, we may become disillusioned, thinking, “I am not seeing Vaiṣṇava displays. Why am I not experiencing this nectar of relationships? Why do I not see that everything is like the spiritual world?” Śrīla Prabhupāda talks about this point very nicely in one letter (Bombay—February 4, 1972*).*

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your very nice letter of January 10, 1972, and I am very much pleased by the sentiments expressed therein. It is not so much that because there may be some faults in our godbrothers and godsisters, or because there may be some mismanagement or lack of cooperation, that this is due to being impersonalists, no. It is the nature of the living condition to always have some fault. Even in the Spiritual World there is some fault and envy—sometimes the Gopis will quarrel over Krishna’s favor, and once Krishna was so much attracted to Radharani that by mistake he tried to milk the bull instead of the cow, and sometimes when the Gopis used to put on their dress and make-up for seeing Krishna, they would be too much hasty and smear *kumkum* and mascara in the wrong places and their ornaments and dresses would appear as if small children had been trying to dress themselves and they were not very expert, like that. There are so many examples. But it is not the same as material fault or material envy; it is transcendental because it is all based on Krishna. Sometimes when one Gopi would serve Krishna very nicely, the others would say, Oh, she has done so nicely, now let me do better for pleasing Krishna. That is envy, but it is transcendental, without malice. So we shall not expect that anywhere there is any Utopia. Rather, that is impersonalism. People should not expect that even in the Krishna Consciousness Society there will be Utopia. Because devotees are persons, therefore there will always be some lacking—but the difference is that their lacking, because they have given up everything to serve Krishna—money, jobs, reputation, wealth, big educations, everything—their lackings have become transcendental because, despite everything they may do, their topmost intention is to serve Krishna. “One who is engaged in devotional service, despite the most abominable action, is to be considered saintly because he is rightly situated.” The devotees of Krishna are the most exalted persons on this planet, better than kings, all of them, so we should always remember that and, like the bumblebee, always look for the nectar or the best qualities of a person. Not like the utopians, who are like the flies who always go to the open sores or find the faults in a person, and because they cannot find any utopia, or because they cannot find anyone without faults, they want to become void, merge, nothing—they think that is utopia, to become void of personality. So if there are sometimes slight disagreements between devotees, it is not due to impersonalism, but it is because they are persons and such disagreements should not be taken very seriously. The devotee is always pessimistic about the material world, but he is very optimistic about the spiritual life; so in this way, you should consider that anyone engaged in Krishna’s service is always the best person. (…)

In this letter, Prabhupāda is speaking about the idealists. He calls them “utopians.” Utopians think, “Everything should be perfect. There should be no faults; whenever I see faults, it means that it is not utopia.” Of course, then what happens? Prabhupāda says here that they become impersonalists. They want to merge into void. He says, “We are persons and disagreements should not be taken very seriously.”

Prabhupāda explains that in the spiritual world there is also some fault. He says, “There will always be some fault, because we are persons.” Every human being has both good qualities and faults. Therefore, it is said (*Cc. Ādi* 8.62, Purport): *saj-janā guṇam icchanti / doṣam icchanti pāmarāḥ.* Everyone has a combination of faults and glories, but a Vaiṣṇava, a sober man, accepts only a man’s glories and not his faults. He knows fault will exist, but he is not interested in accepting the faults. Prabhupāda used to always say, “Why not appreciate the moon for its illuminating potency instead of trying to look for the little marks on the moon? This is the mentality of the critic.”

Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta once told Prabhupāda a story about the mentality of people who find faults, and Prabhupāda quoted this story to his own disciples. He said, “One man was speaking to his friend about another friend who had become a high-court judge. The other man said,

‘No, it is not possible. He could not become a high-court judge.’ However, the man said, ‘No, I saw him sitting on the bench. He is a high-court judge. I saw him with my own eyes.’ So, the other friend said, ‘Well, maybe he is sitting on the bench, but he is not receiving any salary!’”

He was giving this as an example to illustrate that this type of person cannot tolerate something good; he somehow has to turn things around to find something bad. This, of course, is not Vaiṣṇava mentality. The mentality of Vaiṣṇavas is to always accept the good—not be like the fly that always goes only to the sore. Have you ever tried to keep flies away from nasty places? You cannot keep them away. You may have a sore or cut, and a fly comes directly to it, and keeps coming back even though you try to swat it away. This is the same thing as the “fault-finding mentality.” You might try to do something to divert a person’s consciousness from looking for fault, but the faultfinder cannot control himself.

It is sometimes necessary to point out this mentality. Somebody could say, “He is talking about not having faults, but he is talking about people who find faults. Why is he doing that?” However, sometimes it is necessary to point out a general misbehavior or fault that exists in somebody to teach and conclude a particular subject.

Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura gives the example of Śukadeva Gosvāmī in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.* He speaks about envious householders who are engaged in sex life, and then during the day, they are engaged in making money. They are attached to body, wealth, and home. Therefore, sometimes it is necessary to speak about a general class of people and give them a particular instruction to make it clear that we should try to avoid this type of misbehavior. Thus, we have to be discriminating, and we have to know what the wrong type of mentality is. But we should not always dwell on it. If we want to dwell on it, we should look within ourselves to see our own tendencies and not dwell on the faults of others. There is something wrong with the consciousness of those who are always absorbed in the faults of others. This is not the Vaiṣṇava mentality.

The natural behavior of a Vaiṣṇava is to always look for good qualities. We should set aside whatever faults there may be and look for what other devotees are sincerely doing. Sometimes it may be very difficult to find something favorable, and we might conclude, “Oh, this person is not a devotee.” We may think so many other thoughts. If our consciousness is absorbed in that way, we should seek out the association of the devotees in whom we can see good qualities. We should not dwell on the others—we can respect them from a distance.

Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī explains in *Śrī Upadeśāmṛta* that we should mentally honor a neophyte devotee, and it may be necessary to do it from a distance so that we will not always be absorbed in looking for some fault. Therefore, we should keep ourselves in the association of those devotees who have good qualities that we can see and appreciate.

If we think we cannot find anybody with good qualities, then we have a problem. If one thinks, “I cannot find any good qualities in the people I am associating with,” the following conclusion should be drawn: “I must be like a fly.” However, it unfortunately does not happen that way. Sometimes a proud person thinks, “I am too good for everyone. I know that I am more advanced than everyone else who is around me, and I am not going to benefit from this association.” Such persons can be saved if they seek out the association of somebody within whom they can find some good qualities. They should render menial service to that devotee and be prepared to accept everything as Kṛṣṇa’s mercy.

Yet, unfortunately sometimes a neophyte devotee thinks that he is too advanced even for the association of such devotees and will seek out the association of some greater personality so that he can have some higher connections. He thinks, “See? He is great. I am connected to him; therefore, I am great too!” Such a person uses great personalities, not wanting to say anything good about them. Instead, he tries to get the greater devotee to say good things about him, using him for his own glorification. Of course, great personalities are fortunately very merciful, and they know just how to deal with people who have this kind of mentality.

There is the example of Vallabha Bhaṭṭa, who always wanted to go directly to Lord Caitanya and try to get Him to listen to and appreciate his commentaries on *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam.* He would say, “See? Caitanya Mahāprabhu appreciates this.” However, Lord Caitanya dealt with him by refusing to hear anything he said. Every time he tried to speak, Lord Caitanya would completely divert the discussion. Vallabha Bhaṭṭa was getting very frustrated because everybody saw that Lord Caitanya was dealing with him in this way. Vallabha Bhaṭṭa went to everybody else, trying to get them to hear him, but no one else would listen to him either. His pride was completely crushed. Although He was crushing his pride, Lord Caitanya was very merciful. He later explained to him that just as He dealt with Indra to crush his pride, He was dealing with Vallabha Bhaṭṭa with the same objective.

If one maintains this proud mentality, he cannot advance in spiritual life. Lord Caitanya does not like pride; He is not attracted to this quality. He is attracted to real humility—not false humility. When devotees genuinely feel themselves fallen, it is usually by always looking for the good qualities in others and speaking about them—glorifying the devotees. It is not artificial because they genuinely know how to see good qualities in others; that is their vision.

That was also the vision of Haridāsa Paṇḍita. He was *adoṣa-darśī*. *Darśī* means “to see,” and *adoṣa* means “without any faults.” Haridāsa Paṇḍita always saw others as having no faults. How did he do this? He engaged his heart and soul only in satisfying the Vaiṣṇavas. He was thinking, “I have such a wonderful opportunity to serve Vaiṣṇavas since I am surrounded by them.” He was always looking for different ways to render service, as opposed to always trying to find different ways to get others to serve him, which is completely the opposite mentality of a Vaiṣṇava.

A Vaiṣṇava wants to serve the Lord and His devotees, and because he has that kind of mentality, others become eager to serve him. The real secret in getting others to assist is having this real Vaiṣṇava mentality. Then others think, “Oh, just see, he is so kind towards us. He just wants to serve. How can I accept service from him? Let me do something to serve him! I am embarrassed to have to accept service from him. He is always trying to serve. He is always thinking of the welfare of others. He is never thinking of himself. Let me do something for him!” That is how a society of Vaiṣṇavas grows very naturally and how loving exchanges become manifest.

Cultivation of this consciousness is the responsibility of each and every one of us, although we may have the tendency to think, “No, let somebody else do it. Let somebody else manifest that consciousness, and when I find somebody, I will surrender.” This is incorrect thinking. Each and every one of us has to try to cultivate this Vaiṣṇava mentality. That is therefore why we have so many lectures about Vaiṣṇava etiquette. They are meant for every one of us to learn how to behave as Vaiṣṇavas. When you see these kinds of exchanges amongst advanced Vaiṣṇavas, it melts the heart. You see such humility. The Vaiṣṇava’s heart by nature is very soft. It becomes soft because his heart is always eager to serve and please the Lord and His devotees.

Such was the mentality of Haridāsa Paṇḍita, as is being described here. As we said, this is a general quality of all Vaiṣṇavas, but now Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī is speaking about a unique way that Haridāsa Paṇḍita also displayed these qualities in order to explain why he felt so compelled to do what Haridāsa Paṇḍita told him to do. He was not thinking that it was just anyone who had asked him to write about Lord Caitanya’s pastimes. Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī thought, “Haridāsa Paṇḍita asked me! He is such an exalted Vaiṣṇava. Because he has asked me, I feel I have to do it to serve him! Haridāsa Paṇḍita is very eager to hear about the pastimes of the Lord, and is begging me to write. I must please him in this way. Furthermore, the other Vaiṣṇavas are also very eager. They want to hear the nectar of Lord Caitanya’s pastimes. I must also serve their order, even though I am shameful and unqualified.” This is what Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī is opening his heart about. He is opening his heart, saying, “I have to do it because they are such exalted Vaiṣṇavas.”

Because he was a Vaiṣṇava, he could see such wonderful displays and qualities. We should become very determined to serve Vaiṣṇavas. We should become very eager to serve the Vaiṣṇavas we associate with and be anxious for the opportunity to render service unto them. We must always keep our heart open and eager to render service. This is what we are learning from this example in *Caitanya-caritāmṛta—vaiṣṇava*-*saṅga.*

*\* What does “having an open heart” mean?*

We must keep our heart open to look for the good qualities in others. I was using that expression to help us understand that when we do not find fault, our heart is open to see the good in others. When our heart is open, we naturally are very eager to want to serve others.

Opening our heart also means to express love. Rūpa Gosvāmī describes in *Śrī Upadeśāmṛta* that offering gifts and revealing one’s mind in confidence are exchanges of love. Therefore, an open heart means to be open to having loving exchanges. However, there cannot be loving exchanges when we always think, “What are they going to do for me?” There is no love in that; it is business. Loving exchanges can only exist when we think about keeping our hearts open—“How can I serve others?” Having an open heart means being eager to serve others.

When we have that mentality, we will see a natural reciprocation take place. Natural reciprocation means that it is not demanded; there is no condition for the service. “Natural” means that the other person wants to do it and is very eager. It is very natural for Kṛṣṇa to love His devotees, because He wants to do it. He is very eager to serve, glorify, and render service to His devotees. Because a devotee loves Kṛṣṇa and because He wants to reciprocate, it is a natural exchange. It is not natural when one “must” reciprocate.

**Ādi-līlā: 8.72–73**

**Kiev—May 10, 2000**

**Text 72**

*more ājñā karilā sabe karuṇā kariyā*  
*tāṅ-sabāra bole likhi nirlajja ha-iyā*

**By their mercy, all these devotees ordered me to write of the last pastimes of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu. Because of their order only, although I am shameless, I have attempted to write this** ***Caitanya-caritāmṛta.***

**Purport**

To write about the transcendental pastimes of the Supreme Personality of Godhead is not an ordinary endeavor. Unless one is empowered by the higher authorities, or advanced devotees, one cannot write transcendental literature, for all such literature must be above suspicion, or, in other words, it must have none of the defects of conditioned souls, namely mistakes, illusions, cheating and imperfect sense perceptions. The words of Kṛṣṇa and of the disciplic succession that carries the orders of Kṛṣṇa are actually authoritative. To be empowered to write transcendental literature is a privilege in which a writer can take great pride. As a humble Vaiṣṇava, Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī, being thus empowered, felt very much ashamed that it was he who was to narrate the pastimes of Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī was ashamed in his attempt to write *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*; however, the Vaiṣṇavas convinced him to do it. By their mercy and order he became qualified to describe the pastimes of the Lord. Śrīla Prabhupāda warns that unless one is empowered by higher authorities, he cannot write transcendental literature, which must be free from all the defects of conditioned souls. Transcendental literature is the perfect authority. Interpreting scripture or speaking in a speculative way, especially about the pastimes of the Supreme Lord, creates chaos.

Prabhupāda often quoted the verse *śruti-smṛti-purāṇādipañcarātra-vidhiṁ vinā.* (*Brs.* 1.2.101) If devotional service is not executed according to the *śruti*, *smṛti*, and *Purāṇas,* it is a disturbance to society. *Śruti* is knowledge spoken directly by the Lord, and *smṛti* is knowledge about the Lord spoken by the empowered Vaiṣṇavas. *Smṛti* is as good as *śruti* because the speaker is empowered by the Lord. For example, Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī previously related that everything Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura had written was as if Lord Caitanya had spoken through his writings.

Similarly, Śrīla Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura and others have often said that the songs of Narottama dāsa Ṭhākura are as good as *śāstra*. They are as good because he is realized, and the Lord manifests Himself in the words of realized Vaiṣṇavas. However, if somebody who is not realized writes scripture or poetry, people will be misled by the descriptions of the pastimes of the Lord. Sometimes unrealized writers mix mellows when describing the Lord’s pastimes; this is called *rasābhāsa*. However, empowered devotees fully understand the science of devotional mellows and present everything very consistently.

There is an example in *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* of a *brāhmaṇa* from Bengal who was very eager to write poetic descriptions of Lord Caitanya. He composed many verses in glorification of Lord Caitanya, and he came to Jagannātha Purī, very eager to recite his poetry to the Lord. He was an acquaintance of Bhagavān Ācārya who is an associate of Lord Caitanya. When he came to Purī, he met Bhagavān Ācārya and stayed in his home. He expressed his desire to read his poetry to the Lord, so Bhagavān Ācārya listened to the *brāhmaṇa*’s recitation of his poetry. He was very moved by the poetry. He thought, “This is a wonderful glorification of the Lord,” and therefore introduced his *brāhmaṇa* friend to other Vaiṣṇavas, who also heard this poetry. They were also impressed and became very eager for Lord Caitanya to hear it.

Lord Caitanya had a very strict policy—He would not hear any literature that was not first presented to Svarūpa Dāmodara. Svarūpa Dāmodara, being very expert in the mellows of devotional service, would be able to find any discrepancies that may exist in the writings. He knew that if the Lord heard such discrepancies, He would become very angry. Thus, Bhagavān Ācārya approached Svarūpa Dāmodara and said, “Please listen to this poetry so that we may present it to the Lord.”

Svarūpa Dāmodara warned him, “If there is any hint of *rasābhāsa* in the poetry, then it will be a disturbance to the Lord. Only those who are fully surrendered souls to the Supreme Lord, Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu, are qualified to write poetry in glorification of Him. Only those who have taken full shelter of His lotus feet can glorify the pastimes of either Lord Caitanya or Lord Viṣṇu.”

Bhagavān Ācārya said, “Yes, this poetry is definitely of that caliber. Please listen to it. Give your stamp of approval so that we can let the Lord hear it.”

Svarūpa Dāmodara replied, “Oh, you are just a simple cowherd boy, and you are always eager to taste any kind of poetry that someone will read to you.” Nevertheless, Bhagavān Ācārya went on continuously for several days, and Svarūpa Dāmodara finally agreed. Svarūpa Dāmodara sat down and when the poet came, he said, “Now, please recite to me your poetry verses of glorification.”

The *brāhmaṇa* immediately recited his first verse, which was something like: “The Supreme Personality of Godhead has appeared in an effulgent, golden form, and He has become the soul in the body of Lord Jagannātha, whose blooming lotus eyes are always wide. He has descended to Jagannātha Purī to bring dull matter to life. May that Lord Caitanyadeva be merciful upon you.”

Many Vaiṣṇavas heard this verse, and they commented, “What wonderful poetry!”

Svarūpa Dāmodara said, “Can you explain this verse to me?” The poet began to explain the verse, saying that the body of the Deity, Lord Jagannātha, is very beautiful, and Lord Caitanyadeva is the owner of that body. He said, “The Supreme Lord, Caitanya, has descended to Jagannātha Purī to completely spiritualize the inert matter of this material world.” Svarūpa Dāmodara became very angry, saying, “You rascal! What qualification do you have to speak about the Lord? Not only do you have no qualification to speak about the Lord, you also have no faith in the Lord; therefore any poetry you recite about the Lord is useless. You have presented the Lord to be different from His body! The Lord always has a transcendental body, but you have made a distinction between Him and His body.
