When Mādhavendra Purī entered Vṛndāvana, he also experienced symptoms of ecstatic love. The first time Kṛṣṇa requested service from him in a dream was in Vṛndāvana. Mādhavendra Purī lay down in one place, and a young cowherd boy came and gave him a pot of milk. The boy said, “Please accept this milk.” Mādhavendra Purī was looking at the beautiful form of this young cowherd boy and was very captivated by His beauty, but he did not recognize that it was none other than his worshipful Lord who was appearing before him to give him some milk.

Mādhavendra Purī said, “Who are You? Where do You reside? And how did You know that I was fasting?” (*Cc. Madhya* 4.27)

The young cowherd boy replied, “Well, in this village, nobody fasts. Everybody is provided with adequate food and milk. If there is something lacking, I will personally bring and give some milk to anybody who needs it in this village.”

The cowherd boy gave him the milk and said that He would come back later and collect the pot from him. Mādhavendra Purī took the milk and waited for the cowherd boy to return, but He never did. Mādhavendra Purī fell asleep late at night after continuously chanting all day. The cowherd boy returned, but this time it was in his dream. The cowherd boy said, “For a long time, I have been waiting for you to come so that I could accept your service.” Being eager to accept some service from Mādhavendra Purī, the Lord explained to him that He was lying in a bush in the forest in the form of the Deity, Gopāla.

Quite some time ago, He was taken and hidden in the forest by His *pūjārī* when the village was attacked by the Mohammedan king. The Lord asked Mādhavendra Purī to go into the forest and take Him out of the bush, install Him, and begin worshipping Him.

Mādhavendra Purī re-established the worship of Gopāla in a very elaborate way. There is a long description in *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* (*Madhya* 4.47-106) on how he began the worship and expanded it by his devotion.

Once the worship had been established very nicely, gradually word about the worship of Gopāla began to spread throughout Vṛndāvana and all the neighboring villages. All the people would come, bringing the fruits of their labor to offer to the Gopāla Deity. The neighboring villagers brought grains, vegetables, rice, ghee, etc., and performed the Annakūṭa ceremony day after day. Mādhavendra Purī also initiated as his disciples all the *brāhmaṇas* who had come. Later, two *brāhmaṇas* came from Bengal, and Mādhavendra Purī initiated them and entrusted the worship of Gopāla to them.

The Lord appeared again to Mādhavendra Purī in a dream and requested him to bring sandalwood from Jagannātha Purī.

**\[The Lord said\], “Bring sandalwood pulp from Jagannātha Purī. Kindly go quickly. Since no one else can do it, you must.”**

**After having this dream, Mādhavendra Purī Gosvāmī became very glad due to ecstasy of love of Godhead, and in order to execute the command of the Lord, he started east toward Bengal. (***Cc. Madhya* **4.107–8)**

It is then described how Mādhavendra Purī first stopped at the house of Advaita Ācārya. When Advaita Ācārya saw the symptoms that were manifest in Mādhavendra Purī, He wanted to become his disciple through initiation.

Mādhavendra Purī initiated many disciples including Advaita Ācārya, Īśvara Purī, Paramānanda Purī, and Brahmānanda Bhāratī. Lord Nityānanda also considered Mādhavendra Purī as His spiritual master.

According to *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* many of Lord Caitanya’s associates received initiation from Mādhavendra Purī. Lord Caitanya treated both Brahmānanda Bhāratī and Paramānanda Purī very respectfully because they both took initiation from Mādhavendra Purī.

On his way to South India, Mādhavendra Purī stopped in Remuṇā, where there is the Deity of Gopīnātha. When Mādhavendra Purī saw the beauty of the Deity, he became overwhelmed.

**In the corridor of the temple, from which people generally viewed the Deity, Mādhavendra Purī chanted and danced. Then he sat down there and asked a** *brāhmaṇa* **what kinds of foods they offered to the Deity. From the excellence of the arrangements, Mādhavendra Purī understood by deduction that only the best food was offered. (***Cc. Madhya* **4.113–114)**

Here we see that in worshipping the Lord in the form of the *arcā-vigraha,* the *pūjārīs* and servants of the Lord should always offer the best possible foods for the satisfaction of the Lord. Mādhavendra Purī could understand that these *pūjārīs* were offering the highest quality food.

Similarly, there is a story of Rāghava Paṇḍita, who was offering coconuts to his Deity. Although coconuts were readily available everywhere, he would travel 30 kilometers to get the best possible coconuts to offer. The Lord is very pleased when His servants render service to Him with such devotion. He is very eager to accept their service and offerings.

Here it is described that Mādhavendra Purī understood that the Gopīnātha Deity was getting the best possible food.

**Mādhavendra Purī thought, “I shall inquire from the priest what foods are offered to Gopīnātha so that by making arrangements in our kitchen, we can offer similar foods to Śrī Gopāla.” (***Cc. Madhya* **4.115)**

In other words, having seen the very high standard of worship that was being offered to Gopīnātha, he wanted to worship Gopāla in a similar way. He wanted to find out all the wonderful recipes being used, so that he could make the same preparations for Gopāla.

When Śrīla Prabhupāda was establishing Deity worship, he also tried to educate the devotees in a very similar way by establishing one place where the Deity worship was at a very high standard. Devotees from the other temples could then come to that place for training. This high standard was established in Los Angeles, and he wanted to see it established throughout all his temples. Thus, all the devotees who are engaged in worshipping the Deity should try to understand the proper standard. The standard is that the devotees should try to always offer to the Deities the best that they possibly can.

We see from this example that the devotee is willing to accept personal inconvenience to satisfy the Lord. Mādhavendra Purī was traveling from Vṛndāvana all the way to Purī on foot to carry a large quantity of sandalwood and camphor, just to satisfy his worshipful Lord.

This should be the standard of worship—that the devotee is willing to accept any inconveniences to see that the Lord’s standards are established and maintained. Even if one stays up late at night, the *pūjārī* should be thinking that he must get up early in the morning so that the Lord can rise at the same time that He is accustomed to rising everyday.

All the servants should also be thinking, “Yes, we should be up.” We have to accept inconvenience. Once we establish Their worship, then that standard must be followed. When devotees are ready to accept some personal inconvenience for the pleasure of the Lord, the Lord becomes very pleased by their standard of devotional service. Service to the Deity should not revolve around our life. Our life should revolve around the service to the Deity. Until we become qualified and come to the level of spontaneous devotional service, we must follow rules and regulations very strictly. Therefore, Śrīla Prabhupāda established this standard of worship for the Deities, and he wanted his devotees to know and follow the proper standard.

**When the** *brāhmaṇa* **priest was questioned about this matter, he explained in detail what kinds of foods were offered to the Deity of Gopīnātha. The** *brāhmaṇa* **priest said, “In the evening the Deity is offered sweet rice in twelve earthen pots. Because the taste is as good as nectar \[***amṛta***\], it is named** *amṛta-keli***. This sweet rice is celebrated throughout the world as** *gopīnātha-kṣīra***. It is not offered anywhere else in the world.”**

**While Mādhavendra Purī was talking with the** *brāhmaṇa* **priest, the sweet rice was placed before the Deity** **as an offering. Hearing this, Mādhavendra Purī thought as follows. “If, without my asking, a little sweet rice is given to me, I can then taste it and make a similar preparation to offer my Lord Gopāla.” (***Cc. Madhya* **4.116–120)**

Mādhavendra Purī did not realize that he was in fact asking, and that the Lord was hearing. The devotee does not want to ask, but because the desire is there in the heart, he is already asking. Meanwhile, the Lord already knows and arranges everything to fulfill the desire of His devotee. However, Mādhavendra Purī was not asking for himself; he was asking for the Lord, and the Lord is very anxious to fulfill whatever desires are in the core of the heart of His devotees.

Similarly, there is the story of how Advaita Ācārya had a desire to cook food and offer it to Lord Caitanya. The Lord was very eager to accept His offering. Advaita Ācārya also had another desire in His heart, which was to get some of the remnants of the Lord’s food. He was in anxiety about this, thinking, “If Lord Caitanya comes to My home and accepts this meal, He will come with all His *sannyāsī* associates. The *sannyāsī* associates will eat everything and there will be no remnants for Me to take.”

While He was cooking this wonderful feast for the Lord, all of a sudden, big clouds came in the sky, and very hard rain began to fall, followed by hail. Strong winds then began blowing. This was happening everywhere in Navadvīpa, except in the area where Advaita Ācārya was cooking, which had very calm weather. Advaita Ācārya then began offering the food in meditation and Lord Caitanya came to accept the food. However, none of the *sannyāsī* associates came, because they could not get out of their houses due to the rain.

Lord Caitanya sat down, and Advaita Ācārya began serving the preparations. Lord Caitanya began eating each preparation that Advaita Ācārya gave Him, but He would leave a little bit of each preparation on the plate. Advaita Ācārya asked Him, “Why are You leaving a little food on Your plate?” Lord Caitanya said, “Advaita, where did You learn to cook so nicely?”

Advaita would not answer, but the Lord was satisfied with Advaita Ācārya’s desire, and therefore left some food. The Lord understood the desire in the heart of His devotee. If the devotee has some desire that is in connection with the Lord, the Lord is always very eager to satisfy that desire. Advaita Ācārya did not have to ask for remnants; it was already arranged. Thus, King Indra had become subservient to the desire of Advaita Ācārya, and he caused the rain.

Likewise, Mādhavendra Purī had a desire: “If without my asking, I could get some of that sweet rice, then I could taste it, and make a similar preparation to offer to my Lord Gopāla.”

**Mādhavendra Purī became greatly ashamed when he desired to taste the sweet rice, and he immediately began to think of Lord Viṣṇu. While he was thus thinking of Lord Viṣṇu, the offering was completed, and the** *ārati* **ceremony began. After the** *ārati* **was finished, Mādhavendra Purī offered his obeisances to the Deity and then left the temple. He did not say anything more to anyone. (***Cc. Madhya* **4.121–122)**

Although Mādhavendra Purī was thinking in this way, he was actually situated on the platform of ecstatic love. Devotees on the platform of ecstatic love never think of their own enjoyment. To always think of the pleasure of the Lord is one of the symptoms of ecstatic love. Mādhavendra Purī was not thinking about enjoying the sweet rice. He was only thinking about Gopāla’s pleasure: “If I could know what it tastes like, then I can offer the same thing to Gopāla.”

By nature, he was very humble and considered himself fallen for thinking of tasting the sweet rice. It is the humility manifested by the Lord’s devotees that very much attracts the heart of the Lord.

Mādhavendra Purī did not say anything further and left the temple. He avoided begging. This is how little Mādhavendra Purī thought of himself. One who is on the *paramahaṁsa* platform—who has taken *ayācita-vṛtti,* or *ājagara-vṛtti—*does not ask anyone for food. If it is given, he accepts it; otherwise, he never asks. When Mādhavendra Purī entered Vṛndāvana, he was fasting because nothing had been given to him, and therefore Kṛṣṇa brought some milk for him. Mādhavendra Purī would never have asked anyone for food.

Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī also describes this in *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* where he explains the meaning of the verse *tṛṇād api su-nīcena, taror iva sahiṣṇunā*. (*Cc. Ādi* 17.31) The devotee is as tolerant as a tree. Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī says that even if one comes and breaks its branches, the tree will never complain, and even if it is drying up, the tree will never ask for water. He describes that that is the meaning of being more tolerant than a tree.

**\[Thus\] Mādhavendra Purī avoided begging. He was completely unattached and indifferent to material things. If, without his begging, someone offered him some food, he would eat; otherwise he would fast. A** *paramahaṁsa* **like Mādhavendra Purī is always satisfied in the loving service of the Lord. Material hunger and thirst cannot** **impede his activities. When he desired to taste a little sweet rice offered to the Deity, he considered that he had committed an offense by desiring to eat what was being offered to the Deity. Mādhavendra Purī left the temple and sat down in the village marketplace, which was vacant. Sitting there, he began to chant.**

**In the meantime, the temple priest laid the Deity down to rest. Finishing his daily duties, the priest went to take rest. In a dream he saw the Gopīnātha Deity come to talk to him, and He spoke as follows. “O priest, please get up and open the door of the temple. I have kept one pot of sweet rice for the** *sannyāsī* **Mādhavendra Purī. This pot of sweet rice is just behind My cloth curtain. You did not see it because of My tricks.” (***Cc. Madhya* **4.123–128)**

Every evening, the *pūjārī* takes the offering off the altar, but when he went to do so this time, he did not notice that there was a pot of sweet rice that had been taken and hidden behind the curtain. The Lord had “stolen” this pot of sweet rice specifically for Mādhavendra Purī. In the purport to this verse, Prabhupāda describes how the Lord engages in seemingly immoral activities for the sake of His devotees. He became a thief for His devotee. Why would the Lord become a thief? In this world, a person who engages in thievery is not very much appreciated. However, when the Lord steals, the devotees relish talking about it. Such is the nature of the Lord’s pastimes—stealing for His devotee. The Lord will even break His own promise for His devotee. We might think, “Breaking a promise is terrible! How can you break it? Who will accept the word of someone who always breaks their promise?” However, the Lord will break His promise for His devotee, and He will steal for His devotee. Prabhupāda explains this in the purport to verse 133.

**Madhya-līlā: 4.129-133**

**Simferopol—July 2000**

In the previous verses, it was described that the Lord appeared in the dream of the *pūjārī* and told him where He had kept the pot of sweet rice. He said, “This pot of sweet rice is just behind My cloth curtain. You did not see it because of My tricks.” (*Cc. Madhya* 4.128) In the next verse, the Lord continues:

**Text 129**

*mādhava-purī sannyāsī āche hāṭete vasiñā*  
*tāhāke ta’ ei kṣīra śīghra deha lañā*

**A** ***sannyāsī*** **named Mādhavendra Purī is sitting in the vacant marketplace. Please take this pot of sweet rice from behind Me and deliver it to him.**

**Text 130**

*svapna dekhi’ pūjārī uṭhi’ karilā vicāra*  
*snāna kari’ kapāṭa khuli, mukta kaila dvāra*

**Awaking from the dream, the priest immediately rose from bed and thought it wise to take a bath before entering the Deity’s room. He then opened the temple door.**

**Text 131**

*dhaḍāra āṅcala-tale pāila sei kṣīra*  
*sthāna lepi’ kṣīra lañā ha-ila bāhira*

**According to the Deity’s directions, the priest found the pot of sweet rice behind the cloth curtain. He removed the pot and mopped up the place where it had been kept. He then went out of the temple.**

These two verses describe the attention to details on the part of the *pūjārī.* He thought it wise to take a bath before going on the altar, and also, after taking the rice pot, he cleaned up the place where the sweet rice had been kept.

**Text 132**

*dvāra diyā grāme gelā sei kṣīra lañā*  
*hāṭe hāṭe bule mādhava-purīke cāhiñā*

**Closing the door of the temple, he went to the village with the pot of sweet rice. He called out in every stall in search of Mādhavendra Purī.**

**Text 133**

*kṣīra laha ei, yāra nāma ‘mādhava-purī’*  
*tomā lāgi’ gopīnātha kṣīra kaila curi*

**Holding the pot of sweet rice, the priest called, “Will he whose name is Mādhavendra Purī please come and take this pot! Gopīnātha has stolen this pot of sweet rice for you!”**

**Purport**

The difference between the Absolute Truth and relative truth is explained here. Lord Gopīnātha has openly declared herein that He is a thief. He had stolen the pot of sweet rice, and this was not kept a secret because His act of stealing is a source of great transcendental bliss. In the material world, theft is criminal, but in the spiritual world the Lord’s stealing is a source of transcendental bliss. Mundane rascals, who cannot understand the absolute nature of the Personality of Godhead, sometimes call Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa immoral, but they do not know that His seemingly immoral activities, which are not kept secret, afford pleasure to the devotees. Not understanding the transcendental behavior of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, these rascals slur His character and immediately fall into the category of miscreants (rascals, lowest among men, demons and those whose knowledge is taken away by the illusory energy). Kṛṣṇa explains in the *Bhagavad-gītā:*

*na māṁ duṣkṛtino mūḍhāḥ prapadyante narādhamāḥ*  
*māyayāpahṛta-jñānā āsuraṁ bhāvam āśritāḥ*

“Those miscreants who are grossly foolish, who are lowest among mankind, whose knowledge is stolen by illusion, and who partake of the atheistic nature of demons do not surrender unto Me.” (*Bg*. 7.15)

Here it is described how the Lord has openly declared himself to be a thief. Prabhupāda explains that this is the difference between the Absolute Truth and the relative truth. In the spiritual world, on the spiritual platform, the Lord may perform such an act as stealing, and there is no need to hide it or keep it a secret, as there is in the material world. In the material world, one who steals is a thief and is subject to be punished. This is because taking something that does not belong to us is certainly considered to be a criminal activity. There are many verses in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and *Bhagavad-gītā* where it is explained that persons who take something without giving what is required in return are considered to be thieves. They deserve to be punished by the laws of nature. This is described in a verse in the Seventh Canto of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.*

*yāvad bhriyeta jaṭharaṁ*  
*tāvat svatvaṁ hi dehinām*  
*adhikaṁ yo ’bhimanyeta*  
*sa steno daṇḍam arhati*

“One may claim proprietorship to as much wealth as required to maintain body and soul together, but one who desires proprietorship over more than that must be considered a thief, and he deserves to be punished by the laws of nature.” (*SB* 7.14.8)

In other words, anybody who takes or steals is subject to be punished. In the *Bhagavad-gītā,* Kṛṣṇa also explains that when persons take from the demigods, and do not offer sacrifices in return, they are subject to be punished. Therefore, generally persons who are thieves try to keep their activities secret in order to avoid punishment.

However, here Prabhupāda is saying that this is the difference between the Absolute Truth and the relative truth. The Supreme Lord stole the pot of sweet rice, but there was no secret. On the contrary, when devotees hear this description they derive great transcendental pleasure from it. This is because Kṛṣṇa’s stealing has nothing to do with the mentality found in the material world when somebody steals. In the material world when somebody steals, it is because of lust. However, there is no mundane lust in Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa is described as *ātmārāma*. He is fully satisfied; He does not need anything else to satisfy Himself. Why then would Kṛṣṇa steal? Why would Kṛṣṇa do so many other activities that are seemingly immoral?

Kṛṣṇa encouraged Arjuna to fight on the battlefield of Kurukṣetra. Encouraging somebody to kill might also appear to be an immoral activity.

Kṛṣṇa appeared in the form of Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva to kill Prahlāda Mahārāja’s father, Hiraṇyakaśipu, in the presence of Prahlāda Mahārāja. Prabhupāda made this point in one lecture on the *Teachings of Queen Kunti*. Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva kills Hiraṇyakaśipu, and Prahlāda Mahārāja is standing there watching. Prahlāda is offering a garland to Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva, saying, “My dear Lord, killer, please accept this garland.” The son should be anxious to protect his own father, but instead Prahlāda Mahārāja is explaining, “A saintly person is very happy when he sees a scorpion or snake killed.” He says this because scorpions and snakes are considered to be very envious creatures, and Prahlāda Mahārāja considered his father also to be very envious since he created many disturbances for many persons. Thus, Prahlāda was thinking, “Yes, this is good—my father has been killed by the hands of Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva.” The point is that whether the Lord is killing, stealing, or breaking His vow, He is still to be glorified.

We even glorify Kṛṣṇa for being a coward. There is the example of how He ran away from the battlefield when He was being attacked by Jarāsandha. To run away from the battlefield is considered to be an extremely cowardly activity. Kṛṣṇa had already defeated Jarāsandha and his army seventeen times, but the eighteenth time, when Kṛṣṇa decided to run away, Jarāsandha said, “Just see; I knew that Kṛṣṇa was a coward.” However, Kṛṣṇa was actually displaying His opulence of renunciation. He is completely renounced. He could have certainly stayed on the battlefield and killed Jarāsandha’s army for an eighteenth time, but He had another purpose to fulfill. The Lord always has a higher purpose to fulfill. Kṛṣṇa had received a letter from Rukmiṇī who wanted Him to come and kidnap her. Thus, being renounced, He thought, “Why take part in this battle?”

Jarāsandha chased Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma all the way to a mountain. They climbed up the mountain, and Jarāsandha lit a fire below so that the mountain was surrounded by fire on all sides. However, he did not see that Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma had gone all the way to the top of the mountain and had then jumped down. Jarāsandha thought that Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma had been killed. In this way, Lord Kṛṣṇa took the opportunity to immediately leave the battlefield in order to answer the call of Rukmiṇī. For a *kṣatriya* to leave the battlefield is cowardly behavior and is subject to punishment.

In *Bhagavad-gītā*, Kṛṣṇa is instructing Arjuna on the very same point, “If you leave the battle, you will be called a coward. Infamy is worse than death.” Nevertheless, when Kṛṣṇa leaves the battlefield, the devotees glorify His activity.

In India, there are many temples of Raṇacorajī, where the Supreme Lord is worshipped and glorified as a coward, having fled the battlefield. Such is the transcendental nature of the Supreme Lord. The devotees relish it so much because whatever the Lord does is always all good and absolute. He always has a higher purpose to fulfill, which is to satisfy the desire of His devotee.

Another example is that of Kṛṣṇa breaking His promise on the battlefield of Kurukṣetra. That also appears to be an immoral act. Bhīṣmadeva, who was fighting on the side of the Kauravas, had also made a promise because Duryodhana was challenging him. Duryodhana said, “You are not fighting to your full potential. You are favoring the Pāṇḍavas.” In this way, Duryodhana was challenging Bhīṣmadeva. So Bhīṣma made a vow that on the following day he would kill the Pāṇḍavas, including Arjuna. He said, “The only way that I will be stopped is if Kṛṣṇa personally stops me.”

Kṛṣṇa had made the promise that He would not take up weapons and would not fight in the battle. He would only be Arjuna’s chariot driver. However, when Bhīṣma was coming forward to kill Arjuna, nobody could stop him. He was fighting so ferociously. Kṛṣṇa got down from the chariot, picked up the wheel of the chariot, and began running after Bhīṣma, using the wheel of the chariot like a weapon to stop him. Kṛṣṇa broke His vow. Arjuna was very disturbed and did not want Kṛṣṇa to do this.

This can be seen in that painting of Arjuna holding Kṛṣṇa back by the waist, saying, “No Kṛṣṇa! Don’t do it! You are religiosity personified! This is terrible! You cannot break Your promise!” He was trying to prevent Kṛṣṇa from attacking Bhīṣmadeva. Arjuna was saying, “Let him kill me! Don’t break Your promise!” However, Kṛṣṇa was prepared to break His promise to protect His devotees—Arjuna as well as Bhīṣmadeva.

Since Bhīṣma made that vow, Kṛṣṇa had to protect it. The Lord always protects the words of His pure devotees. That is why Kṛṣṇa tells Arjuna (*Bg.* 9.31): *kaunteya pratijānīhi, na me bhaktaḥ praṇaśyati.* “Declare it boldly, Arjuna, that My devotees never perish.” He says, “You declare it, Arjuna, because if I say it, people may not accept it, since sometimes I break My word. If you say it though, everyone will accept it, because My devotees’ words always come true.” In order to uphold the promise of His devotee, Kṛṣṇa breaks His vows, runs from the battlefield, steals sweet rice, and steals butter and yogurt.

Prabhupāda explains in the purport, “Kṛṣṇa acts in very contradictory ways.” He is surrounded by millions of goddesses of fortune, yet He steals. It appears to be very contradictory. Although Kṛṣṇa could have anything that He possibly wants without having to steal it, He does it because He has a higher purpose that He wants to fulfill. Anybody else who steals, tells lies, or acts cowardly is subject to be punished. But Kṛṣṇa is above the laws of material nature. He says (*Bg*. 4.14):

*na māṁ karmāṇi limpanti*  
*na me karma-phale spṛhā*  
*iti māṁ yo ’bhijānāti*  
*karmabhir na sa badhyate*

“There is no work that affects Me; nor do I aspire for the fruits of action. One who understands this truth about Me also does not become entangled in the fruitive reactions of work.”

In other words, Kṛṣṇa says, “For Me, there are no reactions.” How can Kṛṣṇa have any reactions to His work? He is the Supreme Personality of Godhead. The laws of nature act according to His direction. He is completely above the laws of material nature, and is therefore not bound by them. He can act completely independently and never incur sin. Someone may say, “Well, why did Kṛṣṇa do that? He is God. He should be teaching by His example.” Kṛṣṇa says in *Bhagavad-gītā* (3.21):

*yad yad ācarati śreṣṭhas*  
*tat tad evetaro janaḥ*  
*sa yat pramāṇaṁ kurute*  
*lokas tad anuvartate*

“Whatever action a great man performs, common men follow. And whatever standards he sets by exemplary acts, all the world pursues.”

In fact, Kṛṣṇa is explaining this point in the Third Chapter of the *Bhagavad-gītā* when He explains that He also performs duties. Although in certain circumstances it may appear that Kṛṣṇa is acting in an immoral way, for Him it is not immoral. There are no reactions to His acts. We may do something similar, but if we try to imitate, we will get a reaction.

This is described in the Tenth Canto of the *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam.* Parīkṣit Mahārāja asked Śukadeva Gosvāmī about imitating Kṛṣṇa’s enjoying with the *gopīs*. He asked the question for the benefit of everyone else who might have the same question, and Śukadeva Gosvāmī explained that one cannot imitate the activities of the Lord. He gave the example of how Lord Śiva drank the ocean of poison and held it in his throat. That is why one of the names of Lord Śiva is Nīlakaṇṭha (blue throat). He held the poison in his throat to protect everyone from the ocean of poison that was being churned by the demigods. However, one should not imitate such great powerful controllers; doing so only causes one’s ruination.

The Supreme Lord cannot be imitated at any time. When the Lord displays such pastimes, He does so just to show how He is the Supreme Personality of Godhead. This is one of His opulences. Certainly, He also shows by His example. He accepts duties and He performs them, but He also displays other activities, such as enjoying in the dead of the night with others’ wives. However, Kṛṣṇa is completely free from lust.

Prabhupāda gives the example in the Second Canto of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* of how Bhṛgu Muni tested the Supreme Lord by placing his feet on Lord Viṣṇu’s chest. Prabhupāda explains in the purport that the Lord did not become angry by the acts of Bhṛgu Muni. He did not become controlled by anger. Prabhupāda says, “If there is no anger, how can there be any lust. Anger is even more powerful than lust. It is due to lust that anger is caused. However, in the Lord, there is no lust and there is no anger, although sometimes the Supreme Lord may manifest symptoms of anger.”

Similarly, the Lord appeared to be angered by the acts of Aśvatthāmā, and His eyes were described to be like copper— red-hot. However, the Supreme Lord’s anger is not because of any action directed towards Himself. In the material world, when someone becomes angry, it is due to unfulfilled lust. It is called *virodha yukta krodha*. *Virodha yukta krodha* means that lust is unsatisfied. There is no such anger in the Lord though, because there is no lust in the Lord. The Lord is completely on the transcendental platform. That means that He is not controlled by lust, anger, greed, illusion, or happiness. These are all symptoms of the influences of the modes of material nature. The Lord is controlled by one thing only—the love of His devotees. Nothing else can control the Lord because He is fully self-satisfied. The Supreme Lord is known as Puruṣa, the supreme enjoyer. He is eternally enjoying, because there is nothing else He has to do.

*na tasya kāryaṁ karaṇaṁ ca vidyate*  
*na tat-samaś cābhyadhikaś ca dṛśyate*  
*parāsya śaktir vividhaiva śrūyate*  
*svābhāvikī jñāna-bala-kriyā ca*

(Śvetāśvatara Upanisad 6.7–8)

It is described in the *Viṣṇu Purāṇa* that for the Supreme Lord there is no one to be found who is equal to Him, and there is nothing for Him to do, because everything happens very perfectly and systematically by His own sweet will. The Lord does not have to do anything because He is the supreme controller. How can the supreme controller, the creator of all laws, have to do anything? He does not need anything; He is fully self-satisfied and has a transcendental body, which is not subject to birth, death, disease, and old age. His senses are completely spiritual. He can enjoy anything with His spiritual senses. Why would there be anything that He has to do?

As ordinary living entities, being incomplete, we have to perform certain activities to fulfill our needs. Kṛṣṇa says, “Everybody has to work.” The living entities cannot be inactive even for a moment. Therefore, because the souls are embodied, they have to perform some sort of work. If they do not, they will have to suffer the reactions.

*yajñārthāt karmaṇo ’nyatra*  
*loko ’yaṁ karma-bandhanaḥ*

Work performed as a sacrifice to the Supreme Lord has to be performed; otherwise, work will bind one to the material world. But for Kṛṣṇa, there is nothing He has to do. (*Bg.* 3.9)

As long as we have these bodies, there are certain things that we must do. Everybody must eat to maintain the body. If we do not, there are consequences. Those who have a wife and children must do something to support their family. If they do not, there will also be consequences. We must answer the demands of the body, although we may not want to.

If there was nothing that we were obliged to do, what would we do with our time? If there were no need to do the things that need to be done, we would then do the things that we want to do. It is very clear. But there is nothing that Kṛṣṇa is obliged to do. Therefore, He says, “There is no work that affects Me, nor do I aspire for the fruits of work.” Being the supreme enjoyer, all Kṛṣṇa does is simply enjoy. That is what it means to be in the supreme position. He is supremely independent, and therefore He can do whatever He wants to do.

However, He does not want to keep this enjoyment for Himself; He wants to share it with others. He therefore says, “One who knows the truth about Me will not become entangled in fruitive reactions.” Thus, the living entities can also enjoy on the transcendental platform. Kṛṣṇa says (*Bg*. 10.9):

*mac-cittā mad-gata-prāṇā*  
*bodhayantaḥ parasparam*  
*kathayantaś ca māṁ nityaṁ*  
*tuṣyanti ca ramanti ca*

“The thoughts of My pure devotees dwell in Me, their lives are fully devoted to My service, and they derive great satisfaction and bliss from always enlightening one another and conversing about Me.”

Prabhupāda also makes this point. He gives the example that if Kṛṣṇa is immoral, how is it that those who are always glorifying the Supreme Lord become purified and completely free from all contamination? The more we glorify the Supreme Lord, His supreme activities, His pastimes, and His holy names, the more our heart becomes clean. We not only become free from all sinful reactions, but our heart becomes free from all sinful desires. Therefore, the devotees derive great satisfaction and bliss from hearing about these wonderful activities and pastimes of the Supreme Lord, and how the Lord enjoys the association of His pure devotees. When He stole the pot of sweet rice, the Lord was enjoying the association of His pure devotee.

Mādhavendra Purī desired within his heart: “If, without asking, I could just taste some of that sweet rice, then I will find out how this sweet rice can be offered to my Lord Gopāla.” He then became embarrassed. “Oh, I had such terrible, lusty thoughts, thinking of enjoying the sweet rice before it was offered to the Lord.” Kṛṣṇa, however, was thinking that his desires were completely pure.

Kṛṣṇa likes to be controlled by the love of His pure devotees. He derives His greatest pleasure by becoming subordinate to His pure devotees. He does this because He wants to please and satisfy His pure devotee.

When the *gopīs* worshipped goddess Kātyāyanī for Kṛṣṇa to be their husband, Kṛṣṇa wanted to satisfy their desire because their love for Him was so unalloyed and pure. He did not want to satisfy lusty desires.

Prabhupāda explains that miscreants, not understanding the transcendental behavior of the Supreme Lord, think, “Why is Kṛṣṇa stealing the garments of the *gopīs*?” They cannot understand. The *gopīs* were bathing, and Kṛṣṇa took their garments. They thus had to come one by one to retrieve their garments.

A woman cannot be seen exposed before anyone but her husband. Kṛṣṇa, knowing their desire to be His wives, satisfied the *gopīs’* desire through this pastime. These were not lusty desires that He was satisfying, but rather the desires deep within the heart of His pure devotees.

The Lord is always inclined to satisfy the desires of His pure devotees. *Yoga-māyā* therefore arranges things in such a way that the Lord always fulfills their desires. But those who are miscreants cannot understand. It remains a complete mystery to them because they cannot understand the purpose of the Lord. A devotee, however, is one who does understand the purpose of the Lord, and thus, a devotee never tries to imitate the Lord. He only serves the Lord, and follows His instructions.

Prabhupāda always used to speak out against the *sahajīyas* who try to imitate Kṛṣṇa in *rāsa-līlā*. For them, it is just like imitating Lord Śiva drinking the ocean of poison. Prabhupāda says, “Yes, they are very quick to imitate Kṛṣṇa’s *rāsa-līlā,* but when it comes time to lift Govardhana Hill, what can they do? They want to imitate *rāsa-līlā.* Okay, then lift Govardhana too.” Immediately, however, they want to go right to *rāsa-līlā,* and they want to hear these topics because they are seemingly filled with lusty desires. They think, “Yes, by hearing these activities, I can also imitate these activities and enjoy with others’ wives.” Such thoughts bring ruination to them. Enjoying with others’ wives is just like drinking poison. One cannot imitate the activities of the Supreme Lord. That is why Prabhupāda is here describing such persons as miscreants.

Prabhupāda continues his explanation (*Cc. Madhy* 4.133, Purport):

Mundane rascals cannot understand that whatever Kṛṣṇa does, being absolute in nature, is allgood. This quality of the Lord is explained in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (10.33.29). One may consider certain acts of a supremely powerful person to be immoral by mundane calculations, but this is not actually the case. For example, the sun absorbs water from the surface of the earth, but it does not absorb water only from the sea. It also absorbs water from filthy sewers and ditches containing urine and other impure substances. The sun is not polluted by absorbing such water. Rather, the sun makes the filthy place pure. If a devotee approaches the Supreme Personality of Godhead for an immoral or improper purpose, he nonetheless becomes purified; the Lord does not become infected.

The example is also given that fire can consume whatever is put into it but never becomes polluted by what it consumes. This is explained in a verse from the *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam* (2.3.10):

*akāmaḥ sarva-kāmo vā*  
*mokṣa-kāma udāra-dhīḥ*  
*tīvreṇa bhakti-yogena*  
*yajeta puruṣaṁ param*

We should worship the Supreme Personality of Godhead whether we are full of material desires, have no material desires, or desire liberation. Even if we are filled with material desires, if we come in contact with the Supreme Lord, we will be purified of those desires. The Lord purifies the desires within the heart, but He does not become infected by taking out those desires. For whatever reason we approach the Supreme Personality of Godhead—even for the fulfillment of material desires—our desires become purified.

Kṛṣṇa says, “If one engages in My transcendental loving service but at the same time wants the opulence of material enjoyment, he is very, very foolish. Indeed, he is just like a person who gives up ambrosia to drink poison. Since I am very intelligent, why should I give this fool material prosperity? Instead I shall induce him to take the nectar of the shelter of My lotus feet and make him forget illusory material enjoyment.” (*Cc. Madhya* 22.38–39)

When devotees approach the Lord for the fulfillment of material desires, the Lord will fulfill their desires in such a way that they will not come back and ask for anything else. Śrīla Prabhupāda explains (*Cc. Madhya* 4.133, Purport): “In *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (10.29.15) it is stated that if one approaches the Supreme Lord even out of lust, anger or fear (*kāmaṁ krodhaṁ bhayam*), he is purified.”

This verse from the Tenth Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* is:

*kāmaṁ krodhaṁ bhayaṁ sneham*  
*aikyaṁ sauhṛdam eva ca*

A person who constantly thinks of the Lord out of lust, anger, fear, protective affection—like Mother Yaśodā trying to protect Kṛṣṇa—friendship, or feelings of impersonal oneness will certainly become purified, because he is always absorbed in thoughts of Lord Hari. Prabhupāda continues:

The *gopīs*, being young girls, approached Kṛṣṇa because He was a beautiful young boy. From the external point of view, they approached the Lord out of lust, and the Lord danced with them at midnight. From the mundane point of view, these activities may appear immoral because a married or unmarried young girl cannot leave home to mix with a young boy and dance with him. Although this is immoral from the mundane viewpoint, the activities of the *gopīs* are accepted as the highest form of worship because it was Lord Kṛṣṇa whom they approached with lusty desires in the dead of night.

But these things cannot be understood by nondevotees. One must understand Kṛṣṇa in *tattva* (truth). One should use his common sense and consider that if simply by chanting Kṛṣṇa’s holy name one is purified, how then can the person Kṛṣṇa be immoral? Unfortunately, mundane fools are accepted as educational leaders and are offered exalted posts for teaching irreligious principles to the general populace. This is explained in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (7.5.31): *andhā yathāndhair upanīyamānāḥ*. Blind men are trying to lead other blind men. Due to the immature understanding of such rascals, common men should not discuss Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes with the *gopīs*. A nondevotee should not even discuss His stealing sweet rice for His devotees. It is warned that one should not even think about these things. (*Cc. Madhya* 4.133, Purport)

The non-devotees think that it is alright to do these things, imitating Kṛṣṇa, but they know nothing about Kṛṣṇa. It is not possible to imitate the Supreme Lord. Only the Supreme Lord can be the supreme enjoyer. Anybody who tries to imitate the supreme enjoyer does so out of envy. All the living entities that are conditioned in the material world are here precisely because they try to imitate the Supreme Lord and enjoy independently from Him. Therefore, nondevotees cannot understand these pastimes.

This is why Prabhupāda warns that non-devotees should not even discuss these pastimes. Only devotees can understand the purpose of the Lord. To understand the Lord means one has to engage in His service. Therefore, these pastimes are described to be confidential pastimes of the Supreme Lord.

In order to know something confidential about someone, you have to be close to him and trusted by him. You cannot approach someone you do not know and ask, “Can you tell me something about your deep personal life?” In the same way, nobody can know anything about Kṛṣṇa’s personality unless he renders service to Him. It is therefore explained that unless one gets a drop of mercy from the lotus feet of Lord Kṛṣṇa, he will never be able to understand His greatness. We cannot understand Him by mental speculation or even by studying the *Vedas* for many births.

One has to get some mercy from the Lord’s lotus feet if he wants to know something about the Personality of Godhead. Otherwise, the Lord’s personality will always remain a mystery. This is why Kṛṣṇa told Arjuna that austerities, Vedic sacrifices, and charity did not qualify Arjuna to see and understand Him. Kṛṣṇa said, “Only by unalloyed devotional service can I be seen as I am, standing directly before you. Only then can you enter into the mysteries of understanding Me.” It is not possible to understand anything about Kṛṣṇa without rendering devotional service. The purport continues (*Cc. Madhya* 4.133, Purport):

Although Kṛṣṇa is the purest of the pure, mundane people, thinking of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes that appear immoral, themselves become polluted. It is warned that one should not even think about these things.

Here it states that when mundane people think about Kṛṣṇa’s confidential pastimes, they become polluted. Prabhupāda often used to quote: “Milk touched by the lips of a serpent becomes poisonous.” Similarly, one should not hear Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes from non-devotees—especially the Māyāvādīs. To hear from the Māyāvādīs is just like pure milk touched by the lips of a serpent; it has a poisonous effect. Prabhupāda goes on to explain how persons can begin to understand something about Kṛṣṇa:

Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu therefore never publicly discussed Kṛṣṇa’s dealings with the *gopīs*. He used to discuss these dealings only with three confidential friends. He never discussed *rāsa-līlā* publicly, as the professional reciters do, although they do not understand Kṛṣṇa or the nature of the audience. However, Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu encouraged the public chanting of the holy name on a huge scale for as many hours as possible.

This is how one should become qualified to understand Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes. One should begin one’s devotional service by chanting the holy name of the Lord:

*etan nirvidyamānānām*  
*icchatām akuto-bhayam*  
*yogināṁ nṛpa nirṇītaṁ*  
*harer nāmānukīrtanam*

Śukadeva Gosvāmī says to Parīkṣit Mahārāja:

“O King, constant chanting of the holy name of the Lord after the ways of the great authorities is the doubtless and fearless way of success for all, including those who are free from all material desires, those who are desirous of all material enjoyment, and also those who are self-satisfied by dint of transcendental knowledge.” (*SB* 2.1.11)

One can begin his devotional service and become qualified to understand the pastimes of the Lord, but the entire process of devotional service must be accompanied by the chanting of the holy names of the Lord. Therefore, Prabhupāda says, “Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu encouraged the public chanting of the holy name on a huge scale for as many hours as possible. He never publicly discussed Kṛṣṇa’s dealings with the *gopīs*, because one first has to become qualified to understand these topics.” (*Cc. Madhya* 4.133, Purport)

If we want to understand the glories of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, certainly we have to hear from the proper sources.

*nitya-siddha kṛṣṇa-prema ‘sādhya’ kabhu naya*  
*śravaṇādi-śuddha-citte karaye udaya*

(Cc. Madhya 22.107)

Pure love for Kṛṣṇa is eternally situated in the heart of every living being. It does not come from any other source. If the living entity hears from the right source about Kṛṣṇa, then that pure love is gradually awakened (*SB* 1.2.17):

*śṛṇvatāṁ sva-kathāḥ kṛṣṇaḥ*  
*puṇya-śravaṇa-kīrtanaḥ*  
*hṛdy antaḥ stho hy abhadrāṇi*  
*vidhunoti suhṛt satām*

“Śrī Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, is the Supersoul in the heart of every living entity. He cleanses the desire for material enjoyment within the heart of the truthful devotee, who develops the urge to hear His messages, which themselves are virtuous if properly heard and chanted.”

These are different examples of how Kṛṣṇa may appear to be immoral. Śrīla Prabhupāda has given us this elaborate purport so that we can properly understand the meaning of Lord Gopīnātha’s stealing of the sweet rice.

## THE ACTIVITIES OF SĀKṢI-GOPĀLA

**Madhya-līlā: 5.10–119**

**Donetsk—August 26, 1993**

The pastime of Sākṣi-gopāla in this chapter was revealed to Lord Nityānanda when He was touring India to see the places of pilgrimage. In His travels, He came to Kaṭaka to see the Sākṣi-gopāla Deity. There, Lord Nityānanda heard the story of Sākṣi-gopāla from the townspeople. Lord Nityānanda is now reciting this pastime to Lord Caitanya, who has come to this same village and is hearing this narration with great pleasure.

**Formerly, at Vidyānagara in South India, there were two** *brāhmaṇas* **who made a long tour to see different places of pilgrimage. First of all they visited Gayā, then Kāśī, then Prayāga, and finally, with great pleasure, they came to Mathurā. After reaching Mathurā, they started visiting the different forests of Vṛndāvana and came to Govardhana Hill. They visited all twelve forests \[***vanas***\] and at last came to the town of Vṛndāvana. In the village of Pañcakrośī in Vṛndāvana, where the site of the Govinda temple is situated, there was a great temple where gorgeous worship of Gopāla was performed. (***Cc. Madhya* **5.10–13)**

This temple in Vṛndāvana is the Rādhā-Govinda temple, which at one time was a big temple where Rādhā-Govinda Deities were worshiped. During the time when Muslims were invading different places in Mathurā and Vṛndāvana, many of the temples were attacked. The Rādhā-Govinda Deities were taken to Jaipur where Their worship continues today. In Jaipur there is a very large temple of Rādhā-Govinda in the middle of the city. Every morning, most of the townspeople come to *maṅgala-ārati.*

The Deities there are the original Rādhā-Govinda Deities that were taken from Vṛndāvana. This is where, as described in the verse, there was a great temple where the gorgeous worship of Gopāla was performed. Today one can see very beautiful carvings on the remains of this temple, but the temple used to be much bigger.

The Muslim king, Aurangzeb, would see the temple from the window of his room, and was envious of its height. He sent his men to remove all the upper floors of this temple. What was left of the Rādhā-Govinda temple is that which exists today. This temple existed during the times of Lord Caitanya.

Lord Caitanya and His associates had visited this temple, and then, after taking bath in different bathing places along the river Yamunā, the pilgrims returned to the temple of Gopāla and took rest there.

**The beauty of the Gopāla Deity stole away their minds, and feeling great happiness, they remained there for two or four days. One of the two** *brāhmaṇas* **was an old man, and the other was young. The young man was assisting the old one. Indeed, the young** *brāhmaṇa* **always rendered service to the older one, and the old man, being very satisfied with his service, was pleased with him.**

**The older man told the younger, “You have rendered various types of service to me. You have assisted me in traveling to all these places of pilgrimage. Even my own son does not render such service. By your mercy, I did not become fatigued while on this tour. If I do not show you any respect, I would be ungrateful. Therefore, I promise to give you my daughter in charity.”**

**The younger** *brāhmaṇa* **replied, “My dear sir, please hear me. You are saying something very unusual. Such a thing never happens. You are a most aristocratic family man, well educated and very rich. I am not at all aristocratic, and I am without a decent education and have** **no wealth. Sir, I am not a suitable bridegroom for your daughter. I render service to you only for the satisfaction of Kṛṣṇa.” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.15–23)**

**Purport**

Both *brāhmaṇas* were pure Vaiṣṇavas. The younger man took special care of the older one simply to please Kṛṣṇa. In *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (11.19.21) Kṛṣṇa says, *mad-bhakta-pūjābhyadhikā*: “It is better to render service to My devotee.” Thus, according to the Gauḍīya-Vaiṣṇava philosophy of Caitanya Mahāprabhu, it is better to be a servant of the servant of God \[*Cc*. *Madhya* 13.80\]. One should not try to serve Kṛṣṇa directly.

The younger *brāhmaṇa*, was only serving the older *brāhmaṇa* for Kṛṣṇa’s pleasure. He was very devoted in this way. He was not expecting any kind of remuneration for his service. When a devotee thinks that he is rendering service for Kṛṣṇa’s pleasure, he is satisfied if Kṛṣṇa is satisfied.

**\[The younger** *brāhmaṇa* **continued,\] “Lord Kṛṣṇa is very pleased with service rendered to** *brāhmaṇas***, and when the Lord is pleased, the opulence of devotional service increases.”**

**The older** *brāhmaṇa* **replied, “My dear boy, do not doubt me. I will give you my daughter in charity. I have already decided this.” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.24–25)**

The older *brāhmaṇa* was utterly fixed in his conviction, and although the younger *brāhmaṇa* did not want to accept anything in charity, the older *brāhmaṇa* felt that it was a suitable way to reciprocate with him.

In Vedic times, this was common for parents to do, especially with the daughter. The father’s duty was to see that the daughter was married by a certain age. Thus, this older *brāhmaṇa* wanted to give his daughter in charity to someone who would be a suitable husband. However, as there was no suitable husband for her, he decided to give her in charity to this *brāhmaṇa.*

**The younger** *brāhmaṇa* **said, “You have a wife and sons, and you have a large circle of relatives and friends. Without the consent of all your friends and relatives, it is not possible to give me your daughter in charity. Just consider the story of Queen Rukmiṇī and her father Bhīṣmaka. King Bhīṣmaka wanted to give his daughter, Rukmiṇī, in charity to Kṛṣṇa, but Rukmī, his eldest son, objected. Therefore he could not carry out his decision.”**

**The elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **said, “My daughter is my own property. If I choose to give my property to someone, who has the power to stop me? My dear boy, I will give my daughter to you in charity, and I will neglect the position of all others. Don’t doubt me in this regard; just accept my proposal.”**

**The younger** *brāhmaṇa* **replied, “If you have decided to give me your young daughter to me, then say so before the Gopāla Deity.”**

**Coming before Gopāla, the elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **said, “My dear Lord, please witness that I have given my daughter to this boy.” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.26–32)**

Prabhupāda explains in the purport that an old custom in India is for a man’s daughter to be offered to someone upon his word.

**Purport**

(…) In India the custom is to honor any promise made before the Deity. Such a promise cannot be canceled. In Indian villages, whenever there is a quarrel between two parties, they go to a temple to settle the quarrel. Whatever is spoken before the Deities is taken to be true, for no one dares to lie before the Deities. The same principle was followed on the battlefield of Kurukṣetra. Therefore, at the very beginning of *Bhagavad-gita* it is said, dharma*kṣetre kuru-kṣetre* \[*Bg*. 1.1\].

Śrīla Prabhupāda continues to explain in the purport that Vedic people were of a very high moral character. They understood the laws of God. No one would dare speak a lie before the Deity. Therefore, during Vedic times disputes were settled in this way.

In contrast, in modern times when there is a dispute, people come before a judge in the courtroom. The Twelfth Canto of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* describes that in the age of Kali-yuga people settle their disputes and obtain justice with money. That is a symptom of Kali-yuga. The person who has the most money pays for justice. This is how it is in most places in the world. If you want to have the protection of the law, you have to hire an attorney, go to court, and pay the court fees. If you want a very good attorney, you have to pay a great deal of money—and the “best” attorney is one who knows the judge. Many thousands of dollars must be paid to get such an attorney who knows the judge, and who will therefore often make a decision in favor of his associate’s client. Thus, in Kali-yuga there is no real justice. Similar to the saying, “Might makes right,” if you are strong, powerful, and have money, you win. If you do not have money, you lose. That is Kali-yuga.

However, in Vedic culture it was not like that. If there was a quarrel or dispute between two people, because of their very high standards of morality, a person could very amiably say, “Let us go to the Deity!” The other person amiably would say, “Yes, this is how the dispute will be settled.” Since they knew that they had to speak the truth before the Deity, there was no need for quarrelsome decisions, hiring lawyers, spending money for investigations, and bringing many witnesses whom you would sometimes have to pay.

The younger *brāhmaṇa* knew this and anticipated that the old *brāhmaṇa* would run into some problems. The younger *brāhmaṇa* was not attached to receiving the old *brāhmaṇa’s* daughter in marriage. But he insisted that the older *brāhmaṇa* promise before the Deity because he wanted to protect the honor of the older *brāhmaṇa*. He did not want him to make a statement that he could not keep.

**Then the younger** *brāhmaṇa* **addressed the Deity, saying, “My dear Lord, You are my witness. I shall call for You to testify if it is necessary later on.” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.33)**

Since the Deity was acting as a witness (*sākṣi*) to the *brāhmaṇa’s* promise, He was called Sākṣi-gopāla. Neither of the *brāhmaṇas* saw the Deity as a statue; they saw Him as God.

**After these talks, the two** *brāhmaṇas* **started for home. As usual, the young** *brāhmaṇa* **accompanied the elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **as if the old** *brāhmaṇa* **were a** *guru* **(spiritual master), and rendered him service in various ways. After returning to Vidyānagara, each** *brāhmaṇa* **went to his respective home. After some time, the elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **became very anxious. He began to think, “I have given my word to a** *brāhmaṇa* **in a holy place, and what I promised will certainly come to pass. I must now disclose this to my wife, sons, other relatives and friends.” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.34–36)**

Although the old *brāhmaṇa* gave his promise and realized that he had to keep it, he suspected that there might be some resistance from his family.

One day the *brāhmaṇa* called all his relatives and told them of everything that had taken place in front of the Gopāla Deity. When the family members heard this narration, they showed great disappointment, and they all requested him not to give such promises again. They unanimously said, “If you give your daughter to a degraded family, your aristocracy will be lost. When others hear of this, they will laugh at you.”

**The elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **said, “How can I undo the promise I made in a holy place while on pilgrimage? Whatever may happen, I must give him my daughter in charity.”**

**The relatives unanimously said, “If you give your daughter to that boy, we shall give up all connection with you.” Indeed, his wife and sons declared, “If such a thing happens, we shall take poison and die.”**

**The elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **said, “If I do not give my daughter to the young** *brāhmaṇa***, he will call Śrī Gopālajī as a witness. Thus he will take my daughter by force, and in that case my religious principles will become meaningless.”**

**His son replied, “The Deity may be a witness, but He is in a distant country. How can He come to bear witness against you? Why are you so anxious over this? You do not have to flatly deny that you spoke such a thing. There is no need to make a false statement. Simply say that you do not remember what you said. If you simply say, ‘I do not remember,’ I shall take care of the rest. By argument, I shall defeat the young** *brāhmaṇa***.” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.40-45)**

Śrīla Prabhupāda explains in his purport that the son of the elderly *brāhmaṇa* was an atheist, and although he was very expert in dealing with money, he was a great fool. Not having any faith in the Supreme Lord, he did not believe in the spiritual position of the Deity. Such consideration of the form of the Lord to be made of stone or wood is idol worship. The son assured his father that since his witness was only a stone Deity, it would not be capable of speaking. He further argued that since the Deity was situated far away, He would not be able to come bear witness. The essence of what he was telling his father was, “Do not have any anxiety! You don’t have to lie directly. You should just speak like a diplomat.”

Similarly, King Yudhiṣṭhira had said to Droṇācārya, “*aśvatthāmā hata iti gajah*,” which means “Aśvatthāmā is dead.” But the truth in “*aśvatthāmā hata iti gajah*” is that it was actually the elephant, Aśvatthāmā, that was dead.

The reason why Yudhiṣṭhira said this was because Droṇācārya could only be killed if he was lamenting, and Aśvatthāmā was a son of Droṇācārya. Lord Kṛṣṇa told Yudhiṣṭhira Mahārāja to tell Droṇācārya that his son, Aśvatthāmā, had been killed. However, Yudhiṣṭhira Mahārāja did not want to lie and thus hesitated. But it so happened that at this particular time there was an elephant named Aśvatthāmā who had been killed. He said, “Aśvatthāmā the elephant has been killed,” but when he said “the elephant,” a bugle was suddenly blown very loudly. All that Droṇācārya could hear was “Aśvatthāmā has been killed.” Thus, when Droṇācārya heard the name “Aśvatthāmā”, he immediately thought of his son, although King Yudhiṣṭhira was referring to the elephant Aśvatthāmā. Droṇācārya was thus thrown into lamentation and that is how he was defeated on the battlefield of Kurukṣetra. Otherwise, he could not have been killed.

The elderly *brāhmaṇa’s* son thus said, “You should speak like a diplomat, like King Yudhiṣṭhira. Following King Yudhiṣṭhira, you should say that you do not remember anything and are not aware of what this young *brāhmaṇa* is claiming.” The son explained that if his father simply started by saying that, then he would fill in the argument and defeat the young *brāhmaṇa* by word jugglery. The atheistic son of the elderly *brāhmaṇa* was very intent on preventing his father from giving away his sister in marriage.

**When the elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **heard this, his mind became very agitated. Feeling helpless, he simply turned his attention to the lotus feet of Gopāla. \[He\] prayed, “My dear Lord Gopāla, I have taken shelter of Your lotus feet, and therefore I request You to please protect my religious principles from disturbance and at the same time save my kinsmen from dying.” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.46–47)**

We can see the qualities and characteristics of this elderly *brāhmaṇa*. He was very devoted to the Lord and therefore prayed to Him, “Please, protect my religious principles.”

**The next day the elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **was thinking about this matter very deeply when the young** *brāhmaṇa* **came to his house. He came and offered his respectful obeisances, then folded his hands and spoke as follows: “You had promised to give your daughter in charity to me. Now, you don’t say anything. What is your conclusion?” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.48–50)**

The elderly *brāhmaṇa* remained silent. Taking the opportunity provided by the silence of his father, his son immediately came out of his house with a stick to strike the younger *brāhmaṇa.*

**The son said, “Oh, you are most degraded! You want to marry my sister, just like a dwarf who wants to catch the moon!” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.52)**

The young *brāhmaṇa* saw the stick in the hand of the son and ran. The next day, he gathered together all the people of the village, who then called for the elderly *brāhmaṇa.* He was brought to the meeting place, and the young *brāhmaṇa* began to speak to all of them as follows:

**This gentleman has promised to hand over his daughter to me, yet now he does not follow his promise. Please ask him about his behavior.**

**All the people gathered there asked the elderly** *brāhmaṇa***, “If you have already promised to give him your daughter in charity, why are you not fulfilling your promise? You have given your word of honor.”**

**The elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **said, “My dear friends, please hear what I have to submit. I do not exactly remember making a promise like that.”**

**When the elderly** *brāhmaṇa’s* **son heard this, he took the opportunity to juggle some words. Becoming very impudent, he stood before the assembly and spoke as follows \[to uphold his father’s story\].**

**While touring various holy places of pilgrimage, my father carried much money. Seeing the money, this rogue decided to take it away. There was no one besides this man with my father. Giving him an intoxicant known as** *dhuturā* **to eat, this rogue made my father mad. Having taken all my father’s money, this rogue claimed that it was taken by some thief. Now he is claiming that my father has promised to give him his daughter in charity. All of you assembled here are gentlemen. Please judge whether it is befitting to offer this poor** *brāhmaṇa* **my father’s daughter. (***Cc. Madhya* **5.55-62)**

All the people were a little bit doubtful. They thought it was possible that because of attraction for riches one might give up his religious principles, but they doubted the words of the son.

Then the young *brāhmaṇa* said, “Please hear. Just to gain a victory in an argument, this man is lying.” He then told the whole story in truth: “The elderly *brāhmaṇa* wanted to repay me for my services. I warned him not to make such a promise, but he insisted that I accept his daughter. I then told him,

‘You are a learned *brāhmaṇa,* and your wife and your relatives would never agree to this proposal.’ I then requested that we go to the Deity Gopāla.

“This gentleman said in front of the Gopāla Deity, ‘My dear Lord, please be my witness.’

“Accepting the Gopāla Deity as my witness, I then said, ‘If this *brāhmaṇa* later hesitates to give me his daughter, I shall call on You, my dear Lord, as a witness.’

**Thus I have called upon a great personality in this transaction. I have asked the Supreme Godhead to be my witness. The entire world accepts the words of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.**

**Taking this opportunity, the elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **immediately confirmed that this was really true. He said, “If Gopāla personally comes here to serve as a witness, I shall surely give my daughter to the young** *brāhmaṇa***.”**

**The elderly** *brāhmaṇa’s* **son \[who is an atheist\] immediately confirmed this, saying, “Yes, this is a very nice settlement.” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.76-78)**

Then the young *brāhmaṇa* said, “Please, write this down on paper so you may not again change your word of honor.” This statement was written and signed by both *brāhmaṇas* before all the assembled people, who would serve as mediators to settle the dispute.

The young *brāhmaṇa* spoke, “I want all of you to know that this elderly *brāhmaṇa* is very truthful. He is following religious principles. He had no desire to break his promise, but fearing that his family members would commit suicide, he did not want to tell the truth. To keep the promise of the pious elderly *brāhmaṇa* intact*,* I shall call the Supreme Personality of Godhead as a witness.”

Hearing the statement of the younger *brāhmaṇa*, some atheists started to immediately say jokes, but someone then said, “The Lord is merciful, and if He likes, He can come.”

At the conclusion of the meeting, the young *brāhmaṇa* went to Vṛndāvana. After offering his respectful obeisances to the Deity, he told the Deity everything, in full detail.

**\[The young** *brāhmaṇa***\] said, “My Lord, You are the protector of brahminical culture, and You are also very merciful. Therefore, kindly show Your great mercy by protecting the religious principles of us two** *brāhmaṇas***. My dear Lord, I am not thinking to become happy by getting the daughter as a bride. I am simply thinking that the** *brāhmaṇa* **has broken his promise, and that is giving me great pain. (…) You are very merciful, and You know everything. Therefore, kindly be a witness in this case. A person who knows things as they are and still does not bear witness becomes involved in sinful activity.” (***Cc. Madhya* **5.88-90**

Lord Kṛṣṇa replied by instructing the *brāhmaṇa* to go back home and call a meeting. The Lord promised that if the *brāhmaṇa* simply remembered Him, He would appear there to bear witness to the promise and therefore protect the honor of both *brāhmaṇas.*

The young *brāhmaṇa* replied that even if He were to appear in His four-handed Viṣṇu form, the village people would still not believe His words. The young *brāhmaṇa* said that they would only believe Him if He were to go in this beautiful form of Gopāla and speak to all the people.

Lord Kṛṣṇa argued that He had never heard of a Deity walking from one place to another.

The *brāhmaṇa* agreed, but then said, “You are talking to me now. How is that possible for a Deity? If You can talk, then surely You can walk.” The *brāhmaṇa* told Lord Gopāla that He is not a statue, but rather the son of Mahārāja Nanda. He then asked the Lord to do something that He had never done before, for the sake of the old *brāhmaṇa.*

**Śrī Gopālajī then smiled and said, “My dear** *brāhmaṇa***, just listen to Me. I shall walk behind you, and in this way I shall go with you. (…) Do not try to see Me by turning around. As soon as you see Me, I shall remain stationary in that very place. You will know that I am walking behind you by the sound of My ankle bells. Cook one kilo of rice daily and offer it. I shall eat that rice and follow behind you.”**

**The next day, the** *brāhmaṇa* **begged permission from Gopāla and started for his country. Gopāla followed him, step by step. (***Cc. Madhya* **5.97-101)**

The young *brāhmaṇa* was very pleased to hear the tinkling sound of Gopāla’s ankle bells as He followed him. He cooked first-class rice for Gopāla to eat. The young *brāhmaṇa* continued walking until he arrived in his country. As he approached his village, he thought, “Now that I have arrived at my village, I shall go home and inform everyone of the witness’s arrival.”

Then the *brāhmaṇa* became concerned that if the people did not directly see the Gopāla Deity, they would not believe the *brāhmaṇa’s* word of His arrival. But he then concluded that there is nothing to fear if Gopāla stays where He is now, as it is close enough to his home. He turned around and saw that Gopāla was standing there smiling.

The Lord told the *brāhmaṇa* to go home and that He would remain where He is.

This Gopāla Deity was a very big Deity. It was therefore not possible for the *brāhmaṇa* to have carried Him from His temple to the village where he lived. The only way the Deity could have arrived there is by walking Himself.

After Gopāla gave His assurance that He would not leave, the young *brāhmaṇa* went to the town and informed the people of Gopāla’s arrival. They were struck with wonder and went to see the witness Gopāla. Upon seeing the Lord standing there, they immediately offered their respectful obeisances. They were very pleased by the beauty of Gopāla and were very surprised to hear that He had actually walked there.

**Then the elderly** *brāhmaṇa***, being very pleased, came forward and immediately fell like a stick in front of Gopāla.**

**Thus in the presence of all the townspeople, Lord Gopāla bore witness that the elderly** *brāhmaṇa* **had offered his daughter in charity to the young** *brāhmaṇa.*

**After the marriage ceremony was performed, the** **Lord informed both** *brāhmaṇas***, “You two** *brāhmaṇas* **are** **My eternal servants, birth after birth. (…) I have become very much pleased by the truthfulness of you both. Now you can ask for a benediction.” Thus with great pleasure the two** *brāhmaṇas* **begged for a benediction.**

**The** *brāhmaṇas* **said, “Please remain here so that people all over the world will know how merciful You are to Your servants.”**

**Lord Gopāla stayed, and the two** *brāhmaṇas* **engaged in His service. After hearing of the incident, many people from different countries began to come to see Gopāla.**

**Eventually the King of that country heard this wonderful story, and he also came to see Gopāla and thus became very satisfied. The King constructed a nice temple, and regular service was executed. Gopāla became very famous under the name of Sākṣi-gopāla \[the witness Gopāla\]. (***Cc. Madhya* **5.111-118)**

This story was narrated to Lord Caitanya when He was visiting the temple of Sākṣi-gopāla.

Śrīla Prabhupāda used to tell this story to illustrate how the Supreme Lord reciprocates with His pure devotees. The Deity is not to be considered as brass or stone. Rather, the Lord actually reciprocates with the pure love of His devotees. The Lord very kindly agrees to manifest Himself in the form of the Deity to accept the service of His devotees.

Thus, Lord Gopāla described that due to the purity of these two *brāhmaṇas,* they are eternal servants of the Lord, birth after birth. These *brāhmaṇas* were not ordinary conditioned souls. They were pure devotees of the Lord, and based on their pure devotion to the Lord, He agreed to become their witness.

One should not think that this is just a story. We should understand that it is an actual fact. This temple even exists today, and pilgrims come from many places to take *darśana* of the Sākṣi-gopāla Deity.

Śrīla Prabhupāda often describes how the Deity is nondifferent from Kṛṣṇa; it is Kṛṣṇa Himself who agrees to appear in this form.

Śrīla Prabhupāda gives the example of an expert electrician who can realize the electrical energy and manipulate it according to his will—either for cooling or for heating. In the same way, the Supreme Lord can transform material to spiritual. He can make what appears to be material completely spiritual. Although Deities may be made out of brass or stone, if the Supreme Lord agrees to appear in that form of the Deity, then it is non-different from Kṛṣṇa.

It is explained in Vedic literature that it is by the request of the pure devotee of the Lord that the Lord appears as the Deity to accept the service of the devotees. He appears because He is bound by the desire of the pure devotee. We see that Śrīla Prabhupāda installed many Deities all over the world.

One time, Śrīla Prabhupāda was performing an installation ceremony in Australia, and he was very concerned because he could see that his disciples were not qualified. He said, “I must take great risks.” He installed the Deity and prayed to the Deity, “Please protect me and forgive me. Please accept the worship of my disciples.” Prabhupāda was praying in this way for the Lord to appear in the form of the Deity to accept this service of the worship of his disciples.

By the intense praying of His pure devotee, the Lord agrees to appear. In this way, Advaita Ācārya’s intense prayers, worship of *śālagrāma-śilā,* and offerings of *tulasī* induced Lord Caitanya to appear. Such is the power of the pure devotee. The pure devotee, binding the Lord by his prayer, can invoke His presence.

Thus, the Deity Gopāla was invoked to bear witness by the intense prayers of these *brāhmaṇas,* because they were praying in pure devotion.

## THE LORD BEGINS HIS TOUR OF SOUTH INDIA

**Madhya-līlā: 7.2–21**

**Vladivostok—November 6, 1998**

**All glories to Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu! All glories to Lord Nityānanda Prabhu! All glories to Advaita Ācārya! And all glories to all the devotees of Lord Caitanya! After delivering Sārvabhauma Bhaṭṭācārya, the Lord desired to go to South India to preach. (***Cc. Madhya* **7.2-3)**

**Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu called all His devotees together and, holding them by the hand, humbly informed them, “You are all more dear to Me than My life. I can give up My life, but to give you up is difficult for Me. You are all My friends, and you have properly executed the duties of friends by bringing Me here to Jagannātha Purī and giving Me the chance to see Lord Jagannātha in** **the temple. I now beg all of you for one bit of charity. Please give Me permission to leave for a tour of South India. I shall go to search out Viśvarūpa. Please forgive Me, but I want to go alone; I do not wish to take anyone with Me. Until I return from Setubandha, all of you dear friends should remain at Jagannātha Purī.”**

**Knowing everything, Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu was aware that Viśvarūpa had already passed away. A pretense of ignorance was necessary, however, so that He could go to South India and liberate the people there. (***Cc. Madhya* **7.7-13)**

Thus, Lord Caitanya is very anxious to go to South India to execute His mission. This took place shortly after Lord Caitanya accepted the renounced order of life and was beginning to spread the *saṅkīrtana* movement.

He was begging permission from all the devotees to allow Him to go. Although He is the independent Supreme Personality of Godhead, the Lord is so bound by the love of His devotees and cannot act independently from them. The first part of this chapter will describe how the Lord is so bound by the love of His devotees that He sometimes appears to act in contradictory ways.

It is this feature of the Lord’s pastimes that is very attractive. The Lord places Himself in a submissive role before His devotees. In this particular case, He is asking for their permission to go to South India to preach. The Lord feels that unless they give Him their permission, then He cannot leave. This is how dependent the Lord is on His devotees.

Elsewhere it is described how Lord Caitanya had asked for permission to go to Vṛndāvana many times. When He wanted to go to Vṛndāvana, He went to some of His intimate associates such as Sārvabhauma Bhattācārya and Rāmānanda Rāya. He asked for their permission: “I am very anxious to go to Vṛndāvana, and I am asking you to please give Me permission to go.” However, they presented various excuses for why He should not leave.

This is the kind of loving exchange that the Lord creates with His devotees. He binds them in such loving thoughts, and then the devotees also bind the Lord with their love. This is the whole meaning of reciprocal exchanges between the devotees and the Lord. Although Lord Caitanya appeared and displayed these pastimes over 500 years ago, these pastimes are eternally taking place in the spiritual world. Therefore, the Lord’s eternal associates are always experiencing loving reciprocal exchanges with the Supreme Lord. When the Lord descends to this world, He specifically descends to display loving exchanges. One such exchange is being described in this pastime.

The Lord was approaching many of His devotees and asking for their permission to go to South India. He said that He wanted to go to search out Viśvarūpa, His elder brother.

Many years prior, Viśvarūpa had accepted the renounced order of life, and thus caused great pain and separation in the hearts of Jagannātha Miśra and Śacīmātā. However, their pains of separation for Viśvarūpa were relieved because they had their young son, Nimai, who became the sole source of life for them. His every action was a source of their satisfaction.

In fact, after Viśvarūpa had accepted the renounced order of life, Nimai was still a young child, and Jagannātha Miśra became very anxious, thinking that if Nimai were to become a scholar and study like His elder brother, Viśvarūpa, then He might accept the renounced order like His brother. Jagannātha Miśra thought, “If Nimai does that, then our lives will be finished.”

Thus, one day Jagannātha Miśra said to Śacīmātā, “I think we should tell our son to stop studying. If He continues to study, He may come to learn that this material world is false and illusory, and He may want to give it up in the same way that His brother did.” There was some discussion on this matter between Jagannātha Miśra and Śacīmātā. Jagannātha Miśra was offering the argument that Nimai should give up His studies so that He can stay at home and remain a householder. But Śacīmātā was arguing, “If you keep your son a fool, then what woman will want to marry Him? He will never become a householder.”

Nonetheless, Jagannātha Miśra convinced her and told young Nimai, “From this day on, forget Your studies. There is no need for You to be concerned about studies. The Supreme Personality of Godhead, Śrī Kṛṣṇa, is maintaining everyone. Certainly He will maintain You. Just see, I am being maintained by the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Although I am not a wealthy *brāhmaṇa*—in fact, I am a very poor *brāhmaṇa*—still we have everything that we need in our home.”

However, Nimai was very attracted to studies. When Nimai began His studies, He was the top scholar amongst all of His classmates. Thus, when He was told by His father to give up His studies, He had nothing better to do and started getting into mischief. It was at that point that His mischievous pastimes were fully manifest. He warned His father, “If you think that I was mischievous before, then you will see what kind of mischief I will engage in now!”
