Accepting and rejecting, which are the first two characteristics, means that a devotee will always think of what is best for Kṛṣṇa’s service. An automatic impulse in the face of a decision is to calculate what is best for oneself, especially when it requires some immediate decision. Even on the material platform, there is accepting and rejecting. This is called *bhogatyāga*. *Bhoga* means we accept an idea for some enjoyment, and *tyāga* is when we realize that we are not satisfied by this opportunity of enjoyment and thus renounce it for something else.

Since Prahlāda Mahārāja was a fully surrendered devotee of the Lord, the Lord was very much inclined to bestow a benediction upon him. However, Prahlāda Mahārāja, being so much absorbed in devotional service to the Lord and fully satisfied by that service, asked nothing for himself.

Most materialistic people, when given the opportunity to receive a benediction from the Lord, immediately think that it is a great opportunity to receive something great for themselves.

In one prayer, Prahlāda Mahārāja very spontaneously said, “I see that there are many saintly personalities, but they are only interested in their own salvation. Not caring for big cities or towns, they go to the Himālayas or the forest to observe vows of silence and to meditate. They are not interested in delivering others. But for me, O Lord, I cannot possibly think of liberation for myself, leaving aside all these poor fools and rascals, because I know devotional service to Your lotus feet is the only way by which they can become happy. Therefore, I ask that You give me the opportunity to let me bring them to take shelter at Your lotus feet.”

Prahlāda Mahārāja was fully satisfied with the opportunity to render devotional service. Being fully surrendered, the devotee therefore accepts everything favorable for executing devotional service, even if it means he has to stay in the material world birth after birth.

Prahlāda Mahārāja was not thinking about the fact that he might have to stay in the material world birth after birth. He was only thinking about favorable devotional service to the Lord. He thus said, “How can I think about liberation? I cannot think of liberation when I see all these fools and rascals suffering in the material world. You are very anxious to deliver all fallen conditioned souls.” A pure devotee of the Lord is thinking, “It is better that everyone goes back to You before I do, because this will simply increase Your pleasure.” Such a pure devotee thinks that he is completely surrendered and very insignificant.

There is an example of the servant of Caitanya Mahāprabhu, named Govinda, whose service was to massage the lotus feet of the Lord.

Lord Caitanya, completely exhausted, was lying down in front of the doorway and would not move. Govinda was standing outside and was very anxious to massage the lotus feet of the tired Lord. A devotee would not think of stepping over the Lord, as it would be a great offense to do so. Nevertheless, Govinda was so much absorbed in rendering service to his worshipable Lord that he stepped over Him and went into the room and began massaging the Lord’s lotus feet.

When the Lord awoke, He realized that Govinda was there and had been massaging His feet for a long time. He asked Govinda, “How long have you been here?” Govinda replied that it did not make any difference. But the Lord was thinking that he must be very hungry as he had been there for a long time. Yet Govinda was not thinking at all about leaving, because to leave and go to eat would mean he would have to step over the Lord. He could not possibly think of stepping over the Lord for the purpose of going to eat; although, for the purpose of massaging the lotus feet of the Lord, he was willing to accept the reactions for stepping over Him.

This is another example of accepting everything favorable for devotional service. A devotee is always very anxious to satisfy the Lord, and therefore does not get absorbed in this calculation of accepting and rejecting on the basis of sense gratification. This *bhoga-tyāga,* accepting and rejecting on the basis of sense gratification, is always the cause of great suffering in the material world. After feeling a sense of fulfillment from having accepted something, it may appear that we are experiencing some enjoyment, but that enjoyment is the cause of bondage to the material world. After the enjoyment, there is suffering. This is the nature of the material calculation of accepting and rejecting.

Accepting and rejecting has to also be done on the spiritual platform. The accepting and rejecting for devotional service is not the same type of accepting and rejecting. A devotee accepts favorable things—those that are pleasing to Kṛṣṇa. The devotee’s standard for activity is: “What does the Lord want?”

Thus, a surrendered devotee, just like the two examples of Prahlāda Mahārāja and Govinda, is always completely absorbed in thinking of what is best for the service of the Lord. Being absorbed in this mood of full surrender, the third principle, which is that the devotee has firm conviction that the Lord will protect him, becomes manifest. The Lord gives His assurance of such protection, and therefore, whether a devotee is in heaven or hell, he never fears any condition of life. However, a devotee will never pray for his own protection.

Bhaktivinoda prays, “Whatever You wish—if You wish to protect me or slay me—it is Your desire, but You are always my master and I am always Your servant. Even if You slay me, I feel completely protected.”

The Lord will always protect the honor and the words of His devotee. For instance, Indra thought that he had to kill the child who had come from the evil seed of Hiraṇyakaśipu. He feared the child would be a great threat to the demigods. However, Prahlāda Mahārāja was protected because Nārada Muni had said, “No one can slay the child within that womb because he is a great devotee of the Lord.” Thus, one reason for the appearance of Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva was to protect the words of Nārada Muni.

Prahlāda Mahārāja had prayed to the Lord for constant remembrance of Him and not for protection. Constantly chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa, Prahlāda Mahārāja felt that his firm conviction was his protection. When a devotee prays for protection, he prays for the opportunity to always remember Kṛṣṇa and never forget Him for even a moment. This is described as the most important principle.

*smartavyaḥ satataṁ viṣṇur*  
*vismartavyo na jātucit*  
*sarve vidhi-niṣedhāḥ syur*  
*etayor eva kiṅkarāḥ*

“Kṛṣṇa is the origin of Lord Viṣṇu. He should always be remembered and never forgotten at any time. All the rules and prohibitions mentioned in the *śāstras* should be the servants of these two principles.” (*Padma Purāṇa*)

Whether a devotee is in heaven or hell, it does not make any difference to him because he is fully satisfied by being in constant remembrance of the Lord. Prahlāda Mahārāja is always remembering the Lord by always glorifying Him, always chanting the holy names of the Lord, and always praying to give Kṛṣṇa consciousness to others.

It is thus described in *Madhya-līlā* (22.100) that another principle of surrender is that the devotee must be fully convinced that Kṛṣṇa will always give him protection. No one else can offer such protection.

This kind of faith is different from the faith of the impersonalists who want to merge into the Brahman effulgence in order to benefit by the cessation of repeated birth and death. The impersonalists want liberation, thinking that that would be protection. Śrīla Prabhupāda explains that it is actually not protection at all. But since a devotee always wants to be engaged in the Lord’s service, Kṛṣṇa is merciful and gives him all protection from the dangers found on the path of devotional service.

Kṛṣṇa says, “In this endeavor there is no loss or diminution, and a little advancement on this path can protect one from the most dangerous type of fear” (*Bg.* 2.40). The most dangerous type of fear is forgetting Kṛṣṇa. A non-devotee who is under the protection of the illusory energy forgets the service of the Lord, as the Lord allows him to forget. Meanwhile, the Lord causes His devotee to forget material activities and the desire to enjoy the material world. He forgets because Kṛṣṇa has accepted him under the protection of His internal potency.

*mahātmānas tu māṁ pārtha*  
*daivīṁ prakṛtim āśritāḥ*  
*bhajanty ananya-manaso*  
*jñātvā bhūtādim avyayam*

“O son of Pṛthā, those who are not deluded, the great souls, are under the protection of the divine nature. They are fully engaged in devotional service because they know Me as the Supreme Personality of Godhead, original and inexhaustible.” (*Bg.* 9.13)

To be under the protection of His divine nature means to always remember His position in any condition of life— whether in happiness or distress. It does not make any difference where they are. Those situated in transcendental consciousness are completely aloof to material happiness and distress. Transcendental consciousness means always remembering Kṛṣṇa and always being fully satisfied rendering service to Kṛṣṇa. This is the firm faith that the devotee has in the Lord’s protection.

Therefore he is not afraid to make decisions for the service of the Lord. Kṛṣṇa says, “*mā śucaḥ*”—do not fear. “Abandon all varieties of religion and just surrender unto Me.” (*Bg.* 18.66) A devotee who has this firm faith understands that surrender to Kṛṣṇa means to accept everything favorable to satisfy Kṛṣṇa.

A devotee should accept Kṛṣṇa as his supreme maintainer. Kṛṣṇa is not only protecting but also maintaining one. He is giving everything one needs to maintain his body and soul together. One’s primary duty is to serve the Lord and to be maintained in this devotional service. When we think of maintenance, we always think of bodily maintenance, but on the spiritual platform, Kṛṣṇa is maintaining His devotees by engaging them in loving service to Him. A devotee feels fully satisfied with the service itself, and therefore always feels maintained by Kṛṣṇa. Thus, he favorably accepts whatever Kṛṣṇa gives him to use in His service, and if Kṛṣṇa does not give, he does not need.

The fifth item is self-surrender. This means remembering that one’s activities and desires are not independent. The devotee is completely dependent on Kṛṣṇa, and acts and thinks as Kṛṣṇa desires.

Śrīla Prabhupāda gives the example that the devotee always thinks himself to be an animal of a master. Just as the animal is always dependent upon what the master gives and whatever decision he makes, similarly, the devotee is always dependent upon Kṛṣṇa’s desire. He never tries to become independent from the Lord’s desire. Therefore, when the devotee always feels completely dependent upon the Lord, the Lord is very anxious to protect such a devotee.

It is described in the last division that the devotee is meek and humble. This quality of humility is a very important characteristic of a devotee. Humility is defined by Śrīla Prabhupāda in the *Bhagavad-gītā* as not being interested in honor for oneself. Generally in the material world, one is always looking for some honor. However, one who is genuinely humble is neither motivated by honor nor is he looking for it.

A materialistic person thinks, “If I do something great, I will become recognized. When I get this recognition then it will be very satisfying for me.”

However, a devotee does not think that by performing an activity he will become recognized by others. A devotee is always anxious to satisfy Kṛṣṇa, and he is much more interested in becoming recognized directly by Kṛṣṇa.

Kṛṣṇa does not recognize a show or a grand performance. Kṛṣṇa sees what is in the heart of a devotee and recognizes his devotion. He recognizes when a devotee is genuinely humble and when he does not want this recognition. Kṛṣṇa is in the heart and knows every desire that is there even better than we do. He knows what motivates us and sees everything perfectly clearly.

*vedāhaṁ samatītāni*  
*vartamānāni cārjuna*  
*bhaviṣyāṇi ca bhūtāni*  
*māṁ tu veda na kaścana*

“O Arjuna, as the Supreme Personality of Godhead, I know everything that has happened in the past, all that is happening in the present, and all things that are yet to come. I also know all living entities; but Me no one knows.” (*Bg.* 7.26)

Kṛṣṇa gives everyone what he wants. When He sees that one is motivated by honor, then He will give honor. However, he does not get Kṛṣṇa because he desires honor. This honor will not capture the attention of Kṛṣṇa. When Kṛṣṇa sees that a devotee does not want honor, He glorifies him. Kṛṣṇa wants to show off this quality of His devotee—not wanting personal honor.

**Madhya-līlā: 22.100**

**Ottawa—December 12, 1999**

**The six divisions of surrender are the acceptance of those things favorable to devotional service, the rejection of unfavorable things, the conviction that Kṛṣṇa will give protection, the acceptance of the Lord as one’s guardian or master, full self-surrender, and humility.**

Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura explains in one of his songs that for those who participate in this six-fold practice of surrender, taking refuge in the Lord, the youthful son of Nanda Mahārāja (Nanda-kumāra) is very eager to hear their prayers. In other words, the Lord is very much inclined to reciprocate with this process of surrender.

Kṛṣṇa says in the *Bhagavad-gītā* (4.11):

*ye yathā māṁ prapadyante*  
*tāṁs tathaiva bhajāmy aham.*

“As they surrender unto Me, I reward them accordingly. Everyone follows My path in all respects, O son of Pṛthā.”

For those who manifest these qualities of surrender, Kṛṣṇa is very eager to hear their prayers and reciprocate with their innermost desires.

Prabhupāda quotes from the *Bhagavad-gītā (*15.15):

*sarvasya cāhaṁ hṛdi sanniviṣṭo*  
*mattaḥ smṛtir jñānam apohanaṁ ca.*

“I am seated in everyone’s heart, and from Me come remembrance, knowledge and forgetfulness.”

The Lord knows everything that is going on within the heart of the living entity. He knows their past, their present, and all things that are going to come. He also knows the purpose and the intention of the living entities.

Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī explains in the *Bhakti-rasāmṛtasindhu* that accepting those things favorable for devotional service is one of the characteristics of *uttamā bhakti*:

*anyābhilāṣitā-śūnyaṁ*  
*jñāna-karmādy-anāvṛtam*  
*ānukūlyena kṛṣṇānu*  
*śīlanaṁ bhaktir uttamā*

(BRS 1.1.11)

The word *ānukūlyena* means favorable, and thus Rūpa Gosvāmī says that a person should be engaged in devotional service with a favorable intention according to Kṛṣṇa’s desires. The symptoms of *uttamā bhakti,* pure devotional service, are that it must be performed without desire for material profit or gain. The entire *Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu* is based on this one verse of Rūpa Gosvāmī, which describes the characteristics of pure devotion.

Throughout the *Mahābhārata*, we see that there is the common theme of the obvious difference between the Pāṇḍavas and Duryodhana. The Pāṇḍavas were always completely inclined towards what was most pleasing to Kṛṣṇa. In all their activities, that was the final determinant. However, Duryodhana had a different mentality.

When Duryodhana and Arjuna went to appeal to Kṛṣṇa, He was lying down and Arjuna was at His feet and Duryodhana was at His head. Kṛṣṇa said that He would fulfill the request of that person whom He first saw. When He opened His eyes, He saw Arjuna first and gave him the first choice. Arjuna wanted Kṛṣṇa.

Duryodhana was thinking, “Kṛṣṇa has a whole army, but Arjuna is only asking for Kṛṣṇa, who has already made a vow that He will not fight in the battle! What benefit is having Kṛṣṇa? I will get His whole army! I am in a much better position with His army.”

Duryodhana was not inclined to be dependent upon the Lord. Even when Duryodhana was defeated and taken by the Dānavas, the Dānavas told him with assurance that on the strength of their health he would certainly win the battle. With these blessings, Duryodhana felt certain that he would be successful and was very much inclined to take part in the battle.

Just before the Dānavas gave their assurance of success, Duryodhana had been thinking of killing himself, because he had been so insulted by Arjuna completely defeating his whole army when they came to attack the kingdom of Virāṭa. But he received his last hope from the assurance of the Dānavas, and thought that he could be successful, although he was being told by Bhīṣma, Droṇa, Vidura, and even towards the end by Dhṛtarāṣṭra that it was not possible to be victorious in this battle. Still, he was oblivious to all good instructions, thinking, “What is the value of Kṛṣṇa’s protection?”

On the other hand, the Pāṇḍavas were faithful to Kṛṣṇa and always sought His protection. They always wanted to please the Lord.

Similarly, Arjuna’s life was almost threatened when he was being attacked by Droṇa, who had called upon a celestial weapon. Kṛṣṇa had to immediately jump out and protect him. He accepted the weapon on His chest, which immediately turned into a flower garland around His neck.

Arjuna was very disturbed. “Kṛṣṇa, You broke Your promise! Why did You do this!? People will insult You! They will be very concerned about Your integrity! I was standing here on the chariot prepared to fight; I had all weapons.”

Kṛṣṇa told him that this particular weapon had a benediction that anyone whom it hit would be killed. So Kṛṣṇa personally protected him. But Arjuna was only thinking of Kṛṣṇa.

In the *Bhagavad-gītā* (9.31), Kṛṣṇa says, “*kaunteya pratijānīhi, na me bhaktaḥ praṇaśyati.*” He tells Arjuna to declare boldly that His devotee will never be vanquished.

Being fully surrendered to the Lord, the devotee is always thinking of how to please the Lord in everything he does. A devotee’s criterion for rejecting something is if it is not favorable for devotional service; if it is, then he will accept it for the service of the Lord.

To be able to think this way about accepting and rejecting, one also has to include the other processes of surrender. One has to have firm faith that under all conditions, as long as he executes his duties faithfully in devotional service, Kṛṣṇa will certainly give him protection and His mercy.

Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura describes the characteristics of patience in devotional service. He says that the devotee continues to execute his service thinking, “Whether now, ten years from now, one hundred years from now, or in some future birth, I will one day get the Lord’s mercy.” He has firm faith that as long as he continues executing his duties faithfully, then he will one day obtain Kṛṣṇa’s mercy.

We must be patient in the execution of our devotional service and execute it in this consciousness. That means in all our duties—not only in chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa, but in all our work.

Chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa also includes the work that we perform. If we maintain this body for hearing and chanting the glories of the Lord, then whatever work we perform and whatever duties we carry out also become a part of our devotional service. Therefore, we should execute our duties in this consciousness, with firm faith that by so doing we will be protected by the Lord. We are then capable of rejecting everything that is unfavorable for devotional service. We need the faith that if we reject that which is unfavorable, Kṛṣṇa will give us protection. Kṛṣṇa will give us all that we need to continue serving Him.

In *Bhagavad-gītā* (9.22), it is stated:

*ananyāś cintayanto māṁ*  
*ye janāḥ paryupāsate*  
*teṣāṁ nityābhiyuktānāṁ*  
*yoga-kṣemaṁ vahāmy aham*

Kṛṣṇa says that for those who are always meditating upon Him and endeavoring to please Him, He preserves what they have and carries what they lack. Because a devotee renders devotional service with this faith, Kṛṣṇa will certainly hear his prayer. The youthful son of Nanda Mahārāja hears the prayers of anyone who takes refuge in Him through the sixfold process of surrender. Not only does He hear it, but He fully reciprocates with the devotee.

Śrīla Prabhupāda says:

No one else can actually give one protection, and being firmly convinced of this is called faith. This kind of faith is different from the faith of an impersonalist who wants to merge into the Brahman effulgence in order to benefit by cessation of repeated birth and death. A devotee wants to remain always in the Lord’s service. (*Cc. Madhya* 22.100, Purport)

Prabhupāda would always speak very strongly against impersonalism. In *Renunciation Through Wisdom,* Prabhupāda speaks out against Dr. Radhakrishnan’s interpretation of *Bhagavad-gītā.* Dr. Radhakrishnan explains that when Kṛṣṇa says, “Surrender to Me,” it means to surrender to the impersonal aspect of Kṛṣṇa.

Prabhupāda says, “How can one surrender to something that is impersonal? It is not possible. How can one surrender to the impersonal Brahman? There is no surrender.”

Surrender means to surrender desires. There is no surrender to Brahman. Prabhupāda says if one does not surrender to Kṛṣṇa, but thinks that surrendering to Brahman is surrender, then he will find himself surrendering to family, to wife, and to society. Surrender must be done unto a person, but there is nobody to surrender to in the impersonal Brahman. Therefore, it is a misinterpretation to think that the all-pervasive impersonal Brahman feature of the Lord can give one protection. There is no real protection coming from the impersonal *brahmajyoti.*

*daivī hy eṣā guṇa-mayī*  
*mama māyā duratyayā*  
*mām eva ye prapadyante*  
*māyām etāṁ taranti te*

\[Kṛṣṇa says,\] “This divine energy of Mine, consisting of the three modes of material nature, is difficult to overcome. But those who have surrendered unto Me can easily cross beyond it.” (*Bg.* 7.14)

*sarva-dharmān parityajya*  
*mām ekaṁ śaraṇaṁ vraja*  
*ahaṁ tvāṁ sarva-pāpebhyo*  
*mokṣayiṣyāmi mā śucaḥ*

\[Kṛṣṇa also says,\] “Abandon all varieties of religion and just surrender unto Me. I shall deliver you from all sinful reactions. Do not fear.” (*Bg.* 18.66)

Lord Brahma says in his prayers to Lord Kṛṣṇa in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (10.2.32):

*ye ’nye ’ravindākṣa vimukta-māninas*  
*tvayy asta-bhāvād aviśuddha-buddhayaḥ*  
*āruhya kṛcchreṇa paraṁ padaṁ tataḥ*  
*patanty adho ’nādṛta-yuṣmad-aṅghrayaḥ*

“O lotus-eyed Lord, although nondevotees who accept severe austerities and penances to achieve the highest position may think themselves liberated, their intelligence is impure. They fall down from their position of imagined superiority because they have no regard for Your lotus feet.”

Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura describes the process of *śaraṇāgati* as a gradual process of surrender whereby one goes through its different stages.

We first have to accept the process of devotional service according to the rules and regulations of *bhakti-yoga*. By so doing, gradually we can come to the platform of being able to remember the Supreme Lord constantly. Engaging one hundred percent in devotional service is the perfectional stage.

Kṛṣṇa deals differently with the living entities according to their position. The living entity’s position is to either be under the protection of the illusory (external) energy of the Lord or under Kṛṣṇa’s personal (internal) protection. The illusory energy of the Lord causes the nondevotee to forget devotional service to the Lord. This is called *apohana*.

It is very difficult to remember Kṛṣṇa when one is always thinking himself to be the enjoyer. To think that Kṛṣṇa is the supreme enjoyer is completely incompatible with the desire of trying to be the enjoyer.

If one does not accept Kṛṣṇa as a person and wants to surrender to the unborn within, how can he be thinking of Kṛṣṇa’s desires? Instead, he thinks, “I will serve everybody.” He is only serving himself by trying to serve everybody. The best way to serve everybody is to serve their best interest, and that means to first serve Kṛṣṇa. Thus, one has to know what the Lord wants.

The devotee serves the Lord according to five basic relationships on the spiritual platform: neutrality, servitorship, friendship, parental affection, or conjugal love. Although Kṛṣṇa is God, He wants to experience these relationships. He does not always like to think of Himself as the Supreme Being. He likes to assume the relationship of being a servant of His devotees or a servant of His friends.

Similarly, Arjuna thought of Kṛṣṇa as a friend. When Kṛṣṇa would come to visit Arjuna, they would sit on the same bed together and talk just like friends. Kṛṣṇa would put His head on Arjuna’s lap, and Arjuna would put his head on Kṛṣṇa’s lap.

When Kṛṣṇa revealed His universal form to Arjuna on the battlefield of Kurukṣetra, Arjuna became so awestruck by seeing this feature of the Lord that he became very reverential towards Kṛṣṇa. He forgot his relationship of friendship and said, “Forgive me Kṛṣṇa. How could I possibly have been thinking of You as my friend, sitting on the same bed with You? Please forgive me for all that I have said and done.”

He lost his sense of friendship momentarily and was thinking in a reverential way towards the Lord. Arjuna no longer wanted to see that feature and just wanted to see his friend Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa therefore assumed His four-armed form and then His two-armed form. Thus, the relationship of friendship was re-established between the Lord and His devotee.

The Lord also wants to experience the parental relationship by being the son of His devotee. Being very anxious to satisfy the Lord, the devotee will satisfy the Lord in a parental relationship. A devotee thinks that the most important thing is his relationship with Kṛṣṇa. Even though the devotee may be aware of Kṛṣṇa’s opulence and greatness, he does not think it is as important as his personal relationship with Him.

Similarly, when mother Yaśodā was holding Kṛṣṇa in her lap and saw the entire cosmic manifestation in His mouth, she began thinking, “Who is this boy on my lap?”

Seeing that His mother had become awestruck, He did not want her to think that way any longer. He wanted her to return to her natural relationship of looking at Him as her son. Kṛṣṇa therefore covered her again so that she would think, “This is my son. Let me offer Him my breast-milk for His pleasure.” This is how the devotees render service to the Lord according to His desire in the spiritual world.

The topmost platform of devotional service, or the perfectional stage, is called *jñāna-śūnyā bhakti* in Sanskrit. There are different stages of knowledge, the first of which is called *pratyakṣa*. *Pratyakṣa* is when we see something through direct sense perception.

Then there is *parokṣa,* which means that we do not perceive it with our senses but may hear about it from someone else. By hearing from someone, we learn. *Parokṣa* may not necessarily give us perfect knowledge since we may hear from an imperfect source.

Then there is *aparokṣa*, which is compared to the impersonal Brahman realization of the Lord.

The next stage is *adhokṣaja*, where the Lord reveals the opulence of Vaikuṇṭha. This stage of knowledge is on the transcendental platform and descends only by the grace of the Lord. We may try to get it by our own efforts, but that alone is insufficient. We may even pray, cry, or do anything for it, but we can never know the *adhokṣaja* platform, unless Kṛṣṇa agrees to reveal it.

Beyond *adhokṣaja* is the stage called *aprākṛta*. This is *kevala-bhakti*. The stage of *aprākṛta* is the transcendental relationships of the spiritual world, in Goloka Vṛndāvana. *Aprākṛta* is described also as *jñāna-śūnyā bhakti*, which is the highest stage of knowledge. *Jñāna-śūnyā bhakti* means that stage of devotional service that is rendered without knowledge of the Lord’s being the Supreme Personality of Godhead and without knowledge of understanding how great oneself is.

Even though such devotees are on the highest stage of knowledge, they do not know it; they are not aware of their greatness. All they know is Kṛṣṇa as their son or Kṛṣṇa as their friend.

Although they may be aware of the opulence of the Lord, that is not the primary characteristic of their relationship with Him. They are therefore devoid of that knowledge of the greatness and opulence of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. They only think of the Lord according to their particular relationship and serve Him in that way. The Lord is very pleased to accept the service of the devotees, which is rendered in spontaneous devotional service.

It is described in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (10.9.21):

*nāyaṁ sukhāpo bhagavān*  
*dehināṁ gopikā-sutaḥ*  
*jñānināṁ cātma-bhūtānāṁ*  
*yathā bhaktimatām iha*

The greatness of Kṛṣṇa’s personality can only be understood by rendering spontaneous devotional service. Kṛṣṇa cannot be understood by studying the *Vedās* or by performing austerities or penances. None of these means can bring one to the platform of being able to render service to the Lord with complete unalloyed devotion, the highest stage of devotional service, which is rendered by the devotees who are residents of the spiritual world.

If one wants to attain this stage of pure unalloyed devotion, then one has to surrender. The process of surrender includes accepting those things that are favorable to pleasing Kṛṣṇa, rejecting anything that is unfavorable to pleasing Kṛṣṇa, being completely convinced that Kṛṣṇa will give one protection, accepting the Lord as one’s guardian and master, full self-surrender, and being meek and humble.

Devotees should always think themselves to be very insignificant, and should never become proud of their position, thinking themselves to be great or superior to others. A devotee always thinks himself to be the servant of the Supreme Lord and therefore a servant of every living being.

Prabhupāda was once giving a lecture to an audience at a university. He was speaking about the different classes of society and there was one man who was challenging him, thinking that Prabhupāda was speaking vainly when saying that the higher sections of society are the *brāhmaṇas,* and *brāhmaṇas* are preachers. The man thought that Prabhupāda was speaking about himself and said, “You think you are a *brāhmaṇa*? You think you are superior to everyone else?”

Prabhupāda said, “No, I am not thinking that way. I don’t think I’m a *brāhmaṇa*.”

The man said, “You were just speaking as if you were a first class man.”

Prabhupāda replied, “Oh no, I’m not a first class man. I’m a fifth class man because I’m a servant of all four classes.” Prabhupāda was actually thinking this way out of his natural humility. He did not think himself to be on the topmost platform, but rather a servant of everyone else. That is real humility when one thinks himself the servant instead of the master.

Sometimes he may have to serve by giving instructions. Sometimes he may have to serve by giving chastisement. Sometimes he may have to serve by enlightening others. He may appear to be in the superior position because of his knowledge and surrender, but his internal consciousness is always that of humility. He is always surrendered to the Supreme Lord and to the instructions of the higher authorities, and is thus situated on the platform of surrender. In this way, he becomes freed from pride. The devotee’s being meek and humble is the sixth characteristic of surrender.

In Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura’s song, *Śaraṇāgati,* where he describes these different qualities of surrender, we see that he presents himself to be very lowly and fallen. He teaches us what the proper mentality to develop is in order to become free from pride and become situated on the proper platform of humility. We see that such great exalted Vaiṣṇavas as Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura think themselves to be very lowly and fallen living entities.

If we can give up our false pride and develop the quality of humility, and learn how to depend upon the Lord by surrendering to Him, He will become very eager to hear our prayers. He will certainly fulfill all our desires, ultimately bestowing upon us the greatest boon and the greatest benediction that one can possibly obtain in life, which is the gift of devotional service.

*\* Do those qualities develop altogether as you practice devotional service?*

If someone tries to develop one, then the others will naturally follow.

*\* Do they come naturally, or do we have to endeavor separately to develop these qualities?*

There must be surrender in order to develop the qualities. Prabhupāda used to quote the verse:

*yasyāsti bhaktir bhagavaty akiñcanā*  
*sarvair guṇais tatra samāsate surāḥ*

(SB 5.18.12)

All the qualities of the demigods, such as knowledge, religion, and renunciation, automatically manifest in one who is engaged in unalloyed devotional service to the Lord. Unalloyed devotional service means that it is free from *karma, jñāna,* and *yoga*.

The good qualities will automatically manifest in an unalloyed devotee. However, until one comes to that stage, he has to practice development of those qualities. It is not that he simply waits for the qualities to come and thinks that he can go on not practicing humility, tolerance, simplicity, and pridelessness.

Thus, the beginning stage of devotional service is devotional service in practice. Kṛṣṇa says in the *Bhagavad-gītā (*12.9):

*atha cittaṁ samādhātuṁ*  
*na śaknoṣi mayi sthiram*  
*abhyāsa-yogena tato*  
*mām icchāptuṁ dhanañjaya*

“If you cannot always fix your mind upon Me, then practice the regulative principles of devotional service. In this way, you will develop the desire to attain Me.”

Thus, we must practice. We may not be able to fully manifest the qualities of a devotee, but by our endeavor Kṛṣṇa helps us. A devotee tries to practice tolerance in the face of adversity by becoming equipoised in happiness and distress.

By depending on the Lord, the Pāṇḍavas were able to tolerate all kinds of adversity. Even when they were exiled and when there was an attempt made to kill them in the house of lac, they tolerated it.

We cannot expect for these qualities to manifest automatically without practice. If Kṛṣṇa sees the endeavor, then He helps.

*\* Should devotees be diplomatic?*

If devotees are *brāhmaṇas,* the brahminical quality is to be free from diplomacy. Part of being a *kṣatriya* is that he has to employ different types of diplomacy. However, the characteristic of a *brāhmaṇa* is that he is always very straightforward in his dealings. If he is straightforward, then he ultimately has to depend upon the Lord. Thus, we should all try to develop the brahminical qualities and be free from diplomacy.

*\* If we surrender everything, must we also surrender our good qualities?*

Surrendering does not mean that we give them up. Surrender means that we use them for Kṛṣṇa’s service. That is real surrender. Whatever good qualities we have, Kṛṣṇa is very eager to preserve those good qualities. We should use them. Real surrender or renunciation means using whatever we have for Kṛṣṇa.

*anāsaktasya viṣayān y*  
*athārham upayuñjataḥ*  
*nirbandhaḥ kṛṣṇa-sambandhe*  
*yuktaṁ vairāgyam ucyate*

“When one is not attached to anything, but at the same time accepts everything in relation to Kṛṣṇa, one is rightly situated above possessiveness. On the other hand, one who rejects everything without knowledge of its relationship to Kṛṣṇa is not as complete in his renunciation.” (*BRS* 1.2.255-256)

Whatever good qualities we have should be used for the service of the Lord. Qualities that are bad because they are self-centered can be dovetailed in the service of the Lord.

For example, a person may be very ambitious and is therefore always disturbing others and exploiting them. But that ambition should instead be used to please Kṛṣṇa. A bad quality can become a good quality when dovetailed for the service of the Lord.

We should not give up these qualities. That is an incomplete and imperfect understanding of renunciation. Complete understanding of surrender is that we use whatever we have for Kṛṣṇa.

*etāvaj janma-sāphalyaṁ*  
*dehinām iha dehiṣu*  
*prāṇair arthair dhiyā vācā*  
*śreya-ācaraṇaṁ sadā*

(SB 10.22.35)

Kṛṣṇa is giving the example of how the trees are serving everyone in Vṛndāvana by giving shade and fruit. He says that all living entities should use their *prāṇa* (life), their *artha* (wealth), their *dhiyā* (intelligence), and their *vācā* (words)— whatever they have—to perform welfare activities for the benefit of all living entities by propagating Kṛṣṇa consciousness, the highest welfare activity.

## THE SIXTY-ONE EXPLANATIONS OF THE ĀTMĀRĀMA VERSE

**Madhya-līlā: 24.95–98, 159-161**

**Boston—October 1998**

**Text 95**

*ārta, arthārthī,—dui sakāma-bhitare gaṇi*  
*jijñāsu, jñānī,—dui mokṣa-kāma māni*

**Materialistic devotees take to devotional service and worship Kṛṣṇa when they are distressed or in need of money. Those who are inquisitive to understand the supreme source of everything and those who are in search of knowledge are called transcendentalists, for they desire liberation from all material contamination.**

**Text 96**

*ei cāri sukṛti haya mahā-bhāgyavān*  
*tat-tat-kāmādi chāḍi’ haya śuddha-bhaktimān*

**Because they have a pious background, all four types of people are to be considered greatly fortunate. Such people gradually give up material desires and become pure devotees.**

**Text 97**

*sādhu-saṅga-kṛpā kimvā kṛṣṇera kṛpāya*  
*kāmādi ‘duḥsaṅga’ chāḍi’ śuddha-bhakti pāya*

**One is elevated to the platform of devotional life by the mercy of a Vaiṣṇava, the bona fide spiritual master, and by the special mercy of Kṛṣṇa. On that platform, one gives up all material desires and the association of unwanted people. Thus one is elevated to the platform of pure devotional service.**

**Text 98**

*sat-saṅgān mukta-duḥsaṅgo*  
*hātuṁ notsahate budhaḥ*  
*kīrtyamānaṁ yaśo yasya*  
*sakṛd ākarṇya rocanam*

**‘The intelligent, who have understood the Supreme Lord in the association of pure devotees and have become free from bad, materialistic association, can never avoid hearing the glories of the Lord, even though they have heard them only once.’**

**Purport**

(…) A pure devotee becomes attached to Kṛṣṇa by hearing the Lord’s glories. The Lord’s glories and the Lord Himself are identical. One has to be qualified to understand this Absolute Truth; therefore one should be given a chance to associate with a pure devotee. Our Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is meant for this purpose. We want to create pure devotees so that other people will benefit by their association. In this way the number of pure devotees increases. Professional preachers cannot create pure devotees. There are many professional preachers of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* who read this work to earn their livelihood. However, they cannot convert materialistic people to devotional service. Only a pure devotee can convert others to pure devotional service. It is therefore important for all the preachers in our Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement to first become pure devotees and follow the regulative principles, refraining from illicit sex, meat-eating, gambling and intoxication. They should regularly chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* on their beads, follow the devotional process, rise early in the morning, attend *maṅgala-ārati* and recite *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and the *Bhagavad-gītā* regularly. In this way, one can become purified and free from all material contamination. (…)

To make a show of devotional service will not help one. One must be a pure devotee following the devotional process; then one can convert others to devotional service. Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu practiced devotional service and preached (*āpani ācari’ bhakti karila pracāra*). If a preacher behaves properly in devotional service, he will be able to convert others. Otherwise, his preaching will have no effect.

This is Lord Caitanya’s explanation of the *ātmārāma* verse*.* This verse describes the characteristics of those who are *akāma* (desireless), and the necessity of associating with such *akāma* devotees. *Kṛṣṇa-bhakta niṣkāma, ataeva* *‘śānta’—*a devotee of Lord Kṛṣṇa is desireless.

There are three different categories of devotees: *akāma* (desireless), *mokṣa-kāma* (one who desires liberation), and *sarva-kāma* (those who desire material perfection).

In this chapter, Prabhupāda also quotes the following verse (*Cc. Madhya* 24.39): *bhukti-mukti-siddhi-sukha chāḍaya yāra gandhe.* A devotee has one desire, which is to please the Supreme Lord.

Prahlāda Mahārāja spoke a verse describing that when one takes the dust of a devotee, he becomes purified and freed from contamination. The next verse says *\[referring to SB* *5.12.13\]*, “Who are these pure devotees? In an assembly of pure devotees, there is no question or discussion of sociology. In such an assembly, the discussion is only of the qualities, forms, and pastimes of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. He is praised and worshipped with great attention. By hearing such topics respectfully, even a person who wants to merge into the impersonal Brahman (the impersonal effulgence of the Lord), will give up these desires and gradually become attached to rendering service to the Supreme Lord.”

Similarly, Jaḍa Bharata says, “Pure devotees do not engage in topics of discussion about activities that are connected to the material body. Therefore, they do not engage in discussions about politics or sociology, which are both directly connected to the body. When pure devotees of the Lord get together, they are simply interested in describing the qualities, forms, and pastimes of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.” In the Tenth Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* Śukadeva Gosvāmī describes the characteristics of a pure devotee of the Lord. He says, “*Paramahaṁsas* who have accepted the essence of life are attached to Lord Kṛṣṇa within the core of their heart; He is the aim of their life. It is their nature to talk only about Kṛṣṇa at every moment, just as if such topics were newer and newer.”

This purport (*Madhya* 24.98) is explaining that the method by which one becomes freed from material contamination is by exposing himself to the association of those who have an attraction to hearing and chanting about Kṛṣṇa.

Similarly, in the Fourth Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* Nārada Muni explains that pure devotees are following the rules and regulations in pure consciousness and with great eagerness are hearing and chanting the pastimes of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. In the association of such devotees, this constant flow of nectar, which is exactly like the waves of a river, will cause one to lose all attraction to the material necessities of life. One will become immune to all illusions and lamentations. Nārada Muni describes that that is what happens to somebody who goes to a place where a pure devotee resides.

Jaḍa Bharata said, “Wherever there is an assembly of pure devotees, there is no question of sitting down and talking about mundane topics.” It is like the constant flow of a river. If one goes to the banks of the river and submerges himself within it, the river is still continuously flowing, and he is always subjected to that constant flow.

In the same way, Nārada Muni describes that place where pure devotees are residing. They are only interested in discussing the form, pastimes, and qualities of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, as if such topics were ever-fresh. They are always churning these descriptions of the characteristics of the Lord. Therefore, if one goes to that place—just as Nārada Muni gives the example of the waves of the river—and hears the continuous flow of nectar, he will lose his attraction for all the necessities of life, such as hunger and thirst. He will become immune to all kinds of illusion, lamentation, and confusion. Thus, there are many examples in the *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam* of persons who go to places where pure devotees are assembled, discussing the glories of the Supreme Lord.

Nārada Muni describes that in a previous life he had come into the association of great devotees (*bhaktivedāntas*) and heard the descriptions of the characteristics, pastimes, and form of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. He said, “Only once I took their remnants of foodstuffs. By having taken the remnants and having heard their descriptions, my desire to hear about Kṛṣṇa was completely awakened within my heart.” Associating with such devotees results in one wanting to hear.

Prabhupāda says (*Cc. Madhya* 24.98, Purport):

Our Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is meant for this purpose. We want to create pure devotees so that other people will benefit by their association. In this way the number of pure devotees increases. Professional preachers cannot create pure devotees.

There is a verse in the Fourth Canto of *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam*, which says that the story of Dhruva Mahārāja must be recited by those who have no interest in any kind of remuneration. In the purport to that verse, Prabhupāda humorously says, “Professional *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* reciters are expert in putting out the blazing fire in their bellies; however, they cannot put out the blazing fire of material existence.”

The professional *Bhāgavatam* reciters sit and read *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* and people come and hear. However, what is the interest of the professional *Bhāgavatam* reciter? His interest is to accumulate some wealth, adoration, or distinction. His interest is something other than glorifying the Supreme Lord; his motivation is for personal gain. Someone who is motivated by personal gain cannot make others Kṛṣṇa conscious; he cannot put out the fire of material existence that is blazing in the heart of conditioned souls. At best, he can only put out the blazing fire within his belly, because he is simply interested in accumulating some wealth to maintain the material body by filling his stomach. Thus, although he may speak of topics that appear to be related to Kṛṣṇa, such topics do not have any effect because he is interested in maintaining the material body.

Prabhupāda says (*Cc. Madhya* 24.98, Purport), “If a preacher behaves properly in devotional service, he will be able to convert others. Otherwise, his preaching will have no effect.” In other words, although the audience will hear, their heart will not stimulate them to think, “I want to increase my desire to render devotional service to Kṛṣṇa.” That is why it is described in *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* that pure love for Kṛṣṇa is eternally situated in the heart of every living being. When a living entity hears about Kṛṣṇa from the right source, then that pure love becomes naturally awakened. The “right source” means that the cleansing process will be invoked and the purification process will take place. The heart will gradually become pure. That is the result of associating with that which is pure; however, it may not manifest immediately.

In *Caitanya-bhāgavata,* there are many verses that say, “If one hears the pastime of, for example, Murari Gupta and how he was carrying Lord Caitanya upon his shoulders, certainly he will also become an associate of the Lord and will one day see the Supreme Personality of Godhead.”

Thus, in many places, it is described, “If one simply hears, he will certainly see Kṛṣṇa face to face.”

One might say, “I am hearing; why am I not seeing Kṛṣṇa face-to-face? Is this process an advertising ploy to convince people to hear? There is no real effect; I am not seeing Kṛṣṇa.”

When Vṛndāvana dāsa Ṭhākura says, “If one hears this pastime, certainly he will see Lord Caitanya face to face,” he means that by hearing, gradually one’s heart will become purified and cleansed, and certainly he will see Kṛṣṇa faceto-face.

Something happens when one hears from the right source. It may not be directly perceivable, like seeing Kṛṣṇa face-to-face, but actually there is some cleansing taking place within the heart. The more one subjects his heart to the cleansing process, gradually he gets more and more direct perception of Kṛṣṇa. His attraction to devotional service becomes awakened, and he loses attraction to material things. This is the result of association with devotees. The more he hears, the more the heart becomes clean.

Similarly, Prabhupāda says (*Cc. Madhya* 24.98, Purport), “To make a show of devotional service will not help one.” There are many different ways that one can make a show of devotional service.

Sometimes a person may think, “I want to live in the temple and I have been doing so because there are certain comforts that I can get fulfilled.” If one has a comfortable situation in a temple, he will feel that he does not have to work so hard; he will always be looking for the minimum amount of obligation just so that he can be satisfied and remain in the temple. In this connection, Prabhupāda says, “A show of devotional service will not help one.”

They are actually thinking, “I just want to stay in the temple so that I don’t have to go out and get a job. I don’t want to go home and live with my parents. I will therefore sit in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class, but I will not listen to it.” This is an example of making a show of devotional service; but actually, nothing happens. There is no cleaning taking place.

Another example is that of one particular leader at a temple who was not very inclined to take part in the morning program. It came to a point where he was going to lose his authority because devotees were gradually starting to revolt due to his absence. Some pressure was therefore put on him: “If he does not attend the morning program, then we are going to continue our revolution.”

In these circumstances, a person may be thinking, “I am going to lose my authority unless I attend the morning program!” Thus, he may attend the morning program, but he is thinking, “I don’t want to lose control of everybody in the temple, therefore I have to attend the morning program.”

However, Śrīla Prabhupāda did not want the devotees to attend the morning program to make a show. Prabhupāda expected something to happen as a result of attending. He expected that a little attraction would start to awaken within the heart. Not making a show of devotional service means that one has to develop some taste.

In an assembly of pure devotees, there is no question of hearing material subjects such as politics and sociology. You do not go to *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class to discuss politics and sociology; you go to neither discuss it nor to hear it.

One should stick to the principle that is set down by the previous *ācāryas,* as described by Lord Caitanya to Devānanda Paṇḍita.

Devānanda Paṇḍita asked Lord Caitanya, “I speak from a book called *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, but *how* should I speak?”

Lord Caitanya replied, “The beginning, middle, and end of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* are glorifying the devotional service of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.” That is the whole purpose of speaking from *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam—*to glorify devotional service. It is not to speak about politics, sociology, or mundane subject matters in the guise of *kṛṣṇa-kathā.* The conclusion must always be established. The purpose of speaking *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* is for one to come to its conclusion, which is devotional service.

However, wherever we are speaking from, we may speak a little bit about politics or sociology. Śrīla Prabhupāda also spoke about such subjects. When speaking about the *Mahābhārata,* he spoke about the intrigues of the Pāṇḍavas and Duryodhana—that was politics. Nevertheless, why did Śrīla Vyāsadeva speak about such subjects in the first place? It was ultimately to bring one to hearing Kṛṣṇa’s message of *Bhagavad-gītā,* to bring one to hearing Kṛṣṇa’s instructions, and gradually to increase one’s attraction to the pastimes of the Supreme Lord. Thus, one who knows the conclusion of the scriptures always leads everyone to the right conclusion, which is devotional service to the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

In order to please the Supreme Personality of Godhead, we should follow rules and regulations of devotional service, chant Hare Kṛṣṇa, and regularly hear the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and *Bhagavad-gītā*. In this way, one can become purified and free from all material contamination. Therefore, to make a show of devotional service will not help. One must become a pure devotee, following the devotional process, and then one can converse on devotional service.

Lord Caitanya describes to Rūpa Gosvāmī the characteristics of the devotional creeper. He described that the hearing process waters the creeper of devotional service. It is most important to hear, but sometimes unwanted creepers, such as creepers of material desires for enjoyment or liberation from the material world, grow along with the creeper of devotional service. The varieties of such unwanted creepers are unlimited (*Cc. Madhya* 19.159):

**Some unnecessary creepers growing with the** *bhakti* **creeper are the creepers of behavior unacceptable for those trying to attain perfection; diplomatic behavior; animalkilling; mundane profiteering; mundane adoration; and mundane importance. All these are unwanted creepers.**

**Purport**

There is a certain pattern of behavior prescribed for those actually trying to become perfect. In our Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement we advise our students not to eat meat, not to gamble, not to engage in illicit sex and not to indulge in intoxication. People who indulge in these activities can never become perfect; therefore these regulative principles are for those interested in becoming perfect and going back to Godhead.

Prabhupāda later describes that *kuṭīnāṭī,* diplomatic behavior, is an unwanted creeper. When one is watering a creeper of devotional service, sometimes the unwanted creepers grow along with it. Lord Caitanya explains that these creepers may look exactly like the devotional creeper.

Lord Caitanya says (*Cc. Madhya* 19.160):

**If one does not distinguish between the** *bhakti* **creeper and the other creepers, the sprinkling of water is misused because the other creepers are nourished while the** *bhakti* **creeper is curtailed.**

In other words, if one nourishes the other creepers, he will think, “I am becoming an advanced devotee because I am judging my advancement on externals. I am not checking to see what the motivations in my heart are. Why am I doing this? I am doing it because I like to have followers. I am doing it because I want adoration and distinction. I want to be recognized. I want some profit.” Therefore, we must distinguish between the *bhakti-latā* creeper and these other unwanted creepers. They will otherwise become nourished by chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa.

**As soon as an intelligent devotee sees an unwanted creeper growing beside the original creeper, he must cut it down instantly. Then the real creeper, the** *bhakti-latā***, grows nicely, returns home, back to Godhead, and seeks shelter under the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa.**

**Purport**

If one is misled by unwanted creepers and is victimized, he cannot make progress back to Godhead. Rather, he remains within the material world and engages in activities having nothing to do with pure devotional service. Such a person may be elevated to the higher planetary systems, but because he remains within the material world, he is subjected to the threefold material miseries. (*Cc. Madhya* 19.161)

One has to identify the unwanted creepers within the heart—faultfinding, *jīva-hiṁsana* (envy of other living entities), and *pūjā* (desire for worship or adoration). Moreover, those who are intelligent and have understood the Supreme Lord in the association of pure devotees become free from bad materialistic association and can never avoid hearing the glories of the Lord. Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead and Supersoul in the heart of every living being, cleanses the desire for material enjoyment within the heart of one who develops the urge to hear His name and pastimes.

To begin the process of identifying the creepers, we first have to realize what our desires are. Sometimes when devotees are not careful and meditate on their desires, that meditation actually chokes the determination for the process of devotional service, and they lose their attraction. Therefore, the most important process for cleaning our heart is hearing. Under all circumstances, whenever there is an opportunity, a devotee’s life should be molded in such a way as to always hear and chant about Kṛṣṇa.

We cannot immediately cut away the unwanted creepers. To mold our life in such a way means to always think of Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa must always be remembered and never forgotten. This is the purpose of all the rules and prohibitions that are given in the *śāstra.* Thus, attending *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class is meant for remembering Kṛṣṇa; honoring Kṛṣṇa *prasādam* is meant for thinking of Kṛṣṇa; speaking about Kṛṣṇa is meant for thinking of Kṛṣṇa; distributing Kṛṣṇa’s books is meant for thinking of Kṛṣṇa; worshipping the Deity is meant for thinking of Kṛṣṇa; and cleaning the temple is meant for thinking of Kṛṣṇa. Everything we do is meant for thinking of Kṛṣṇa.

If we mold our life in such a way, we are giving ourselves the best opportunity to hear about and remember Kṛṣṇa. We can then make a distinction of what is an unwanted creeper, and to what we should really be applying our life. Gradually by hearing about Kṛṣṇa, we will uproot the unwanted desires from within the heart.

Prabhupāda says that the business of a devotee in this institution is, from 5:00 in the morning until 10:00 at night, to mold their lives to hear about Kṛṣṇa. Although it may not be so easy, still, from whatever stage of devotional service we are in, we should never give up or abandon the process of hearing about Kṛṣṇa from the right source. Gradually, the core of the heart will become clean of whatever residual desires are there.

Similarly, Lord Kṛṣṇa told Nārada Muni, “By your meditation upon Me, whatever residual desires are left within the core of your heart will be burnt up.” Kṛṣṇa is acting from within the heart to help us in this way; we cannot cut down these unwanted creepers ourselves.

*naṣṭa-prāyeṣv abhadreṣu*  
*nityaṁ bhāgavata-sevayā*

“By regular attendance in classes on the *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam* and by rendering service to the pure devotee, all that is troublesome to the heart is almost completely destroyed, and loving service unto the Personality of Godhead, who is praised with transcendental songs, is established as an irrevocable fact.” (*SB* 1.2.18)

Furthermore, in one purport of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (7.14.4), Prabhupāda explains that one must hear every day— *abhīkṣṇam* (again and again). If he does not, then he is *gṛhamūḍha-dhī. Gṛha-mūḍha-dhī* means one who has lost sight of the goal of life.

If one does not have the opportunity to hear every day, then hopefully by great fortune one has the opportunity to associate with devotees and render service unto them. By serving advanced devotees, one develops an attraction for hearing and chanting.

Therefore, one should either hear regularly, or if he has the opportunity to associate with devotees, he should servethem. Of course, the best way to serve such devotees is to hear from them. When somebody hears about Kṛṣṇa from the right source, he naturally becomes inspired to serve, and gradually his heart becomes clean. However, one may not necessarily be hearing the holy name or the message of the *Bhāgavatam* from the right source.

Prabhupāda talks about that in the purport to *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam* (7.14.4). He says there are different *bhakti-latābījas.* One can get a *karma-bīja* or a *jñāna-bīja* depending upon whom he is associating with. However, if one wants to get the *bhakti-latā-bīja,* then he has to associate with a devotee who has the *bhakti,* the devotion.

Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura says, “One can chant, but it is simply the alphabet unless he is chanting purely—committing no offenses.” Other things will become nourished by this impure chanting for material gain such as name and fame.

*\* Can a devotee have desires?*

One may have desires, but they should be dovetailed. Dovetailed means (*SB* 2.3.10):

*akāmaḥ sarva-kāmo vā*  
*mokṣa-kāma udāra-dhīḥ*  
*tīvreṇa bhakti-yogena*  
*yajeta puruṣaṁ param*

Whether one has material desires, no material desires, is filled with material desires, or desires liberation, if he has broader intelligence, he must by all means worship the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Therefore, even if he is filled with material desires, or desires to serve the Lord in a particular way, he can dovetail his desires for the service of the Lord. However, dovetail means by the association of a devotee.

**Madhya-līlā: 24.227–252**

**Minsk—January 20, 2000**

**Text 227**

*nirgrantha-śabde kahe tabe ‘vyādha’, ‘nirdhana’*  
*sādhu-saṅge seha kare śrī-kṛṣṇa-bhajana*

**The word ‘***nirgrantha***,’ when combined with ‘***api***’ used in the sense of certainty, indicates a person who is a hunter by profession or who is very poor. Nonetheless, when such a person associates with a great saint like Nārada, he engages in Lord Kṛṣṇa’s devotional service.**

Lord Caitanya is explaining a verse from the *ŚrīmadBhāgavatam* in several different ways. The word *nirgrantha* is an adjective modifying the word *muni,* and it indicates association with a devotee. Lord Caitanya will now explain that a person who is a *nirgrantha* can become a devotee of the Lord.

In this chapter of *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* Lord Caitanya personally tells the story of Mṛgāri, the hunter, to elaborate how somebody who is even a hunter can become a devotee. Lord Caitanya speaks as follows:

**The words ‘***kṛṣṇārāmaś ca***’ refer to one who takes pleasure in thinking of Kṛṣṇa. Even though such a person may be a hunter, he is still worshipable and is the best of devotees. I shall now narrate the story of how a hunter became a great devotee by the association of such** **an exalted personality as Nārada Muni. From this story, one can understand the greatness of association with pure devotees.**

**Once upon a time the great saint Nārada, after visiting Lord Nārāyaṇa in the Vaikuṇṭhas, went to Prayāga to bathe at the confluence of three rivers—the Ganges, Yamunā and Sarasvatī. Nārada Muni saw that a deer was lying on the path through the forest and that it was pierced by an arrow. It had broken legs and was twisting due to much pain. Farther ahead, Nārada Muni saw a boar pierced by an arrow. Its legs were also broken, and it was twisting in pain. When he went farther, he saw a rabbit that was also suffering. Nārada Muni was greatly pained at heart to see living entities suffer so. When Nārada Muni advanced farther, he saw a hunter behind a tree. This hunter was holding arrows, and he was ready to kill more animals. The hunter’s body was blackish. He had reddish eyes, and he appeared fierce. It was as if the superintendent of death, Yamarāja, were standing there with a bow and arrows in his hands.**

**When Nārada Muni left the forest path and went to the hunter, all the animals immediately saw him and fled. When all the animals fled, the hunter wanted to chastise Nārada with abusive language, but due to Nārada’s presence, he could not utter anything abusive.**

**The hunter addressed Nārada Muni: ‘O** *Gosvāmī***! O great saintly person! Why have you left the general path through the forest to come to me? Simply by seeing you, all the animals I was hunting have now fled.’**

**Nārada Muni replied, ‘Leaving the path, I have come to you to settle a doubt that is in my mind. I was wondering whether all the boars and other animals that are half-killed belong to you.’**

**The hunter replied, ‘Yes, what you are saying is so.’ Nārada Muni then inquired, ‘Why did you not kill** **the animals completely? Why did you half-kill them by** **piercing their bodies with arrows?’**

**The hunter replied, ‘My dear saintly person, my name is Mṛgāri, enemy of animals. My father taught me to kill them in that way. When I see half-killed animals suffer, I feel great pleasure.’**

**Nārada Muni then told the hunter, ‘I have one thing to beg of you.**

**The hunter replied, ‘You may take whatever animals or anything else you would like. I have many skins, if you would like them. I shall give you either a deerskin or a tiger skin.’**

**Nārada Muni said, ‘I do not want any of the skins. I am asking only one thing from you in charity. I beg you that from this day on you will kill animals completely and not leave them half-dead.’**

**The hunter replied, ‘My dear sir, what are you asking of me? What is wrong with the animals’ lying there half-killed? Will you please explain this to me?’ (***Cc. Madhya* **24.228–248)**

Someone might ask, “Why did Nārada Muni not just ask, ‘From this day, stop killing animals altogether’?” However, Nārada Muni is very expert. He knows the mentality of everyone that he preaches to, and therefore he preaches to them in such a way that he does not force them to immediately give up their sinful activities. He instructs them according to their mentality.

Similarly, there is the example of his dealings with Dhruva Mahārāja. Dhruva Mahārāja was certainly not of the same mentality as Mṛgāri; nevertheless, it is the same principle. Nārada Muni could have said to Dhruva, “Why are you anxious for your kingdom? Give it up.” But he knew that Dhruva Mahārāja had a strong desire and instead instructed him according to his attachments, so that by following and hearing those instructions, gradually he could give them up.

Thus, Nārada Muni is saying here, “If you want to kill animals, then why are you leaving them half-dead? Why do you not just kill them completely?” However, Mṛgāri was deriving pleasure from seeing them suffer and being in pain.

**Nārada Muni replied, ‘If you leave the animals halfdead, you are purposefully giving them pain. Therefore you will have to suffer in retaliation.’ (***Cc. Madhya* **24.249)**

Nārada Muni’s first point is that it is one thing to be a hunter, but it is another thing to purposely give pain to others. If you try to do something to intentionally give pain to others, then there will be some reaction for that; you will have to suffer. That proportion of pain that you give to others, the same pain will come back to you.

In fact, there is a very common saying, “What goes around comes around.” It is like that with any pleasure in the material world. Of course, people who are blind to the laws of *karma* will not understand that there is a price to pay for sense gratification, and therefore, this is Nārada Muni’s first instruction to Mṛgāri.

**Nārada Muni continued, ‘My dear hunter, your business is killing animals. That is a slight offense on your part. But when you consciously give them unnecessary pain by leaving them half-dead, you incur very great sins.’ Nārada Muni continued, ‘All the animals that you have killed and given unnecessary pain will kill you one after the other in your next life and in life after life.’ In this way, through the association of the great sage Nārada Muni, the hunter was a little convinced of his sinful activity. He therefore became somewhat afraid due to his offenses. (***Cc. Madhya* **24.250–252)**

**Purport**

(...) If we simply surrender unto Kṛṣṇa’s lotus feet and take this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement seriously, we can be freed from the karma incurred by sin. If we are intelligent enough, we shall engage in the loving service of the Lord. Our lives will then be successful, and we will not have to suffer like the hunter, life after life. By killing animals, not only will we be bereft of the human form, but we will have to take an animal form and somehow or other be killed by the same type of animal we have killed. This is the law of nature. The Sanskrit word *māṁsa* means “meat.” It is said, *māṁ saḥ khadati iti māṁsaḥ*: “I am now eating the flesh of an animal that will some day in the future be eating my flesh.”

Earlier in the purport, Prabhupāda explains that if persons strictly follow these principles and chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mantra*, it will make them fit to become devotees. Then when they speak to sinful people about the teachings of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, the people will be affected and will take these instructions to heart.

In this purport and in many other references from Prabhupāda’s books, Prabhupāda emphasizes the point of preaching by example. One of the symptoms of the age of Kali is that people have disturbed minds, and therefore this is called the age of quarrel. It is also called the age of hypocrisy, which means that people say one thing but behave in a different way, and people become religionists simply for name and fame.

In *Caitanya-candrāmṛta,* which was written in the form of a drama, it describes the different *āśramas* in this age. It states that the householders will be attached to wealth, and the only difference between the *sannyāsīs* and the other three *āśramas* is that they will have a different dress. It also describes some of the characteristics of Kali-yuga and the Māyāvādīs. It points out that the Māyāvādīs, people who perform austerities (ascetics and sages), and the so-called religionists will all assume different designations for self-motivated purposes. It is not because they want to follow the principles of scripture; rather, they accepted the specific designations to fulfill some own personal ambition. Therefore, it is explained that the only person we can possibly find who is free from hypocrisy is a devotee of Lord Viṣṇu. Otherwise, it is very difficult to find.

*kṛṣṇa-bhakta—niṣkāma, ataeva ‘śānta’*  
*bhukti-mukti-siddhi-kāmī—sakali ‘aśānta’*

(Cc. Madhya 19.149)

A devotee of the Lord is *niṣkāma*, desireless, and therefore he can be peaceful. Whereas, those who are *bhuktīs, muktīs,* or *siddhīs,* because they still have lusty desires (*sakali* *‘aśānta’),* can never be peaceful. Thus, only a devotee can be genuinely peaceful, especially in this age of Kali.

As was previously mentioned, one of the symptoms of the age of Kali is that of hypocrisy. People will say so many different things, but they will not behave according to what they say. They may preach some religious doctrine, but they do it for self-motivated purposes. When it is done for selfmotivated purposes, people think, “How can I follow this process?” Because people’s minds are always disturbed in Kali-yuga*,* they are always looking for some fault. That is one of the reasons why there is so much tension in Kali-yuga*,* and because everyone is always looking for some fault, as soon as they see somebody preaching something, they think, “This person does not practice what they preach. They found some fault, and therefore they cannot be effective in their preaching.” They may temporarily be effective and expert in creating a facade. That is one quality of such expert pseudoreligionists. They are always busy in creating an image. However, they are so much absorbed in the image that they forget the essence.

People may sometimes think that successful preaching means that the preacher is liked. The goal of preaching is not just getting people to like us. People will naturally like devotees because they are free from hypocrisy; a devotee practices what he preaches. Of course, very envious people will always try to find fault—even in great, exalted devotees, in whom the fault does not exist.

For example, Rāmacandra Khān was very envious of Haridāsa Ṭhākura and tried to find some fault in him, even though there was no fault there. If somebody is serving the Lord without hypocrisy, it is difficult to find fault.

There is also a video about Prabhupāda entitled, *Your Ever Well-Wisher*. In that video, there is a very wealthy industrialist in India who speaks about how he had met Prabhupāda in the 1970’s. He was always trying to find some fault. He was honest about this, and would say, “I am always trying to find some fault with Prabhupāda. Every time, I look for a fault so that I will not have to surrender to him. But actually, I did not find any fault!” As much as he tried, he could not find any fault.

This is what association with a devotee of the Lord is. Devotees have to become freed from fault. People may try to find faults, but they should actually be above all such hypocrisy. Therefore, if a devotee practices what he preaches, those who are looking for something genuine will be attracted.

Of course, if someone is not looking for something genuine, he will get something which is not genuine. Prabhupāda gave the following example: “When you are selling diamonds, not everyone wants to pay the price.” If you want the genuine thing, you have to pay some price for it. Diamonds are not cheap.

Prabhupāda says that if the devotees are strict in following the four regulative principles, they will “become purified by following the four principles and chanting the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā*-*mantra*. This will make them fit to become Vaiṣṇavas. Then, when they speak to sinful people about the teachings of this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, people will be affected” by it (*Cc. Madhya* 24.252, Purport).

In other words, people will be affected by what is going on. We may attract people by so many external reasons, but if we really want to be effective in our preaching, we also have to practice our Kṛṣṇa consciousness internally.

Devotees, amongst themselves, derive pleasure in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. When we go out to preach the glories of the *saṅkīrtana* movement, people see us and think, “What are the devotees doing amongst themselves?” When they see that the devotees are happy associating with each other, deriving pleasure from that association, it is a very strong message. That message speaks for itself. It is more powerful than any other image you can try to create.

The second *śloka* of the *bhajana “Godruma-CandraBhajana-Upadeśa,”* speaks about *grāmya-kathā*. When the devotees are relishing the taste of *hari-kathā* in the association of each other, it is very attractive and a very powerful way to preach. We do not have to be very expert in public relations, or spend thousands and thousands of dollars to make everybody think something great about this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. People will not have to be led to think something by some artificial advertising. People will naturally conclude it when they see it.

Prabhupāda therefore says that the devotees will be very effective in their preaching when they are practicing. Others will then be affected by their instructions. Such is the power of the association of Vaiṣṇavas.

This particular instructive pastime with Mṛgāri the hunter is about association with the devotees of the Lord and how even those who are addicted to all kinds of sinful activity can experience the love and genuine compassion of a Vaiṣṇava.

Nārada Muni said, “I just came to beg some charity from you.” The charity he asked was for Mṛgāri to not half-kill the animals and cause them to suffer. Such Vaiṣṇavas are compassionate, because whatever they ask of others is not for themselves, but rather for the benefit of everyone else. For themselves, they already have what they need. They have Kṛṣṇa.

Thus, devotees who are strictly following are peaceful in their Kṛṣṇa consciousness: *kṛṣṇa-bhakta—niṣkāma, ataeva* *‘śānta’.* He can be peaceful, because he is desireless. He does not have to cheat to get his pleasure. He gets his pleasure directly from Kṛṣṇa. He is therefore the best philanthropic worker in this world.

Prabhupāda says, “A pure Vaiṣṇava is one who has no ulterior motive. He has totally dedicated himself to the service of the Lord. He does not have material desires, and he is not interested in so-called learning and philanthropic work.” (*Cc. Madhya* 24.252, Purport) Prabhupāda is therefore encouraging us all to become pure Vaiṣṇavas by strictly following these principles and taking pleasure in chanting the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* in the association of devotees.

**Madhya-līlā: 24.253–282**

**Minsk—January, 2000**

**Text 253**

*vyādha kahe,—“bālya haite ei āmāra karma*  
*kemane tarimu muñi pāmara adhama?*

**The hunter then admitted that he was convinced of his sinful activity, and he said, ‘I have been taught this business from my very childhood. Now I am wondering how I can become freed from these unlimited volumes of sinful activity.’**

**Purport**

This kind of admission is very beneficial as long as one does not again commit sin. Cheating and hypocrisy are not tolerated by higher authorities. If one understands what sin is, he should give it up with sincerity and regret and surrender unto the lotus feet of the Supreme Personality of Godhead through His agent, the pure devotee. In this way, one can be freed from the reactions of sin and make progress in devotional service. However, if one continues committing sins after making some atonement, he will not be saved. In the *śāstras*, such atonement is compared to an elephant’s bathing. An elephant takes a very good bath and cleanses its body very nicely, but as soon as it comes out of the water, it picks up some dust on the shore and throws it all over its body. Atonement may be carried out very nicely, but it will not help a person if he continues committing sins. Therefore the hunter first admitted his sinful activity before the saintly person Nārada and then asked how he could be saved.

It was previously explained how effective a devotee is by his example in convincing others to give up sinful activity. We can see how Nārada was able to convince the hunter that what he was doing was going to cause him suffering. Such is the power of compassion.

Similarly, we see the power of Lord Nityānanda’s compassion for Jagāi and Mādhāi. A pure Vaiṣṇava can elicit the sense of desire for atonement and repentance within one’s heart.

Of course, what usually causes this feeling of repentance is when one realizes that he is going to have to suffer, or that he is already suffering. He begins to ask the question, “How can I stop this suffering?”

Prabhupāda always explains that the beginning of spiritual life is when a person questions, “Why do I have to suffer? How can I stop suffering?” Sanātana Gosvāmī asked Lord Caitanya these same questions.

The difficulty is that sometimes we become relieved from the suffering and then we forget. That is the nature of the material world. We gauge our activities based upon the fact that we are not suffering right now, and therefore think that everything must be fine. But that is not how we should gauge our activities.

It is explained by Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī in the *Nectar of Devotion* that there is still a lot of suffering lying dormant as a seed, waiting to grow. The only thing that burns up those dormant seeds of sinful reactions is devotional service.

Kṛṣṇa says (*Bg.* 18.66):

*sarva-dharmān parityajya*  
*mām ekaṁ śaraṇaṁ vraja*

“Abandon all varieties of religion and just surrender unto Me. I shall deliver you from all sinful reactions. Do not fear.”

The process of *śaraṇāgati* (surrender) is an ongoing process. If one thinks that he can perform some sense gratification and then atone for it by chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa, this is very offensive. One cannot become freed from all the sinful reactions while maintaining such a sinful mentality.

Unfortunately, we sometimes see that the cumulative effect of such activities bear fruit. Sometimes Kṛṣṇa does not take the suffering away. Then what do we do? Do we give up Kṛṣṇa? Do we become atheists? Do we blame Kṛṣṇa? Actually, we have no one to blame for this except ourselves. Therefore, the intelligent person learns by hearing that he should not commit sinful activities.”
